Japan Trip — Location Almanac

Verified reference for itinerary locations — autumn 2026. All facts sourced; citations inline. One entry for every sightseeing place in the app's data, including the six held in reserve for rain and closures, which are gathered in their own section at the end. The superseded April 2026 section was removed on 2026-08-15 and remains in the repository's history.

Saturday 24 October — Ueno and Yanesen

Tokyo · a museum that calls itself the country's oldest, the country's only Le Corbusier building, and a district the war mostly missed
Tokyo National Museum東京国立博物館
Museum · Ueno · calls itself the oldest institution of its kind in Japan, dating its birth to 1872
▶
It began as an exhibition in 1872

The museum counts its founding from an exhibition. On 10 March 1872, the Ministry of Education's Museum Bureau opened an exhibition in the Taiseiden Hall of Yushima Seido, a former Confucian temple. It was planned to run for twenty days and was extended by ten more, because the crowds were so large that entry had to be limited. The catalogue listed over 600 items. In total, 150,000 people came, around 3,000 a day.

The museum's own summary: "This first exhibition in Japan organized by the government marked the birth of the Tokyo National Museum," and it "marked the inauguration of the nation's first museum with a permanent exhibit."

It moved to Ueno Park in 1882, with Emperor Meiji presiding over the opening of the main building and the zoo on 20 March that year.

⚑ The claim to be Japan's oldest museum comes from the museum itself, and it comes with a qualifier. Its 150th-anniversary page says "Tokyo National Museum (TNM) is the oldest institution of its kind in Japan." Its history pages use a different qualifier: "the nation's first museum with a permanent exhibit." ⇒ The museum describes itself in two ways, both qualified, and neither says simply "the oldest museum in Japan".

Tokyo National Museum official English history pages, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15. The "oldest institution of its kind" wording is tnm.jp/150th/en/about.html · checked 2026-08-21

The museum's buildings and their styles

Four buildings, built at different times, give four different answers to what a Japanese museum should look like:

The first main building was Western. It was designed by the British architect Josiah Conder and built in 1878–81, in brick with two small domes. It was severely damaged in the Great Kanto Earthquake of 1923, and until 1938 the museum had only one surviving building to use.

🔴 The Honkan, which replaced it, was deliberately designed not to look Western. It opened on 10 November 1938. Jin Watanabe designed it after an open competition, in what is called "imperial crown style", which the museum describes as "an Oriental fusion of concrete buildings and roof tiles on top." It was built to commemorate the enthronement of Emperor Showa, took six years to build, and was made earthquake-proof and fire-proof. It became an Important Cultural Property in 2001. ⇒ ⚑ A modern concrete building with a Japanese tiled roof, put up in the late 1930s, reflects the politics of that decade.

The Hyokeikan was built as a wedding gift. It marked the marriage of the future Emperor Taisho and was paid for by around 24,000 donors across the country. Tokuma Katayama of the Imperial Household Ministry designed it, modelled on Greek and Roman architecture. It has been an Important Cultural Property since 1978.

🔴 The Hyokeikan has two dates because two different things happened. The museum's history page says building work began in August 1901 and was completed in September 1908. The finished building was presented to the Crown Prince on 10 October 1908 and named Hyokeikan the following month. The museum's Japanese page for the building says it opened in 1909, and calls it Japan's first full-scale art museum. ⇒ Built in 1908, opened in 1909.

Two of the museum's buildings are by a father and his son. The father, Yoshiro Taniguchi, designed the museum's Toyokan of 1968. The son, Yoshio Taniguchi, designed the Horyu-ji gallery of 1999 and later designed MoMA's expansion in New York.

Tokyo National Museum official English architecture and history pages, quoted and paraphrased · checked 2026-08-15. The Hyokeikan's 1908 completion is from the museum's history page "Royal Gallery: The opening of the Hyokeikan" (English); its 1909 opening is from the museum's Japanese Hyokeikan page · checked 2026-10-17

🔴 There is a whole gallery of Horyu-ji here, and you go to Horyu-ji in five days

The museum's own statement: "The Horyuji Treasures consist of over 300 valuable objects, mainly from the 7th–8th century, which were donated to the Imperial Household by Horyuji Temple in 1878."

⇒ 🔴 On 29 October you visit Horyu-ji itself. Here, five days earlier, you can see three hundred of the objects the temple donated in 1878. ⚑ Seeing this gallery before Horyu-ji makes the Nara day richer: at Horyu-ji you will already know what is no longer in the temple.

The objects are fragile. When the first Horyu-ji gallery opened in 1964, they could be shown only one day a week to protect them. The whole reason for the 1999 rebuild was to let the same objects be shown more often.

Three objects in the gallery are worth looking for. All three are on a long display that runs to April 2027, so they should be on show when you visit:

🔴 The gilt-bronze kanjo banner, from the Asuka period (seventh century), a National Treasure. It is believed to be the item recorded as "one gilded kanjo ceremony article" in a Horyu-ji property inventory compiled in 747. ⇒ You can look at an object and find it in a list written thirteen hundred years ago.

Forty-eight small gilt-bronze Buddhas, none taller than about forty centimetres, believed to have been used for private worship. Also a Gigaku dance mask of Karura, the bird-man, from the seventh century, a National Treasure.

⚠ Outside the Horyu-ji gallery, the displays rotate. The museum changes individual objects on schedules of weeks to a year, so no specific item outside the Horyu-ji gallery can be promised for any given day.

Tokyo National Museum official English page for the Gallery of Horyuji Treasures, quoted verbatim, incl. the 1878 donation, the 747 inventory, and the current rotation dates · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The museum is enormous; do not try to see all of it. ⚠ You are there on a Saturday, when the museum stays open into the evening. Four things to look at:

1. Go to the Gallery of Horyu-ji Treasures first. ⇒ It is the only part of the museum that is directly about a place you are going to. Find the gilt-bronze banner, and remember that it appears in a temple inventory from 747.

2. In the Honkan, stop in the entrance hall before going into the galleries. The museum itself names the marble staircase, which wraps around an open stairwell, as the building's signature spot. ⇒ The hall is part of the 1938 building, which was designed in the 1930s to show what a modern Japanese building should look like.

3. Look at the outside of the Honkan, then at the Hyokeikan. The Honkan is a concrete building with a tiled Japanese roof; the Hyokeikan is a Meiji building modelled on Greek and Roman architecture. They were built about thirty years apart: the Hyokeikan in 1908–09, the Honkan in 1938. ⇒ The museum put up a building on Greek and Roman models first and a Japanese-roofed one about thirty years later, and that shift is the museum's real history.

4. The Kuromon, the black gate. It was the main gate of a daimyo's residence in Edo and was moved here in 1954; it is comparable to the famous Red Gate at Tokyo University. ⚠ It opens only at weekends and holidays, and a closure notice has been posted for it, so do not count on it being open.

Tokyo National Museum official English architecture walk and visitor pages · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Gallery of Horyuji Treasures (English) Official museum history (English)
National Museum of Western Art国立西洋美術館
Museum · Ueno · opened 1959 · the only Le Corbusier building in Japan · World Heritage
▶
🔴 The building exists because France made it a condition

This is the strangest founding story of any museum on the trip, and the museum tells it on its own website.

Kojiro Matsukata (1866–1950), president of Kawasaki Dockyard and son of a prime minister, began buying art in London in the middle of the First World War, using profits from wartime shipbuilding. His collection reached an estimated ten thousand works. He planned his own museum in Tokyo, the "Sheer Pleasure Fine Arts Pavilion", designed with the painter Frank Brangwyn.

Then the collection was broken up. The 1927 financial crisis brought down his company's bank; he resigned, and the Japanese portion of the collection was sold at auction. Works stored in a London warehouse were destroyed by fire in 1939; in the museum's own words, "the details of that loss cannot be confirmed." Around 400 works held in Paris were seized by the French government as enemy property near the end of the Second World War, and became French national property in 1951 under the peace treaty.

🔴 France later returned most of the Paris works, on one condition. In June 1953 the French Embassy sent a formal memorandum requiring that Japan build a museum for French art in Tokyo before the works were returned. Le Corbusier was named architect in March 1955. President de Gaulle authorised the return in December 1958, the agreement was signed in January 1959, and the artworks came back to Yokohama by ship. The museum opened in June 1959.

⇒ ⚑ The museum was built to meet France's condition for returning the works.

National Museum of Western Art official English pages on the Matsukata Collection and the museum's chronology, quoted and paraphrased · checked 2026-08-15

What Le Corbusier built, and why UNESCO named it

The Main Building was completed in March 1959, and the museum describes it as "a symbol of the resumption of diplomatic ties between Japan and France after World War II." Le Corbusier designed it; three of his former students — Junzo Sakakura, Kunio Maekawa and Takamasa Yoshizaka — supervised the work on site. Maekawa also designed the New Wing that opened in 1979.

🔴 In 2016 it was inscribed on the World Heritage list as one of seventeen sites across seven countries in "The Architectural Work of Le Corbusier, an Outstanding Contribution to the Modern Movement." UNESCO's own text singles this building out, calling it "the prototype of the globally transposable Museum of Unlimited Growth."

What "unlimited growth" means. The Japan Tourism Agency's official text on the building explains it. Le Corbusier "envisioned a museum that could be expanded whenever necessary": first by moving the walls inside the galleries, and later by adding rooms and whole floors, until the building took a pyramid-like shape around a central lobby.

The museum in Ueno uses several parts of that plan: an entrance hall in the middle, a corridor-like series of rectangular galleries that spiral upward from the hall, and movable walls in those galleries. ⚠ The extra floors were never added. The same text says building them "was never considered realistic."

⚠ The building was also designated an Important Cultural Property in 2007. The museum's Japanese page says so; its English page does not.

National Museum of Western Art official English and Japanese building pages (the architects, the 1979 wing, the 2007 designation on the Japanese page only); UNESCO World Heritage Centre for the inscription title and the "Museum of Unlimited Growth" description · checked 2026-08-15. Japan Tourism Agency multilingual commentary database, "The National Museum of Western Art Building (Le Corbusier's Design)", for how the Museum of Unlimited Growth was meant to work · checked 2026-10-17

🔴 The forecourt was restored because UNESCO complained

When the site was inscribed in 2016, ICOMOS pointed out that decades of piecemeal changes had compromised Le Corbusier's design for the forecourt. The museum did the work alongside a planned roof-waterproofing job, closing the forecourt from October 2020 to April 2022 and restoring it "as near as possible to its appearance in 1959."

The forecourt restoration meant cutting back planting, returning the main gate to its original position on the west side, and relaying the paving joints according to Le Corbusier's Modulor, his proportional system based on the human body.

⇒ ⚑ The paving you cross to get in was re-laid to Le Corbusier's proportional system because a heritage body said the forecourt no longer matched his design.

National Museum of Western Art official English building page, on the forecourt restoration, its dates, and the Modulor paving · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

⚠ You are there on the opening day of a major Turner exhibition organised with Tate, so the special-exhibition wing will be busy. The permanent collection needs a separate ticket and is a separate visit. Four things to look at:

1. Stand in the forecourt before you go in and look at the paving joints. They were relaid to the Modulor in 2020–22. ⇒ The paving under your feet follows the same proportional system as the building.

2. The Rodin bronzes in the forecourt. The Gates of Hell, The Thinker, The Burghers of Calais, and Bourdelle's Herakles. ⇒ The forecourt was designed around a straight line from the original gate to The Gates of Hell, and the restoration put that line back.

3. Inside, follow the ramp instead of taking the direct route. ⇒ UNESCO calls this building the prototype of Le Corbusier's "Museum of Unlimited Growth", and its galleries spiral upward from the central hall. Following the ramp is how you walk that spiral.

4. Find the Matsukata works in the permanent collection. ⇒ These are the paintings France seized and gave back, and the reason there is a museum here at all. ⚑ A free display on the museum's own conservation history — including Van Gogh's Roses and Renoir's Woman with a Hat — is running in the Main Building through January 2027.

National Museum of Western Art official English exhibition and building pages · checked 2026-08-15. The "prototype" description from the UNESCO World Heritage listing; the galleries' upward spiral from the Japan Tourism Agency text on the building · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (English) Official page on the Matsukata Collection (English) UNESCO listing (English)
Yanaka Ginza谷中銀座商店街
Shopping street · Yanaka · about 170 m long · formed around 1945
▶
What it is, and how old it actually is

A shopping street about 170 metres long, five minutes from Nippori station, lined mainly with traditional individually-run shops. Taito City describes its origin plainly: it "arose spontaneously around 1945" and developed into a neighbourhood shopping street.

⇒ ⚑ The street does not date from the Edo period. It is post-war, and it still looks much as it did in the mid-twentieth century. Taito City says the same: you can see nostalgic Showa-era shopping-street scenery.

Taito City official tourism site (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Why this district looks different from the rest of Tokyo

Taito City's own account of the wider Yanaka area: "In the Edo period, temples were gathered here by urban planning, and the area developed as a temple town. Afterward, damage from earthquakes and war was comparatively slight, so the old townscape survives today." The area has been selected as one of Japan's hundred best-preserved historic landscapes.

⚠ Taito City says the damage was light, but it does not say why. A common explanation is that the temple grounds acted as firebreaks. The city does not make that claim, and this almanac does not either.

Taito City official tourism site, area feature page (Japanese), quoted and translated · the absence of a stated cause is noted explicitly · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The street takes ten minutes to walk end to end without stopping, but you will stop. Four things to look at:

1. Approach from the Nippori end and stop at the top of the steps. They are called Yuyake Dandan, "sunset steps", and the ward names them as a spot to watch the sun go down over the old low city. ⇒ If you can, arrive here late in the day. The street looks best from the top of the steps.

2. The cats. Taito City's own photo captions invite you to "spot the cats", and one photo shows a shop selling baked doughnuts shaped like a cat's tail. ⇒ The city itself promotes the cat theme as a feature of the street, so it is more than a tourist story.

3. Look at what the shops are. The shopkeepers' association counts about 60 shops on the 170-metre street, mostly long-established, independently owned shops. Most of them sell one kind of goods. It names the trades itself: meat, vegetables, fish, sake, clothes, kimono, pottery and tea. Its shop list also includes a bamboo-craft shop, a tempura shop and a sweet shop that makes its own boiled sweets. ⇒ Most of Tokyo has lost this kind of shopping street. Here it still operates as an ordinary working street.

4. The wooden cats. The association's own history says it put up carved wooden cats on the street in 2008. Two years earlier, in 2006, it gave the shops matching sun awnings and hanging signs. ⇒ The cats and the matching awnings were added by the shopkeepers to give the street one look.

Taito City official tourism site, spot and area pages (Japanese) · checked 2026-08-15. Yanaka Ginza shopkeepers' association, its "about" page and shop list (Japanese), for the shop count, the trades, the named shops, the awnings and the wooden cats, translated · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Taito City tourism page (Japanese) Shopkeepers' association (Japanese)
Yanaka Cemetery谷中霊園
Cemetery · Yanaka · opened 1874 · about 10 hectares · the last shogun is buried here
▶
What it is

The cemetery opened on 1 September 1874 and covers about 102,537 square metres. The Park Association that manages it describes its make-up precisely: it sits within Yanaka's temple town, and the burial grounds of Kan'ei-ji and Tenno-ji are interwoven within it to form a single cemetery area.

⚠ It is often described simply as "the former grounds of Tenno-ji". In fact it is two temples' graveyards and a municipal cemetery, mixed together. The north side rises to meet the railway and looks out toward Skytree.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page for Yanaka Cemetery (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 The last shogun is here, and so is the space where a pagoda stood

Tokugawa Yoshinobu, the fifteenth and last Tokugawa shogun, is buried in this cemetery. He gave up power in 1867 and lived until 1913. The Park Association's own cemetery map marks him by name, in the block near the pagoda ruin. ⚑ He is not in the map's numbered list of notable graves, which fits with his grave being a family plot rather than a listed monument. ⚠ No official page describes what his grave looks like. Japanese Wikipedia, a general reference, says he asked in his will for a Shinto funeral instead of a Buddhist one, to thank Emperor Meiji, and that his grave was therefore built as a round mound like those of the imperial family. Treat this as something to check with your own eyes.

🔴 The pagoda. The Association's page notes the cemetery contains the site of Tenno-ji's five-storey pagoda, which was the model for Koda Rohan's novel The Five-Storied Pagoda. Only the foundation stones remain.

The pagoda burned in the early hours of 6 July 1957. Two bodies were found in the ashes, and the police ruled it an arson-suicide. The pagoda had stood since a rebuilding in 1791, and was one of the four great pagodas of Edo; two of the others had already burned in the air raids of 1945. It was never rebuilt.

⚠ Newspapers at the time reported the fire. A local tradition disputes the official ruling, but it is folklore and is given less weight here.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page and its own cemetery map PDF (Yoshinobu's plot, the pagoda ruin); contemporary newspaper reporting of 6 July 1957 relayed by a documentary source, for the fire and the ruling · the disputing local tradition is flagged as folklore · checked 2026-08-15. Japanese Wikipedia article on Tokugawa Yoshinobu (general reference, no official page found) for the Shinto burial and the mound · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

A quiet public cemetery, open to walk through. ⚠ People come here to visit graves; it is not a park. Four things to look at:

1. The central avenue. The Association singles out its cherry-tree avenue as the finest part of the grounds. ⚠ The trees are bare in late October, but the avenue is the cemetery's main axis and the easiest way to find your way around.

2. Yoshinobu's plot. ⇒ The man who ended two and a half centuries of Tokugawa rule is buried in a municipal cemetery in a residential district, a short walk from a shopping street. Compare the size of the grave with the importance of the office he held.

3. The pagoda foundation stones. ⇒ Only the stones are left of a pagoda that inspired a novel. It was the last of Edo's four great pagodas to be lost.

4. The other names. The Association's map lists around seventy-six notable graves — among them Shibusawa Eiichi, the industrialist; the painter Yokoyama Taikan; the koto composer Miyagi Michio; and the prime minister Hatoyama Ichiro (1883–1959). ⇒ A cross-section of the people who built modern Japan, in one ten-hectare block.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page and cemetery map PDF · checked 2026-08-15. Hatoyama Ichiro's name and dates from the same map's list of notable graves · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official page (Japanese) Official cemetery map, with named graves (PDF, Japanese)
Nezu-jinja根津神社
Shrine · Bunkyo · buildings completed 1706 · seven Important Cultural Properties, none missing
▶
🔴 Seven buildings from 1706, and not one of them is gone

In a city that has burned many times, this shrine matters because its whole set of 1706 buildings survives. The shrine's own words:

"The honden, heiden, haiden, karamon, west gate, latticed fence and romon, completed in Hoei 3 (1706) in the gongen-zukuri form, survive today without a single one missing, and are designated national Important Cultural Properties."

⇒ 🔴 Seven structures were built as one project and finished in the same year, and the complete set still stands in central Tokyo.

⚠ "Survive" needs a qualification, and the shrine gives it. The buildings were damaged in 1945; restoration was completed in 1959, and repairs to the karamon, fence and romon finished in 1962. ⇒ The buildings are the original 1706 structures, repaired after war damage.

Nezu-jinja official site, history page (Japanese), the list of seven quoted verbatim and the 1945–1962 restoration sequence, translated · checked 2026-08-15

Why a shogun built it — and it is a family matter

The land here was the residence of Tokugawa Tsunashige, and the birthplace of his son Tsunatoyo, later the sixth shogun Ienobu.

The shrine's account: the fifth shogun Tsunayoshi named Ienobu, his elder brother's son, as his adopted heir. He then donated the residence land to Nezu Gongen-sha, the shrine of the guardian deity of Ienobu's birthplace, built the present shrine, moved it here from its old site in 1706, and endowed it with a permanent estate of 500 koku. The shrine describes the work as a great construction project of the type known as a nationwide levy.

What a "nationwide levy" was. The Japanese term is tenka-bushin (天下普請). It was the shogunate's way of getting large works built: the work was shared out among the daimyo, the feudal lords, who had to carry it out. Edo Castle and Osaka Castle were built the same way.

⇒ ⚑ A childless shogun adopted his nephew and then rebuilt the shrine of the god who had watched over the boy's birth, as a project the feudal lords had to share. The new shrine was a public announcement of the heir.

Nezu-jinja official site, history page (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15. What a tenka-bushin was: Kotobank, quoting Heibonsha's World Encyclopedia (general reference), translated · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

A complete Edo-period shrine complex on a slope, free and open to all. ⚠ The azalea garden is locked outside the April festival, so you cannot walk into it in October. Five things to look at:

1. The romon, the two-storey gate. The shrine says it is the only surviving romon among shrines within Edo. The guardian figure on the right is said to be modelled on Mito Komon. ⇒ This type of gate was once common in the city, and this is the only one left at a shrine in the old city of Edo.

2. Follow the latticed fence around the buildings. It runs for two hundred metres, with diamond-shaped lattice openings. The shrine says a recent survey found its foundations going eight metres underground, and that after three hundred years it shows no distortion. ⇒ Most of this fence is below the ground you are standing on.

3. The torii tunnel up to Otome Inari. The shrine calls the approach a "thousand torii," lined with donated gates. The small Otome Inari shrine building itself is set inside a hollowed-out hole, and old records call it "Hole Inari."

4. Look for the swastika crest on the buildings. It was the shrine's crest in the centuries when Buddhism and Shinto were practised together here, and it was left in place when the Meiji government stripped Buddhist elements out of shrines. ⇒ The crest is a surviving mark of the older combined practice, on a shrine that was renamed when the two were separated.

5. The house where Mori Ogai wrote The Dancing Girl. It was moved to the shrine grounds and is open only at weekends and holidays; you are there on a Saturday. ⚑ Both Ogai and Natsume Soseki were parishioners here. The shrine's grounds guide lists "the writers' stone" (Bungo no Ishi, 文豪の石), the stone they sat on during walks in the grounds. On the shrine's grounds map it is No. 17, just west of the romon gate, beside the path that leads to the torii tunnel. Near it, No. 18 is a water basin made from the pedestal of a captured shell that Ogai, then an army surgeon, donated after the Russo-Japanese War.

Nezu-jinja official grounds guide (Japanese), per-feature descriptions for all five, translated · checked 2026-08-15. The writers' stone and the Ogai water basin from the same guide; the stone's position read from the shrine's own grounds map on that page · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official history (Japanese) Official grounds guide (Japanese)

Monday 26 October — Mount Takao

West of Tokyo · a temple founded by the same emperor and the same monk as the Great Buddha at Nara, on a mountain where two climate zones meet
Takaosan Yakuo-in高尾山薬王院
Shingon temple · Mount Takao · founded 744 · a Shugendo mountain and a tengu mountain
▶
🔴 Founded by the two people behind the Great Buddha

The temple's formal name is Takaosan Yakuo-in Yuki-ji. The temple says it was founded in 744 by order of Emperor Shomu, with Gyoki as its founding priest, to guard the eastern provinces and pray for them. Its original principal image was Yakushi Nyorai, the healing buddha. ⚑ The temple says its name, Yakuo-in (薬王院), comes from that first principal image, Yakushi Nyorai (薬師如来).

The temple's own English page states the connection outright: Gyoki was "a charismatic priest closely associated with the erection of the Great Buddha at Todai-ji Temple in Nara."

⇒ 🔴 On 31 October you stand in front of the Great Buddha at Todai-ji in Nara. Emperor Shomu ordered both the Buddha and this temple, and Gyoki raised both. ⚑ Yakuo-in is the eastern end of one imperial programme, and Nara is the other end.

Takaosan Yakuo-in official Japanese and English "about" pages, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15. The origin of the name re-read on the Japanese page · checked 2026-10-17

🔴 And it was restarted by a monk from Daigo

The temple later declined. Around 1375 it was revived by Shungen Daitoku, who is called its second founder. The temple says he came from Mount Daigo.

Shungen performed the eight-thousand-stick fire ritual, an extreme form of the esoteric goma fire rite, dedicated to Fudo Myo-o. Through it he received a vision of Izuna Daigongen, and he enshrined that deity as the temple's new principal image.

⇒ 🔴 You go to Daigo-ji, the temple on Mount Daigo, on 4 November. ⚑ The mountain temple outside Tokyo that you climb in week one was revived by a monk from the mountain temple outside Kyoto that you climb in week two. Both belong to the same Shingon mountain tradition, carried four hundred kilometres east.

Takaosan Yakuo-in official Japanese "about" pages, on Shungen Daitoku, his origin at Mount Daigo, the eight-thousand-stick rite and the new principal image · checked 2026-08-15

What is worshipped, and it is five gods in one

Izuna Daigongen is not a standard buddha. The temple's own English explanation: it is "a form in which Fudo Myo-o appears to bring people to salvation," and it "combines the elements of five deities: Fudo Myo-o, Karuraten (Garuda, a divine bird), Dakiniten (a demon that feeds on human hearts), Kangiten (a fertility deity with the head of an elephant) and Benzaiten."

⇒ ⚑ Izuna Daigongen is a composite of a wrathful king, a bird, a demon, an elephant-headed god and a goddess of water and music. Its cult began at Mount Izuna near Zenko-ji in Nagano and spread nationwide.

🔴 Warlords wore Izuna Daigongen on their helmets. The temple records that Takeda Shingen and Uesugi Kenshin venerated Izuna Daigongen as a protector and put it on their helmet crests. The Hojo clan, who ruled the Kanto, gave the mountain special protection, partly because of its strategic position.

Takaosan Yakuo-in official English "about" page, quoted verbatim, and the Japanese page for the warlord veneration · checked 2026-08-15

The tengu, and the two statues in front of the Izuna Gongen hall

What a tengu is. The temple's own English page describes tengu as "long-nosed demon-like beings" believed to live on sacred mountains. They act as messengers of the gods and buddhas, punishing evildoers and protecting the good, and are often shown holding a fan that sweeps away misfortune. The temple's Japanese page adds that on Takao the tengu are the attendants of Izuna Daigongen, the temple's principal deity, and that since ancient times they have been believed to have supernatural powers.

The temple says plainly that Mount Takao has been called a mountain of Shugendo since ancient times, and that practitioners who trained by sleeping out in the mountains came to be called yamabushi. The temple also says that the yamabushi are often identified with the tengu.

🔴 The two tengu statues in front of the Izuna Gongen hall show two ranks, and the temple explains them. The smaller one has a crow's beak; the larger has a long nose. In the temple's own English: "The small tengu is considered to be still undergoing religious training, while the large tengu is often likened to an experienced yamabushi who has attained spiritual power."

⇒ ⚑ The two statues are a junior and a senior. Once you know that, you can read the rank of every tengu pair you see later in the trip.

Takaosan Yakuo-in official English and Japanese "about" pages, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15. What a tengu is: the English "about" page and the Japanese page "Mount Takao and the tengu" (takaosan.or.jp/about/), translated · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Yakuo-in sits partway up the main path. The temple's own trail guide calls that path mostly paved and easy even for beginners. Four things to look at:

1. At the Joshinmon gate, look for the stone stele beside the gate. It reads "killing prohibited." The temple cites it as evidence that all taking of life on this mountain was strictly forbidden for centuries. ⇒ ⚑ The forest you are walking through was protected by religious law long before park law protected it.

2. The two tengu statues at the Izuna hall. ⇒ The one with the crow's beak is the trainee, and the one with the long nose is the master.

3. The waterfalls, if your path passes one. Biwa-taki and Ja-taki are still used for waterfall austerity. The temple opens them to the public on set days, and practitioners wear white robes. ⇒ These falls are working religious sites, still in use today.

4. The missing rubbish bins. The temple's own trail page says Mount Takao is the birthplace of Japan's "carry your rubbish home" movement. ⇒ There are no bins on the mountain on purpose, and the practice goes back to that movement.

Takaosan Yakuo-in official trail guide and English asceticism page (Japanese and English), for the stele, the waterfalls, the paved trail and the rubbish movement · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official history and deity pages (Japanese) Official trail guide (Japanese)
Mount Takao高尾山
Mountain · 599 m · quasi-national park · where two climate zones overlap
▶
🔴 Why the botany here is genuinely exceptional

This is what makes Takao more than a convenient hill. The Tokyo Metropolitan Government explains it this way:

"The forest around Mount Takao sits at the boundary of the cool-temperate and warm-temperate zones, so it has an environment where organisms from both climate zones can grow, and consequently many species of flora and fauna live there."

The result is 1,300 species of higher plants in 153 families on and around the mountain. More striking still, 65 species were first scientifically described from specimens collected here.

⇒ ⚑ Sixty-five plants were first described by science from this one 599-metre hill. One of them is the Takao violet, named after the mountain. The metropolitan government calls Takao "the sacred ground of violets" and counts eighteen violet species inside the park.

The mountain sits in the Meiji no Mori Takao Quasi-National Park, 770 hectares, designated in 1967 as part of the centenary commemoration of the Meiji Restoration.

Tokyo Metropolitan Government Bureau of Environment park pages (the climate boundary and the violets) and the Takao cable-car operator's own page (the species counts), Japanese, translated · checked 2026-08-15

The mountain that stands in for Fuji

The temple's own history gives Takao a role in the pilgrimage from Edo to Mount Fuji. In the Tenbun era (1532–54), Hojo Ujiyasu had the Fuji deity enshrined on Mount Takao. A hall is said to have stood on a peak called Fujimidai ("Fuji-viewing stand"). When its doors were opened, Mount Fuji appeared in the doorway, framed like a painting. The trail from the inner sanctuary toward the summit is still called the Fuji road.

🔴 The temple records that Takao was Fuji's "front stand-in," and Oyama in Sagami its "back stand-in," on the pilgrimage route out of Edo.

⇒ ⚑ Pilgrims climbed Takao in place of Fuji on purpose, and the religion had a name for that role. On a clear day the summit shows Tanzawa and Fuji, and in clear winter air the Southern Alps as well.

Takaosan Yakuo-in official Japanese history page, on the Fuji deity, Fujimidai, the Fuji road and the front/back stand-in framing, translated · checked 2026-08-15

The cable car, and one claim that does not hold up

The Kiyotaki cable car runs 1,000 metres of track and climbs 271 metres. The operator's own figure for the steepest section is 31 degrees 18 minutes, which it claims is the steepest cable-car gradient in Japan. It opened in 1927, was suspended in 1944 under wartime consolidation, and resumed in 1949.

⚠ Takao is often called the most-climbed mountain in the world. The official sources claim less than that. The cable-car operator gives over 2.5 million visitors a year, and the temple says the annual climber count is first in Japan. ⇒ "Most climbed in Japan" has a source. "Most climbed in the world" is repeated everywhere and attributed to nobody.

✅ One superlative does hold up. In 2007, the first Japanese edition of the Michelin Green Guide gave Mount Takao three stars, and the only other mountain it rated that high was Fuji.

Takao Tozan Railway official pages (the cable-car specifications, the visitor figure, the Michelin rating) and Yakuo-in's own page (the "first in Japan" wording); the "world's most climbed" claim is flagged as unsourced · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

⚠ Trail 1, the main approach, is mostly paved, and the temple calls it easy for beginners. The temple describes the Inari-yama route as the hardest. Four things to look at:

1. On the way up, watch the trees change. You are crossing between two climate zones. ⇒ Warm-temperate evergreens grow low down and cool-temperate species grow higher up. This mix is why 1,300 plant species live on one small mountain.

2. The suspension bridge on trail 4. The temple's own guide calls it spectacular, and it is the most distinctive stretch of path on the mountain. ⇒ The bridge is worth the short detour onto trail 4.

3. At the summit, look south-west toward Fuji, then find the Fuji road behind you. ⇒ You have just walked the trail that stood in for the Fuji pilgrimage, and its name still says so.

4. The marker at the foot of trail 1. It marks the eastern end of the Tokai Nature Trail, which the Ministry of the Environment calls Japan's first long-distance nature trail. The Ministry gives its length as 1,748 kilometres, linking 11 prefectures, from the Meiji no Mori Takao park here to the Meiji no Mori Minoh park in Minoh, just outside Osaka. It opened along its whole length in July 1974. ⇒ The path you start on here runs all the way from Tokyo to Osaka.

Takaosan Yakuo-in official trail guide (the paved main trail, the hardest route, the suspension bridge, the Tokai trail marker) · checked 2026-08-15. The Tokai Nature Trail's length, route and opening from the Ministry of the Environment's summary of its Tokai Nature Trail revival plan (July 2024) · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Cable-car operator's page on the mountain (Japanese) Tokyo Metropolitan Government on the park's ecology (Japanese) Temple's trail guide (Japanese)

Tuesday 27 October — Kita-Kamakura

Kanagawa · Japan's first purpose-built Zen monastery, a temple founded to mourn an enemy, and the shrine of the government that built them both
Engaku-ji円覚寺
Rinzai temple · Kita-Kamakura · founded 1282 · second of the Kamakura Five Mountains
▶
🔴🔴 It was founded to mourn the enemy dead as well as its own

The one fact to know before you walk in, in the temple's own words:

"In 1282, Engaku-ji was founded by the priest Mugaku Sogen, whom the regent Hojo Tokimune had invited from Song-dynasty China. Tokimune, the founding patron, had assumed the office of regent at eighteen and deeply devoted himself to Zen under Mugaku Sogen. He resolved to build Engaku-ji out of a wish to protect the nation and to spread Zen — and to mourn, equally and without distinguishing friend from foe, those who died in the Mongol invasions."

⇒ 🔴 Tokimune had just driven off the largest invasion in Japanese history, and he built a temple to pray for the invaders too. ⚑ He was in his early thirties when he founded it, and he died at thirty-three.

The founding priest, Mugaku Sogen, was himself Chinese: a monk who had left Song China as the Mongols were overrunning it. ⇒ Both men had faced the same Mongol conquest. Tokimune drove it back from Japan, and Mugaku Sogen had left China while the Mongols were overrunning it.

Engaku-ji official site, temple history page (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Both of its names are accidents

The temple explains where its name comes from. The name Engaku comes from a copy of the Sutra of Perfect Enlightenment that was unearthed during construction.

Its mountain name, Zuiroku-san ("auspicious deer mountain"), comes from the dedication ceremony: white deer are said to have gathered to listen when Mugaku Sogen gave the opening sermon in the Buddha hall.

⇒ ⚑ One name came from a sutra dug out of the ground, and the other from deer that came out of the woods.

Engaku-ji official history page (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-08-15

The two National Treasures, and what they are for

The Shariden, the relic hall, holds a tooth relic of the Buddha which Minamoto no Sanetomo had brought from a temple in Song China. The temple calls the hall the finest surviving example in Japan of the architectural style transmitted from China in the Kamakura period. That is why it is a National Treasure.

⚑ Look at the roof. The temple points out the detail: the upper rafters fan out from under the eaves like the ribs of a folding fan (ogi-daruki). This makes the roof look larger and the small building grander. The flower-shaped windows have straight lower frames that do not flare outward, and that is how they are dated to the late Kamakura period rather than the Edo period.

The Great Bell, also a National Treasure, was cast in 1301 and is the largest in the Kanto region at about 259.5 centimetres. It was donated by Hojo Sadatoki, Tokimune's son, as a prayer for the peace and stability of the country.

⇒ Tokimune founded the temple after the invasions, and his son Sadatoki gave the bell twenty years later.

Engaku-ji official grounds page (Japanese), on the Shariden's relic, the fan rafters and window dating, and the bell's date, size and donor · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The temple climbs up a narrow valley: first the gate, then the Buddha hall, then the abbot's quarters. Four things to look at:

1. The Sanmon (main gate) and its date. It was rebuilt in 1785 for the five-hundredth memorial of the founding priest's death. The temple gives Mugaku Sogen's dates as 1226–1286. In Japanese Buddhist memorials, the memorial held two full years after a death is called the "third" memorial (三回忌). ⇒ On that count, the five-hundredth memorial of a death in 1286 fell 499 years later, in 1785. Its name plaque was granted by a retired emperor. ⇒ The gate was put up to mark five centuries since Mugaku Sogen died.

2. At the Shariden, look up at the eaves before you look inside, and find the fan-shaped rafters. ⇒ The Shariden is the textbook example in Japan of Zen architecture transmitted from China, because of its roof.

3. The Great Bell. Getting to it is a climb. ⇒ It was cast in 1301, it is the largest bell in the Kanto region, and it was paid for by the son of the man who founded the temple.

4. In the Butsuden (Buddha hall), look up at the ceiling: it has a white dragon. The building is a 1964 reconstruction; the original was destroyed in the Great Kanto Earthquake of 1923. ⇒ The biggest building here is one of the newest, because the earthquake destroyed the original.

Engaku-ji official grounds page (Japanese), on the Sanmon's 1785 rebuilding, the Shariden, the bell and the Butsuden's destruction and 1964 rebuilding · checked 2026-08-15. Mugaku Sogen's dates from the temple's English grounds page; how memorial years are counted from Kotobank (Japanese dictionaries, general reference) (the Japanese page's 五百年遠諱 is followed; the English page says '500th Anniversary of the Foundation') · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (English) Official history (Japanese) Official grounds guide (Japanese)
Meigetsu-in明月院
Temple · Kita-Kamakura · originated 1160 · the last surviving piece of a temple that was abolished
▶
🔴 It is all that is left of a larger temple

Meigetsu-in is the surviving sub-temple of a large temple that no longer exists.

Kencho-ji's own record gives the history. Meigetsu-in began as Meigetsu-an, a hermitage founded in 1160 by a local lord's heir in memory of his father. About a century later Hojo Tokiyori built Saimyo-ji on a corner of the grounds, and his son Tokimune revived it as Zenko-ji. A century after that, around 1380, Uesugi Norikata raised the hermitage to a full temple and made it the chief sub-temple of Zenko-ji.

🔴 Then in the early Meiji period Zenko-ji was abolished, and only Meigetsu-in was left. The national heritage record says so directly: Meigetsu-in was "originally a sub-temple of Zenko-ji, now an abolished temple."

⇒ ⚑ What you visit today is the former sub-temple. Government policy dissolved Zenko-ji, the main temple, and its sub-temple outlived it.

Kencho-ji official site, which lists Meigetsu-in among its affiliated temples with this history (Japanese); Agency for Cultural Affairs Japan Heritage record, credited to Kamakura City's tourism division, for the abolition of Zenko-ji · translated · checked 2026-08-15

⚠ What you will and will not see in late October

Know what you will not see at Meigetsu-in before you go.

⚠ The rear garden is normally closed. It opens to the public only twice a year: for the irises in early to mid June, and for the autumn colour, which reports place in late November into early December. ⇒ On 27 October the garden will almost certainly be shut, so you will see it through the round window in the main hall rather than walking in it.

⚠ The temple is famous across Japan for its hydrangeas, around 2,500 bushes, and they flower in June. ⇒ They will not be in flower in late October, and no change of plan can avoid that.

⚠ The temple has no official website, so the autumn opening dates come only from other reports. Treat the late-November window as approximate, and ask locally if it matters.

Odakyu and Enoden joint official tourism site (the separate rear-garden opening and the hydrangea count), Japanese; the specific autumn dates are from non-official aggregators and are flagged as such; the absence of an official temple site was verified by lookup · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Meigetsu-in is small. Take your time there instead of hurrying through for photos. Three things to look at:

1. The round window in the main hall. Widely called the Window of Enlightenment, it is a circular opening in the wall that frames the rear garden. The circle is the enso, the Zen circle. ⇒ 🔴 With the garden closed, you see the garden the way the window was designed to show it: as a framed picture seen from inside. ⚠ The window's name appears consistently in reports, but none of them is official, because the temple has no website of its own.

2. The yagura (a cave tomb cut into the rock). The Kanagawa Archaeology Foundation defines yagura as cave tombs dug into hillsides from the late Kamakura to the early Muromachi period, from the late 13th to the early 15th century. They are concentrated in and around Kamakura. Remains were placed in them and small memorial towers set up, so they were used for burial and memorial rites.

The national heritage record calls the Meigetsu-in yagura the largest in Kamakura, carved with Buddhist images including Shaka Nyorai. ⚑ The Kanagawa Prefecture tourist association says the yagura is traditionally held to be the tomb of Uesugi Norikata, the lord who made Meigetsu-in a full temple around 1380. It says the figures carved in relief on the wall appear to be Shaka Nyorai and Taho Nyorai.

⚠ Why Kamakura made tombs this way is not settled. The Foundation mentions one theory: that the custom arrived with Chinese Buddhist culture. It also states that yagura were not made because Kamakura was overcrowded with samurai and graves had been banned there.

3. The path up to the temple. ⇒ It was once a path inside the much larger Zenko-ji complex, and it leads to the only building left standing when the rest was dissolved.

Agency for Cultural Affairs Japan Heritage record for the yagura; the round window's name and reading from non-official sources, flagged · checked 2026-08-15. What yagura are: Kanagawa Archaeology Foundation, "Yagura of the Miura Peninsula" (PDF, Japanese). Uesugi Norikata's tomb and the carved buddhas: Kanagawa Prefecture Tourist Association page for Meigetsu-in (Japanese). Both translated · checked 2026-10-17

Links
National Japan Heritage record (Japanese) Kencho-ji's page listing its affiliated temples (Japanese)
Kencho-ji建長寺
Rinzai temple · Kamakura · founded 1253 · Japan's first purpose-built Zen monastery
▶
🔴 The first temple in Japan to be called a Zen temple

Kencho-ji makes the following claim and backs it with evidence:

"As inscribed on the temple bell cast in 1255 — 'Kencho Zen-ji' — this temple is the first in our country to be called a 'Zen temple': a specialist training hall practising only Song-Chinese-style Rinzai Zen."

The temple also explains why this stood out at the time: most medieval temples combined Tendai, Shingon, Pure Land and other teachings under one roof, so a temple devoted to a single school, like Kencho-ji, was very unusual.

Kencho-ji was founded in 1253 by the regent Hojo Tokiyori, with the Chinese monk Rankei Doryu as founding priest. It was modelled on a monastery in Hangzhou that ranked first among the Chinese Five Mountains, and copies its symmetrical layout. Rankei enforced Chinese monastic rules; his handwritten regulations survive as a National Treasure.

⚑ Rankei Doryu's posthumous title was the first "Zen Master" title ever granted in Japan.

Kencho-ji official site, history page (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 It was built on an execution ground

Kencho-ji is open about its site's past. Before the monastery was built, the site was known as "Hell Valley," and it is recorded as a place where criminals were executed.

A hall dedicated to Jizo, the bodhisattva who accompanies the dead, already stood in Hell Valley. When the temple was founded the hall was moved away, but the Jizo image said to have been its principal figure is now enshrined inside Kencho-ji's Buddha hall, surrounded by a thousand small Jizo statues. ⚑ Kencho-ji's own principal image is Jizo, a bodhisattva, instead of a buddha.

⇒ 🔴 At the centre of Japan's first purpose-built Zen monastery is Jizo, the guardian of the dead, who was already worshipped on this ground before the monastery was built.

Kencho-ji official history page (Japanese), on Hell Valley, the Jizo hall's relocation, and the thousand Jizo · checked 2026-08-15

Two buildings that came from a shogun's wife's tomb

The present Butsuden (Buddha hall) is said to be the fourth on its site, and it was built somewhere else. It was originally the mausoleum hall of Sugen-in, wife of the second Tokugawa shogun Hidetada, at Zojo-ji in Shiba, Tokyo, and Kencho-ji received it as a gift.

The Karamon gate beside the Buddha hall has a fully recorded history. It was built at Zojo-ji in 1628 as the gate of the same mausoleum, donated to Kencho-ji in 1647 together with the Buddha hall, used as the abbot's front gate, and taken apart and repaired in 2011.

⇒ ⚑ The Buddha hall and gate at the centre of Japan's first purpose-built Zen monastery came from a seventeenth-century tomb complex, moved sixty kilometres and put to new use.

Kencho-ji official grounds page (Japanese), for the Butsuden's and Karamon's provenance and dates · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The temple is laid out along a long, flat, straight line of gate and halls, with a climb at the back. Four things to look at:

1. The junipers in front of the Buddha hall. The temple states they were grown from seeds Rankei Doryu brought from China at the founding, and gives their age as about 760 years. The largest is about 13 metres tall with a trunk 6.5 metres round. ⇒ 🔴 If the temple's account is right, these trees came as seeds in a Chinese monk's luggage and have been growing here since the monastery opened.

2. The Sanmon (main gate), rebuilt in 1775. The temple quotes the founder's words alongside the gate: "Kencho-ji is open to all who study Zen. In these grounds a pure wind, that discriminates against no one, is always blowing."

3. In the Hatto (dharma hall), look up at the ceiling dragon. The dragon was painted for the temple's 750th anniversary. The Hatto was rebuilt in 1814 and is the largest dharma hall in the Kanto region.

4. Climb to Hanso-bo, the shrine at the back, and look at the tengu. The deity of the shrine was brought here from Hoko-ji temple in Shizuoka in 1890 as the temple's guardian. The temple's own English page says 12 statues of tengu stand along the path up. It explains that tengu are usually shown in one of two ways: with a long nose, or with a beak and wings. 🔴 All twelve statues here are the beaked kind, called karasu-tengu ("crow tengu"). They are the attendants of Hanso-bo, the deity whose main role is to protect Kencho-ji from fire. Hanso-bo is believed to have that power because his statue escaped harm when Hoko-ji caught fire.

⚑ At Takao on 26 October you saw one tengu of each kind, in front of the Izuna hall. The same temple page says that from the viewing platform beside the shrine, on a clear day you can see the beaches of Kamakura and Mount Fuji.

Kencho-ji official grounds page (Japanese), for the junipers' origin and dimensions, the Sanmon and its quotation, the Hatto, and the Hanso-bo's 1890 invitation · checked 2026-08-15. Kencho-ji's English Hanso-bo page (text written by the Japan Tourism Agency) for the twelve crow-tengu statues, the two ways tengu are shown, the fire legend and the view of Fuji · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official history (Japanese) Official grounds guide (Japanese) Official page on Hanso-bo (English)
Tsurugaoka Hachimangu鶴岡八幡宮
Shrine · Kamakura · moved here 1180 by Minamoto no Yoritomo · the shogunate's own shrine
▶
What it is

The shrine is older than Kamakura's rise to importance. Minamoto no Yoriyoshi, an ancestor of Minamoto no Yoritomo, had brought the spirit of Iwashimizu Hachimangu from near Kyoto and enshrined Hachiman as the clan's guardian god down by the beach.

🔴 In 1180, Minamoto no Yoritomo moved the shrine to this site, in the same year he established his government at Kamakura. The shrine describes it the same way: that year he set up the base of the first samurai government and moved his ancestors' shrine to where it stands now.

⇒ ⚑ The shrine and the shogunate were founded together. The shrine became the ceremonial centre of eastern Japan, hosting mounted archery, sumo, court dance and the rite of releasing captive animals, and it still holds several of these.

The shrine honours three deities: Emperor Ojin, Empress Jingu and Hime-gami. Hachiman is venerated as the god of martial fortune, and the shrine notes there are more than forty thousand Hachiman shrines in the country.

Tsurugaoka Hachimangu official site, "knowledge" pages (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 The raised road you walked in on was built as a prayer for a safe birth

Wakamiya Oji, the two-kilometre avenue from the beach to the shrine, was modelled on Suzaku Oji, the great central avenue of Kyoto. It was built under Yoritomo's own direction in 1182, and his retainers, including Hojo Tokimasa, carried earth and stone themselves.

🔴 The shogunate's own chronicle records why: Yoritomo built the avenue as a prayer for his wife Hojo Masako's safe childbirth.

The raised causeway running down the middle of Wakamiya Oji is the Dankazura, built on twin earthen embankments. ⚑ It narrows as it approaches the shrine. This is an optical trick that makes the approach look longer and the shrine more distant and imposing. The Dankazura is a National Historic Site, and about 500 metres of it is maintained today.

⇒ The grandest piece of town planning in Kamakura was built as a husband's prayer for his wife.

Tsurugaoka Hachimangu official site (Wakamiya Oji's purpose and construction) and Kamakura City Tourist Association official page (the Dankazura's embankments, narrowing, dating and designation), Japanese, translated · checked 2026-08-15. The comparison with Kyoto's Suzaku Oji from the shrine's own "knowledge" page, English and Japanese · checked 2026-10-17

🔴 The great ginkgo fell in 2010, and what happened next is the point

The shrine's own words: "The sacred tree that had watched over Kamakura's history collapsed in strong winds in the pre-dawn hours of 10 March 2010. At present, root suckers are growing alongside a sacred tree — the 'parent' ginkgo — that was transplanted next to them. A section of the trunk can be seen up close inside the museum café and shop."

⇒ 🔴 There are three things to find: the shoots growing from the original stump, the parent tree moved in beside them, and a piece of the fallen trunk indoors. ⚑ The shrine did not plant a new tree in place of the old one. It kept the stump, set the parent tree beside it, and is letting the shoots grow back.

Tsurugaoka Hachimangu official grounds page (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 The two ponds are a pun about who won

The Genpei ponds were dug on Yoritomo's order. The shrine describes them:

"The Genji Pond, with three islands carrying a wish for 'san' — birth and prosperity — and the Heike Pond, with four islands symbolising 'shi,' death and decline, still face each other today as if recalling the era of the Genji and the Heike."

⇒ 🔴 In Japanese, "three" sounds like the word for birth, and "four" sounds like the word for death. ⚑ The pond of the Genji, who won, has three islands and the pond of the Heike, who lost, has four, so the whole message is carried by the number of islands.

On the Genji pond stands Hataage Benzaiten — named for the raising of the Minamoto banner. The present hall was rebuilt in 1980, the shrine's eight-hundredth year, from a plan of the 1820s.

Tsurugaoka Hachimangu official grounds page (Japanese), the ponds passage quoted verbatim and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The shrine grounds are broad, busy and mostly outdoors, with a long staircase. Five things to look at:

1. Walk in along the Dankazura instead of the road beside it. ⇒ Notice it getting narrower as you go. The narrowing is deliberate, and the causeway was built by hand as a prayer.

2. The two ponds: count the islands. Three in one pond, four in the other. ⇒ Birth and death, expressed in the number of islands.

3. The ginkgo, at the foot of the great stone steps. Find the shoots from the stump and the transplanted parent tree beside them. ⇒ The tree is being left to grow back instead of being replaced.

4. The Maiden (mai-den, the dance stage) in the middle of the grounds. It stands on the site of the corridor where Shizuka Gozen danced in longing for Yoshitsune. Minamoto no Yoshitsune was Yoritomo's half-brother and a famous warrior; Shizuka Gozen was a celebrated dancer and Yoshitsune's mistress. Yoritomo fell out with Yoshitsune after the shogunate was set up. While Yoshitsune was on the run, Shizuka was separated from him, captured and brought to Kamakura. 🔴 In 1186 she was ordered to dance for Yoritomo, and she recited poems of longing for Yoshitsune. Yoritomo was angered and wanted her executed. His wife Masako intervened and saved her, arguing that she would have shown the same feelings in Shizuka's place. ⇒ ⚑ Later in the trip you follow Yoshitsune at Kurama on 1 November and at Yoshino on 5 November. This stage is where Shizuka Gozen, the woman who stayed behind, was made to perform.

5. Maruyama Inari, a shrine off to the side. It is the shrine's oldest surviving building, from the Muromachi period, and an Important Cultural Property, surrounded by red banners. ⇒ Most buildings here are younger than they look; Maruyama Inari is the exception.

Tsurugaoka Hachimangu official grounds pages (the Maiden and Shizuka Gozen, the ginkgo, the ponds, Maruyama Inari); Kamakura City Tourist Association for the Dankazura · checked 2026-08-15. Yoshitsune, Shizuka's capture, the 1186 dance and Masako's argument from the Japan Tourism Agency multilingual commentary database, "Maiden (Lower Worship Hall)" · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (Japanese) Official grounds guide (Japanese) Kamakura City Tourist Association on the Dankazura (Japanese)

Thursday 29 October — Ikaruga & Nishinokyō

Nara plain · Horyu-ji in the morning, Yakushi-ji and Toshodai-ji in the afternoon · three temples spanning roughly 150 years of Japanese architecture
Horyu-ji法隆寺
Buddhist temple · Ikaruga, Nara · founded c. 607, rebuilt c. 700
▶
What it is

A walled temple complex on the plain west of Nara, in the town of Ikaruga. Its western precinct (a gate, a pagoda, a golden hall and the roofed corridor around them) is, in the temple's own words, the oldest group of wooden buildings in the world. It is the head temple of the Shotoku school (Shotoku-shu), a position it has held only since 1950.

Official site (temple precinct page) · checked 2026-08-15

Why it was built — a political statement beside the regent's palace

Prince Shotoku set up his palace at Ikaruga in 601. The temple had been started for his father, Emperor Yomei, and was finished around 607 under Empress Suiko.

The date matters. Shotoku was regent in the decades when the Japanese court was still deciding whether Buddhism was a foreign cult to be suppressed or a tool for running a centralised state. A generation earlier, the Soga and Mononobe clans had fought over that question with weapons. Building a monumental Buddhist temple right beside the regent's own home was more than private devotion. It was a public statement that the new religion and the new form of government had arrived together, and that the court had taken the Buddhist side in a fight that had recently cost lives.

Official site (precinct page) · Prince Shotoku and Empress Suiko, English Wikipedia

What is worshipped here — and the school it walked away from

For most of its history Horyu-ji belonged to the Hosso school, the Japanese form of Yogacara Buddhism. Hosso's central claim is that what we take to be an outside world is built by our own consciousness. It is one of the most demanding philosophical positions in East Asian Buddhism, and it was Horyu-ji's intellectual tradition for over a thousand years.

In 1950 Horyu-ji left the Hosso school and declared itself head of a new school named after Prince Shotoku. That was an unusual step. A Japanese Buddhist school is normally centred on a buddha, a bodhisattva or a body of scripture; this one is centred on a dead statesman. The idea was not new. Devotion to Shotoku as a semi-divine figure (Taishi shinko, "faith in the Prince") had been growing since the Heian period, and credited him with visions, prophecy and earlier lives in China. The 1950 decision made it official. ⇒ It formalised something Japanese religion had been doing quietly for a thousand years: turning a political founder into a religious one.

Worth remembering this afternoon: Yakushi-ji, which you visit after lunch, is one of the two head temples of the Hosso school that Horyu-ji left. The two temples are twenty minutes apart, and they stand on opposite sides of Horyu-ji's 1950 decision: Horyu-ji left, Yakushi-ji stayed.

Official site (school affiliation) · Hosso / East Asian Yogacara, English Wikipedia · Japanese-language reference works on the Shotoku school (no English edition found)

Are these really the original buildings? A forty-year argument, settled by an excavation

From the Meiji period onward, Japanese historians were bitterly divided over one question. The Nihon Shoki, the 8th-century imperial chronicle, records that the temple at Ikaruga burned to the ground in 670. But the buildings standing here look older than anything that could have been built after that date.

One camp trusted the chronicle and argued that the temple had been rebuilt; the other trusted the buildings and argued that the chronicle was wrong. The historian Kita Sadakichi summed up what was at stake for his whole field in one sentence: if you are permitted to reject the documents, there is no such thing as history.

The argument ran for about four decades and ended in 1939. That year the archaeologist Ishida Mosaku excavated a site just southeast of the western precinct, known as the Wakakusa-garan (若草伽藍, "the Wakakusa temple compound"). He found the foundations of an earlier temple on a different alignment. Its pagoda and golden hall stood in a north-south line, one behind the other, in the older continental arrangement. Today's pagoda and golden hall stand side by side. Nara Prefecture's heritage record states the result: from the roof tiles and other finds, it became likely that the Wakakusa-garan was the original Horyu-ji built by Prince Shotoku, the temple the chronicle says burned. ⇒ The buildings you walk through are a rebuild of the late 7th or early 8th century, put up beside the burned site, and they are still the oldest surviving wooden buildings on earth. Both camps were half right, and it took the excavation to show which half.

Japanese-language reference works (Kotobank, Historist) on the Wakakusa excavation · Nara Prefecture heritage database 「いかす・なら」, page "Horyu-ji (Wakakusa-garan site, western and eastern precincts)" · the temple's own Japanese precinct page records the 670 fire and the rebuilding by the early Nara period, but not the excavation · checked 2026-10-17

Architecture and how the buildings were used

The layout breaks the Chinese rule it inherited. Chinese monasteries of the period put the pagoda and the main hall on a single line, one behind the other. Horyu-ji puts them side by side. The two buildings do different jobs: a pagoda is a reliquary that holds the Buddha's physical remains, and a golden hall holds his image. Setting them side by side treats the two as equals, whereas the Chinese plan put one in front of the other. It is an early, visible case of continental architecture being rearranged in Japan to a new plan.

The pagoda was built to hold relics; it has no room to enter. It is about 32.5 m tall, built around a central pillar (shinbashira) cut from a cypress felled in 594. The relics it was built to house are said to be buried about three metres below its base.

The golden hall is where the temple did its work. It has a two-storey hip-and-gable roof above a lower skirt roof (mokoshi), and its deep eaves rest on cloud-shaped bracket arms (kumo-hijiki). The temple states that this bracket form is unique to the Asuka period, which makes it one of the clearest ways to date a building by eye. Inside stand the Shakyamuni triad, a Yakushi (the healing buddha), an Amida triad, the Four Heavenly Kings and a Kisshoten. The temple's rites were performed in this room, and its monks studied Hosso doctrine here. It is a working hall, never rearranged as a museum and left largely as it was.

Official site, English edition: Golden Hall · Middle Gate · Five-Storied Pagoda pages · checked 2026-08-15

The fire that wrote a national law

On the morning of 26 January 1949, the golden hall was taken apart for repair and painters were inside copying its murals when fire broke out. It destroyed most of the murals, a cycle of 7th-century Buddhist wall paintings regarded as the finest in East Asia.

Until then, protecting cultural property in Japan was largely left to whoever happened to own it. The year after the fire, the government passed the Cultural Properties Protection Law (30 May 1950), which put the state in charge of designating and funding preservation. Every "National Treasure" and "Important Cultural Property" label elsewhere in this almanac is issued under that law. In 1955, 26 January was made the national Cultural Properties Fire Prevention Day. The Agency for Cultural Affairs still gives the reason plainly: it is the day the golden hall of Horyu-ji burned, and January and February are the months when fires are most likely.

⇒ Japan's system for protecting its heritage was written in response to the fire at this one building. The salvaged murals have been kept in a purpose-built storehouse on the grounds since 1952.

Agency for Cultural Affairs, Cultural Properties Fire Prevention Day page · checked 2026-08-15 · official site (storehouse)

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The temple has two walled precincts: the western one (the old one) and the eastern one, built around the octagonal Hall of Dreams. The Gallery of Temple Treasures lies between them. The site is large and flat, the walking is easy, and the ground is gravel throughout. Six things are worth stopping for, roughly in the order you reach them:

1. The middle gate — look at the middle of the doorway. There is a pillar standing in the centre of the entrance. You cannot walk straight through the front gate of this temple; you go round one side or the other. No other major Japanese temple gate has this. The temple's own explanation is structural: the gate is so wide that it needed an extra support. It is the most argued-over detail in the complex, and it is the first thing you see.

2. The brackets under every set of eaves — the ones shaped like clouds. Look up where the roof overhangs, on the gate, the golden hall and the pagoda. The stacked arms holding up the overhang curl into cloud shapes (kumo-hijiki), and the temple states that this shape is unique to Asuka-period architecture. ⇒ This detail is worth remembering, because you are going to Toshodai-ji this afternoon. There the same job is done by a squared-off, three-tiered bracket system, which Toshodai-ji names as the marker of its own century. Look up at both and you can see roughly 150 years of change in structural engineering for yourself, with no other background needed.

3. The corridor pillars — look for the swelling. The roofed corridor around the western precinct stands on columns that bulge slightly at mid-height, a shaping called entasis. It is the same optical correction used on Greek columns. It is easiest to see if you look along the row of columns instead of at a single one. You will meet the idea again this afternoon: Toshodai-ji's own pages compare its colonnade to the Parthenon.

4. The base of the pagoda — go round all four sides. You cannot enter the tower, but set into its four sides at ground level are cave-like scenes, made in 711, holding 97 small clay figures. Each side shows a different scene: the north shows the Buddha's death with his disciples mourning, and is the one people stop at; the west shows his relics being divided up; the east, a layman debating with the bodhisattva of wisdom; the south, the future buddha preaching. Most visitors see one side and move on.

5. Inside the golden hall — two sculptural styles in one room. The Shakyamuni triad is early 7th century. The figures face straight ahead, are symmetrical, have flat drapery, and wear a fixed archaic smile: a style brought from the continent more or less unchanged. The Amida triad in the same hall is later, and visibly softer and rounder. ⇒ Standing between the two triads, you can see in one room the point where Japanese sculpture stopped copying continental models and began adapting them. No museum label points this out.

6. The Hall of Dreams — and the statue that is normally sealed. The octagonal hall in the eastern precinct was commissioned in 739 by the scholar-priest Gyoshin, on the site of Prince Shotoku's own palace; Shotoku died there in 622. Inside is the Kuse Kannon: 179 cm tall, carved from a single block of camphor wood and gilded head to toe. It is a hidden image: the temple shows it for about a month in spring and about a month in autumn, and keeps it closed away the rest of the year. 🔴 The autumn showing runs 22 October to 22 November, and you are there on 29 October, so it will be on view. It is the one thing at Horyu-ji that cannot be seen on most days of the year.

Official site, English edition: Middle Gate · Covered Corridor · Five-Storied Pagoda · Golden Hall · Hall of Dreams · checked 2026-08-15. Unveiling window from the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08); the temple's English page states only "about a month each during the spring and fall"

Links
Official site (English) Official — the pagoda Wikipedia
Yakushi-ji薬師寺
Buddhist temple · Nishinokyo, Nara · founded 680 · head temple of the Hosso school
▶
What it is

A temple laid out around two pagodas facing each other across the main hall. The temple states that this arrangement was the first of its kind in Japan, and it is still called the Yakushi-ji layout. Of everything you see, only one building is original. The rest is 20th-century reconstruction. The temple does not hide this; the rebuilding is part of the story it tells about itself.

Official site, English edition (About; The Temple Complex) · checked 2026-08-15

Why it was built — a vow made over a sickbed

The temple gives its founding plainly: it was established in 680 by Emperor Tenmu to pray for the recovery of his consort from illness. That consort later reigned in her own right as Empress Jito.

The choice of buddha was deliberate. Yakushi is the Medicine Buddha, the buddha of healing. A temple built to him for one sick person was a vow made in the form of a building, and the size of the building matched the size of the request. The emperor died before the temple was finished, and his widow, the woman it was built to cure, completed it. ⇒ The temple outlived both the illness and the marriage behind it. That was roughly the pattern of Japanese state Buddhism at the time: private vows made by emperors became permanent public institutions.

Official site, English edition (About) · Empress Jito, English Wikipedia

What is taught here — the school Horyu-ji left

Yakushi-ji is a head temple of the Hosso school, the Japanese form of Yogacara Buddhism. Hosso is the "consciousness-only" tradition: it holds that the world we take to be outside us is produced by the mind. That is a philosophical position more than a devotional one, and it made Hosso the most academic of the old Nara schools. A temple like Yakushi-ji was as much a college of doctrine as a place of worship.

Yakushi-ji is the other side of the Horyu-ji story from this morning. Horyu-ji belonged to the Hosso school for over a thousand years and left it in 1950 to found a school centred on Prince Shotoku. Yakushi-ji stayed. ⇒ The two temples are twenty minutes apart: one left the tradition that the other still leads, and you see both in a single day.

Hosso / East Asian Yogacara, English Wikipedia · school affiliation from both temples' own pages

One original building, and a temple rebuilt with sutra-copying donations

Fire and war destroyed nearly everything here. The East Pagoda is the only wooden structure on the site to have survived since the founding; the temple says so in exactly those terms. The temple's timeline dates the pagoda to 730, so it has stood for almost 1,300 years.

⚑ Why the pagoda is fifty years younger than the temple. The founding date of 680 belongs to an earlier site. Emperor Tenmu died before the temple was finished, and Empress Jito built it in the new capital of Fujiwara-kyo. After the court moved to Heijo-kyo, the capital at Nara, in 710, Yakushi-ji moved too. The temple's own timeline dates the move to the present site to 718 and the building of the East Pagoda to 730. ⇒ The 680 founding and the 730 pagoda are both correct dates; they belong to two different sites.

The way the other buildings came back is unusual. From 1968 the temple paid for its reconstruction through o-shakyo: donations made by copying out sutras by hand. Members of the public paid to trace out scripture, and the money collected rebuilt the main hall, the West Pagoda and the Great Lecture Hall over the following decades. ⇒ A 7th-century layout was restored in the 20th century through a medieval devotional practice, carried out on a large scale by ordinary people. Few major Japanese temples have modern buildings with a social history like this; most were simply paid for out of a budget.

Official site, English edition (O-Shakyo; About) · reconstruction sequence from the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08) · Official site (Japanese): About Yakushi-ji (built at Fujiwara-kyo, moved with the capital to Heijo-kyo) · history timeline (718 move, 730 East Pagoda) · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The temple is compact and flat, one minute from the station. Toshodai-ji is ten minutes up the lane, and you see both this afternoon. Four things are worth stopping for:

1. Count the roofs on the East Pagoda — then count the floors. It is 33.6 m tall and looks like a six-storey tower. It has three storeys. Each real roof has a smaller skirt roof (mokoshi) beneath it, so you see six roof edges on a building with only three levels. The temple calls the effect a "grand rhythmical balance", and it credits the art historian Ernest Fenollosa with calling the pagoda "frozen music". ⇒ Stand back far enough to see the whole outline before you go closer. The East Pagoda, from about 730, is the only building here that is almost 1,300 years old, and this six-roofs-on-three-storeys effect is why it is famous.

2. Look at the very top of the East Pagoda. The finial carries a bronze openwork ornament called a suien, a "water flame", cut with celestial figures dancing and playing flutes. It was made as a charm against fire: water placed at the top of a wooden tower. ⇒ Fire destroyed every other original building on this site, so the charm on the one building that survived is worth thirty seconds of looking up.

3. Compare the two pagodas closely. The East Pagoda is 8th century; the West Pagoda is a 1981 reconstruction. They face each other, built to the same design about 1,250 years apart, and the new one is painted in the bright vermilion and green that the old one has lost. ⇒ This is the clearest before-and-after in Nara: the West Pagoda shows what the East Pagoda looked like when it was new, and the East Pagoda shows what thirteen centuries of weather do to a building. Very few sites let you see both states of the same building at once.

4. The Yakushi Triad in the main hall. The three statues are bronze, from the founding period, and now almost black. The temple calls them "the world's most beautiful Buddhist statues". Whatever you make of that claim, look at the surface: this is what ancient bronze becomes when it is never restored. Look also at the pedestal of the central figure, carved with foreign motifs: a mix of styles carried along the Silk Road to its eastern end.

Official site, English edition: About · The Temple Complex · East Pagoda page (33.6 m, three storeys reading as six, the central pillar, the water-flame finial, the Fenollosa attribution) · checked 2026-08-15. Reconstruction dates from the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

Links
Official site (English) Official — the East Pagoda Wikipedia
Toshodai-ji唐招提寺
Buddhist temple · Nishinokyo, Nara · founded 759 by the Chinese monk Ganjin
▶
What it is

The temple founded by Ganjin (Chinese: Jianzhen), a monk invited from China to fix a problem that Japanese Buddhism could not fix on its own: there was nobody in Japan properly qualified to ordain monks. The temple describes its Golden Hall as the greatest surviving structure of the Tempyo era (the 8th century). Of the three temples you see today, Toshodai-ji is the quietest.

Official site, English edition · checked 2026-08-15

Why it exists — a bureaucratic crisis solved by a blind man who crossed the sea

By the mid-8th century Japan had temples, sutras and monks in quantity, and one problem underneath all of them: nobody in the country was properly qualified to ordain anyone. A Buddhist ordination requires a quorum of masters, themselves already ordained, who confer the precepts in an unbroken line going back to India. Japan had no such line, so strictly speaking its entire clergy was self-appointed.
