Ordained monks were also exempt from tax and labour, so this was a money problem for the state as much as a question of doctrine for the clergy.

Emperor Shomu invited Ganjin to Japan to provide the unbroken line of ordination that Japan lacked. The temple's own account of the journey is stark: twelve years, five failed attempts, and the loss of his eyesight, before he finally crossed the ocean and reached Nara in 754. He was in his sixties and blind when he arrived.

He then did what he had been brought to do. An ordination platform was built in front of the Great Buddha Hall at Todai-ji, and there he conferred the precepts on senior Japanese priests and also, in person, on the retired Emperor Shomu and Empress Koken. ⇒ The legitimacy of the Japanese Buddhist clergy dates from a ceremony conducted by a blind foreigner who had spent twelve years failing to get there. Toshodai-ji was given to him in 759 as his own base.

Official site, English edition (About Toshodaiji) · checked 2026-08-15

What is taught here — rules, not metaphysics

Ganjin's school is Ritsu, which centres on vinaya, the monastic discipline: the body of rules governing how a monk lives, eats, dresses and is ordained. The temple states it was the first temple in Japan devoted to one of the Chinese Buddhist denominations.

Compare Ritsu with Hosso, the school of the two temples before this one. Yakushi-ji belongs to Hosso, and Horyu-ji did for most of its history; Hosso is a school built on a philosophical claim about the nature of mind. Ritsu is built on procedure. It asks whether a ceremony was performed correctly by people entitled to perform it, and leaves aside the question of what is ultimately real. ⇒ In one day you see both things a religion needs in order to become an institution: a doctrine and a rulebook.

Official site, English edition (About; Ritsu School Academy)

Architecture — the Golden Hall and its open colonnade

The Golden Hall dates from the late 8th century, is a National Treasure, and has a hipped tile roof. Its front is seven bays wide, and the bays are not equal: the central one is about 4.7 m, and they narrow steadily to about 3.3 m at the ends. The eaves rest on a three-tiered bracket system (mitesaki), which the temple names as the marker of its construction period.

The front bay is open: a colonnade with no wall, so the front of the building is a row of freestanding columns. The temple's own pages compare these columns to the Parthenon. ⇒ The comparison holds up. The columns swell at mid-height in the same way as Horyu-ji's corridor pillars, and because the colonnade is open, the swelling is easy to see. The temple's English page also says the Golden Hall's "concise beauty" has been written about in many famous old poems.

Official site: Golden Hall page (bay widths, mitesaki brackets, open front bay) · English edition (the Parthenon comparison) · checked 2026-08-15 · English edition ("concise beauty", old poems) · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Toshodai-ji is a ten-minute walk north of Yakushi-ji, on the same lane. The grounds are wooded and level, and far fewer people visit than at Horyu-ji or Yakushi-ji. Four things are worth stopping for:

1. Stand square in front of the Golden Hall before going in. The building was designed to be seen from here. Notice the narrowing bays: the columns are not evenly spaced. The gap is widest at the centre and gets smaller towards the ends, which draws the eye to the middle and makes the hall look larger and more solid than its actual width. The effect is deliberate, and once you have noticed it you keep seeing it.

2. Look up at the brackets, and compare them with Horyu-ji's this morning. Here the eaves rest on three squared-off tiers, each stepping further out. At Horyu-ji the same job was done by cloud-shaped arms, which that temple states are unique to the Asuka period. Both temples publish the fact that their own bracket form dates them. ⇒ Roughly 150 years separate the two systems, and you have seen both in one day. Comparing them is the easiest way to learn to date a Japanese building by eye.

3. Inside the hall, count the arms. Three great figures stand together: the Rushana Buddha in the centre, over 3 m tall with a halo 5.15 m high; the Yakushi Nyorai at right, 3.36 m; and at left the Thousand-armed Kannon, 5.36 m. The Kannon was made with a thousand arms and now has 953: 42 large and 911 small. ⇒ The temple publishes that exact count. Nearly fifty arms have been lost over twelve centuries, and knowing this lets you see the statue as an object with a history of damage, as well as a religious symbol.

4. The lecture hall behind the Golden Hall was not built for a temple. It was an administrative building of the imperial palace, which the court gave to Ganjin and had moved here. ⇒ It is the only surviving building from the Nara palace complex, and it stands in a temple because the government gave a monk one of its office buildings. What you are looking at is secular 8th-century state architecture.

⚑ What you will not see: Ganjin's dry-lacquer portrait statue. It was made by his own disciples, is a National Treasure, and is one of the oldest portrait sculptures in Japan. It is kept in the Miei-do and shown only a few days a year, around 6 June. You can walk to his tomb, which is in the eastern woods.

Official site: Golden Hall page (bay widths, statue heights, 953 arms) · English edition (the lecture hall's palace origin, the mausoleum, the portrait statue's June opening) · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official — the Golden Hall Wikipedia

Friday 30 October — the Yamanobe-no-michi

The oldest road in Japan · walked north to south from Tenri to Miwa · four sanctuaries, three of which worship without a building
Isonokami Jingu石上神宮
Shinto shrine · Tenri, Nara · the northern end of the walk
▶
What it is

The 8th-century chronicles give the title jingu to only two shrines: this one and Ise. It sits in cedar woods above Tenri, its worship hall is a National Treasure, and long-tailed roosters wander loose on the approach. Admission is free and there is no interior to visit.

Tenri city tourism office · the trip's own verified record (2026-07-29)

Why the state cared about it — a shrine that was an armoury

Isonokami was the shrine of the Mononobe, the clan whose hereditary business was arms and law enforcement, and it served as the weapons store of the Yamato court. Few shrines have had that job, and it explains what is kept here: iron shields, armour and swords.

The Mononobe were also the clan that lost. They led the faction that fought to keep Buddhism out of Japan, and the pro-Buddhist Soga destroyed them in 587. The winning side of that same conflict built Horyu-ji, which you visit on Thursday 29 October. ⇒ Over two days you see the temple the victory built and the shrine of the family that was beaten.

Tenri city tourism office · Mononobe–Soga conflict, English Wikipedia

The god that was dug up

Isonokami originally had no main hall. Behind the worship hall lay a forbidden precinct — ground nobody was permitted to enter — and the tradition held that the shrine's sacred sword was buried in it.

In August 1874 the chief priest obtained government permission and dug up the forbidden precinct. He found beads, ring-pommel swords, stone objects and gilt-bronze ornaments, and also a sword, which was identified as the Futsu-no-Mitama, the sacred sword of the tradition. A second sword was found in a further dig in 1878. A main hall was then built to house what had been excavated, and finished in 1913.

⇒ For most of its existence the shrine worshipped a patch of forbidden ground. Then a Meiji-era priest excavated the ground, found the god, and built the god a house. Isonokami is one of the very few places where you can date the moment an ancient form of worship was replaced by a modern one, and the shrine's own clergy made the change, with a spade and a government permit. The excavated objects are Important Cultural Properties and are held at the Nara National Museum, which you visit on Saturday 31 October.

Agency for Cultural Affairs heritage database · Japanese-language shrine histories · designation 28 December 1897

The seven-branched sword

The shrine's most famous object is the Shichishito — a 4th-century iron blade with six branches projecting from a central shaft, a shape with no practical use whatever. For a thousand years it was assumed to be an odd halberd.

In the 1870s the chief priest Kan Masatomo (1824–1897), the same man who dug up the forbidden precinct, brushed the rust off the blade and found an inscription inlaid in gold, running across both faces. It is corroded, partly unreadable, and scholars have argued over it for more than a century. What it seems to record is that the sword was a gift from the king of Baekje, a kingdom on the Korean peninsula.

⇒ The sword matters far beyond the shrine because the wording of the inscription decides what kind of relationship Baekje and early Japan had: tribute, alliance or something else. That wording is exactly the part that cannot be read with certainty. An 8th-century Japanese chronicle records a "seven-branched sword" presented in the fifty-second year of Empress Jingu's reign; if that is this sword, it dates to the later 4th century. ⛔ You will not see it. It is a National Treasure and extremely fragile (a few millimetres thick at its thinnest), and it is kept in climate-controlled storage.

Japan Ministry of Land, Infrastructure, Transport and Tourism — official multilingual commentary database, "Shichishito Sword" (English) · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free and open-air, with no ticket. It is the first stop, at the northern end of the walk, so you reach it early in the day. Three things to look at:

1. The roosters. Long-tailed roosters roam the cedar-lined approach. In Shinto, chickens are messengers of the gods, and the shrine keeps these birds on purpose. They are the first thing you meet when you arrive.

2. The worship hall, and the ground behind it. The haiden (worship hall) is a National Treasure. Standing in front of it, you face the forbidden precinct: for most of the shrine's life, that ground was the entire object of worship. The main hall behind the worship hall exists only because the ground was dug up in 1874.

3. The worship hall of Izumo-Takeo, a subordinate shrine on the same site, is also a National Treasure. Two National Treasure halls on one free site with no ticket is rare, so the second hall is worth the detour.

Tenri city tourism office · the trip's own verified record (2026-07-29)

Links
Government commentary — the Shichishito (English) Tenri tourism (Japanese) Wikipedia
Chogaku-ji長岳寺
Buddhist temple · Tenri, Nara · founded 824 · the midpoint of the walk
▶
What it is

A Buddhist temple, the only one among the stops on this walk (the others are all shrines), and the natural place to break the walk halfway. It was founded in 824 by Kukai under a decree of Emperor Junna.

Temple's own site · Japanese-language reference works · checked 2026-08-15

Why a Buddhist temple was built for a shrine

Chogaku-ji was built as the jingu-ji (shrine-temple) of Oyamato Shrine, further north on the same road. ⚠ Oyamato is a different shrine from Omiwa at the southern end of the walk, and the two are easily confused.

The jingu-ji is one of the strangest and most revealing institutions in Japanese religion: a Buddhist temple built inside or beside a Shinto shrine in order to preach to the shrine's god. The reasoning was that the kami were themselves unenlightened beings trapped in the cycle of rebirth, so they needed the sutras read to them. For roughly a thousand years this was normal, and Buddhism and Shinto in Japan were practised as one combined religion.

⇒ The arrangement ended by government order. The Meiji separation of Buddhism and Shinto (from 1868) forced the two apart, and most jingu-ji were dissolved. That Chogaku-ji survived at all, on a road whose other stops are shrines, is the visible remnant of the thousand-year arrangement. A temple among shrines looks out of place only if you assume Shinto and Buddhism were always separate.

Temple's own site (Japanese): "founded by Kobo Daishi as the jingu-ji of Oyamato Shrine" · shinbutsu bunri, English Wikipedia

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Paid entry. The temple is small: allow about twenty minutes, or longer if the head priest is reading the hell screen aloud (see 3 below). Three things to look at, and the first is a genuine landmark in the history of sculpture:

1. The Amida triad: look at the eyes. Inscriptions inside the statues date them to 1151, which makes them the oldest securely dated example in Japan of gyokugan: the technique of setting crystal into the eyes from behind, so that they catch the light and look wet. ⇒ Earlier Buddhist statues had carved or painted eyes and looked like carvings. Statues with crystal eyes seem to look back at you. These are the earliest dated statues in Japan made to look alive, and the effect is slightly unsettling, and the room is small enough to see the eyes properly.

2. The bell-tower gate (shoro-mon, 鐘楼門) is an Important Cultural Property from the Heian period, a gate and a belfry in one structure. The temple calls it the oldest bell gate in Japan and the only building left from Kukai's founding. It takes its name from the upper storey, which still has the remains of the frame a bell once hung from. You walk through it to get in.

3. 🔴 The hell screen: the original is on show while you are here. The temple owns a large hell painting by Kano Sanraku, roughly 11 m by 4 m. For most of the year the main hall shows a replica. From 23 October to the end of November the original is hung in its place, and you are here on 30 October. ⚑ The head priest reads the painting aloud, scene by scene, at 1 pm on Saturdays and Sundays. The 30th is a Friday, so there is no scheduled reading; on weekdays a reading happens only when a group has booked one, and individual visitors may sit in.

Temple's own site (Japanese) · display window and reading schedule from the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08) · Temple's own site, cultural properties list (bell-tower gate: Heian period, oldest bell gate, only building from the founding) · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (Japanese) Wikipedia
Hibara Shrine檜原神社
Shinto shrine · subordinate shrine of Omiwa · "the original Ise"
▶
What it is

A clearing on the hillside with a torii standing in the open and nothing behind it. No main hall, no worship hall, no roof. What the torii frames is Mount Miwa, and the mountain is what is being worshipped.

Omiwa Shrine · the trip's own verified record (2026-07-29)

Why it is called the original Ise

Amaterasu, the sun goddess and ancestress of the imperial line, was originally worshipped inside the palace itself. According to the chronicles, Emperor Sujin came to find this unbearable: he feared the power of a god kept under his own roof, and had her removed.

Sujin entrusted the sacred regalia to his daughter Toyosukiiri-hime, who enshrined the goddess out here, in the open, at a place the chronicles call Yamato Kasanui-no-mura. This site is held to be that place. The charge later passed to Yamato-hime, who carried the goddess on a long search for a permanent home and finally settled her at the Isuzu river in Ise.

⇒ The Grand Shrine of Ise began here, in a field with a gate in it. The Association of Shinto Shrines was founded revering Ise as its head shrine, as the Association's own founding sentence says. Ise started as a temporary arrangement for a goddess an emperor was afraid to live with. ⚑ The rite for Toyosukiiri-hime is held in this precinct on 5 November at 11:00, six days after your visit on 30 October.

Omiwa Shrine · Japanese-language accounts of the Moto-Ise tradition, drawn from the Nihon Shoki narrative · Association of Shinto Shrines (jinjahoncho.or.jp), its own founding sentence — "the Association was established revering Ise Jingu as its honso" — for Ise's standing · checked 2026-08-22

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free and open-air, with no gates and no opening hours, so you can walk in at any time. It is about forty minutes north of Omiwa on the trail. Two things to look at:

1. The triple torii, which you can see here. Omiwa Shrine has a mitsu-torii: three torii joined into a single unit, a form found almost nowhere else. At Omiwa it stands inside the forbidden precinct and you cannot see it. At Hibara the same form stands in the open and you can walk up to it. ⇒ Hibara is where you can see what Omiwa's most distinctive object looks like, which is a good reason not to treat Hibara as a minor stop on the way south.

2. Turn round and look west. The precinct is open on that side and looks out over the Nara basin to the twin peaks of Nijo-yama. The shrine has no buildings, so apart from the torii, the view west is the thing to see here.

Omiwa Shrine · the trip's own verified record (2026-07-29) · Japanese-language shrine guides for the mitsu-torii comparison

Links
Omiwa Shrine (Japanese) Omiwa Shrine (English) Omiwa Shrine — Hibara Shrine page (Japanese)
Omiwa Jinja大神神社
Shinto shrine · Sakurai, Nara · the southern end of the walk
▶
What it is

The shrine describes itself as the oldest in the country, and its claim rests on something you can verify by looking: there is no main hall, and there never has been. In its own words, "there is no main shrine even today, and the ancient form of worshipping has been handed down as it was."

Official site, English edition · checked 2026-08-15

What is being worshipped — the mountain, not a building

Behind the worship hall stands Mount Miwa, and the mountain is the shrine. The shrine's English page says so directly: "the mountain itself makes the main shrine where the divine spirit is enshrined". Mount Miwa is a shintai-zan, a mountain that is itself the body of the god, about 16 km around.

⇒ Almost every Shinto shrine you will ever enter is a building that contains a god. At Omiwa the god is the mountain, and the building stands at its foot. The shrine has only a worship hall because no hall is needed to house the god: the god is already there, as roughly four million square metres of forest.

Official site, English edition (About) · checked 2026-08-15

The god — and why sake brewers come here

The deity is Omononushi. The shrine's own account has him working with Sukunahikona to cultivate the land and to establish "every industry such as agriculture, industry and commerce", and lists his particular concerns as curing disease, brewing, medicine-making and marriage.

⇒ A god of both brewing and medicine makes sense. In an early agricultural society, brewing and medicine were the same skill: controlled fermentation, applied to grain or to a body. A god who governs both is a god of the technologies that made settled life possible. ⚑ Because of this, Omiwa is the shrine of the sake trade: brewers across Japan hang a ball of cedar sprigs outside the door when a new batch is ready, and the cedar comes from this mountain.

Official site, English edition (About; enshrined deity) · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free, open at any hour, five minutes from JR Miwa station at the end of the walk. Three things:

1. The triple torii, which you cannot see here. The mitsu-torii stands behind the worship hall, inside the forbidden precinct. ⇒ Hibara Shrine, forty minutes north on the trail, has the same form standing in the open, so Hibara is where you actually see this form. Walking north to south, you reach Hibara first, so by the time you arrive at Omiwa you have already seen what is hidden here. That is why the direction of this walk matters.

2. The cedar balls. Look for sugidama, dense spheres of cedar sprigs, hanging at the shrine and around the sake-related buildings. Brown means the batch has matured; green means it is new. Sake brewers all over Japan use this signal, and it started here on Mount Miwa.

3. You can climb the mountain, but almost certainly not on this day. Registration is at Sai Shrine within the grounds: you give your details, receive a sash, and climb as an act of worship (it is not treated as a hike). ⚠ Reception is 9:00–10:30, and you must be back down and report your descent by 13:30, but walking north to south you reach Omiwa at the end of the day. ⇒ The direction of the walk rules out the climb, whatever your fitness.

Official site: Mount Miwa ascent page (reception hours, registration at Sai Shrine, sash) · English edition (About) · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official — the mountain ascent (Japanese) Wikipedia

Saturday 31 October — Todai-ji, the Shoso-in Exhibition and Kasuga

Nara Park · the state religion of the 8th century, its treasury, and the private shrine of the family that ran it
Todai-ji東大寺
Buddhist temple · Nara · founded 8th century · head temple of the Kegon school
▶
What it is

A Buddhist temple built to hold a bronze Buddha roughly 15 m high, in a hall that is among the largest wooden buildings in the world. It is the head temple of the Kegon school, the Japanese form of Huayan Buddhism. Kegon's central idea is that every thing that exists contains and reflects every other thing.

Official site, English edition · checked 2026-08-15

Why it was built: a statue ordered in a time of disaster

Most accounts skip why Emperor Shomu ordered the Great Buddha. He did not order it out of piety in a settled reign: his reign was in crisis. The temple's own English history names what he was facing: "struggles for political authority, small pox epidemics, and rebellions", and says he came to believe "religion was the only means to heal such troubled times and to seek peace for the nation."

The smallpox epidemic of the 730s is estimated to have killed a substantial share of the population. It also devastated the governing class, killing all four Fujiwara brothers who dominated the court. In 741 Shomu ordered a national network of provincial monasteries, the kokubun-ji, one in each province. In 743 he issued the edict for the Buddha itself.

⇒ The statue in front of you is the state's response to an epidemic and a political crisis, made in bronze. The bronze took three years to cast and was finished in 749. The hall followed in 751, and the consecration was held in 752. Tōdai-ji stood at the head of the kokubun-ji system as "a center for rituals for the peace of the nation and the prosperity of the people", so it was a national temple in the literal, administrative sense.

Official site, English edition — History, 8th century · checked 2026-08-15

Why this Buddha, and not another

The figure is Vairocana (Rushana in Japanese). He was chosen for his place in Kegon doctrine, and his appearance had nothing to do with it. The Kegon school is built on the Avatamsaka (Flower Garland) Sutra, in which Vairocana is the cosmic buddha: every other buddha and every phenomenon is an expression of him. Shomu's edict, as the temple quotes it, speaks of "perfectly harmonious worlds" in which "one encounters no obstacles", and extends the benefit to "all in heaven and on earth, even animals and plants."

⇒ The religious message and the political message are the same. A doctrine in which everything is bound up with everything else and nothing obstructs anything suits a ruler trying to hold a fractured state together. A colossal Vairocana at the centre of a network of provincial temples puts that doctrine on show on a huge scale, in metal, where everyone can see it.

Official site, English edition — History, 8th century (the edict's language) · checked 2026-08-15

Who actually built it

Two men built it. Roben (689–773) was the first chief abbot. Gyoki (668–749) made the project possible. He was a wandering monk with an enormous popular following, who had spent his career building roads, bridges and irrigation works for ordinary people. The same state had earlier suppressed him for preaching to commoners without permission.

Then the court needed him. In the temple's words, Gyoki "regimented the efforts of the people through the solicitation of donations." ⇒ The state could order a colossal statue but could not pay for it, so it took back into favour a monk it had persecuted and used his popularity to raise the money and the labour. Gyoki died in 749, three years before the consecration.

Official site, English edition — History, 8th century · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

You walk in through Nara Park at the start of a long day. Four things to look at:

1. The Great South Gate, before you reach the hall. Two guardian figures roughly 8.4 m tall stand inside it, carved by Unkei and Kaikei's workshop in 1203. They were assembled in about 69 days. ⇒ Look at the muscles and the twisting bodies. This is Kamakura-period sculpture reacting against the calm, front-facing figures you saw at Horyu-ji two days ago, and nowhere in Japan is that contrast clearer.

2. The hall is narrower than the original, and you can see it. The current Great Buddha Hall is a 1709 rebuild after the earlier halls burned in 1180 and 1567. Money was short, so it was rebuilt at about six-tenths of the original width (seven bays instead of eleven) but at the original height and depth. ⇒ That is why the building looks out of proportion: too tall and too deep for its front. Once you know this, you keep seeing how much broader and lower the original must have looked. The narrow front has recorded that budget shortfall for three centuries.

3. The octagonal lantern in front of the hall. It dates from the 8th century and is original, from the founding, which almost nothing else around it is. Its bronze panels show celestial musicians playing flutes. It stands in the open, and most people walk straight past it to photograph the hall behind.

4. The Buddha itself is pieced together from different centuries. The seated figure is 14.98 m high and was cast in 749, but only parts are original: the head is an Edo-period recasting and the hands are Momoyama-period. ⇒ You are looking at a Nara-period body with a 17th-century head, and the replaced parts mark the repairs after the two fires.

Official site, English edition (History; Daibutsuden) · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08) for the hall's dimensions and rebuild

Links
Official site (English) Official — the 8th century Wikipedia
Hokke-do (Sangatsu-do)法華堂（三月堂）
Hall of Todai-ji · Nara · c. 733 · older than the temple it belongs to
▶
What it is: the oldest building at Todai-ji

The oldest building at Todai-ji, dated to around 733, so it is older than the temple itself. It was a main hall of Konshusen-ji, the monastery that stood on this hillside before Todai-ji existed and was later absorbed into it.

⇒ Everything downhill from the Hokke-do (the Great Buddha, its hall, the national temple system) was added later, around it. ⚑ The Kegon sutra, the text the whole Todai-ji project was founded on, is said to have been expounded in Japan for the first time inside this building. The doctrine arrived in this room first, and the state built the statue afterwards.

Official site · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

What is inside — the densest room of National Treasures in Japan

The hall holds ten Nara-period statues, every one of them a National Treasure, crowded together in a small dark room. At the centre is the Fukukensaku Kannon, the Kannon "whose rope never fails to catch": the rope is cast like a net to save living beings. This statue gave the hall its older name, Kensaku-do (羂索堂, "Hall of the Rope"). The hall came to be called the Hokke-do (Lotus Hall) later, because the Lotus Sutra ceremony (Hokke-e) was held in it in the third month of every year.

Around the Kannon stand the two Kongo Rikishi guardians and the Four Heavenly Kings. One figure, the Shukongojin, is a hidden image unveiled on 16 December only, so you will not see it. The Nikko and Gakko figures that once flanked the Kannon have been moved to the Todai-ji Museum to protect them from earthquakes.

⇒ Here you can compare statues in their own hall with statues in a museum, and notice which you prefer. The statues in the Hokke-do stand in the building they were made for, at the spacing they were made for, in its light. Nikko and Gakko are now behind glass in the museum a few hundred metres away: safer and easier to see, but completely out of context.

Official site · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08) · Official site, English and Japanese Hokke-do pages (the older name Kensaku-do, the Hokke-e ceremony) · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it

The rear sanctuary is the original 8th-century structure. The worship hall in front was rebuilt in 1199 by the monk Chogen, so the Hokke-do is really two buildings joined together; look at the roofline from outside and you can see the join. ⚠ The hall closes at 16:00, ninety minutes before the Great Buddha Hall does on Saturday 31 October. Visit this upper part of the temple grounds before mid-afternoon instead of leaving it for last.

Official site · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

Links
Official site (English)
Nigatsu-do二月堂
Hall of Todai-ji · Nara · present building 1669 · home of a rite begun in 752
▶
What it is

A hall on the hillside above the Great Buddha, standing on a pillared platform that projects out over the slope and looks west across the whole Nara basin. There is no gate and no fee; the temple states it can be visited at any hour, day or night.

Official site, English edition · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

A hall built to house a ceremony

Its name means Second Month Hall, after the Shuni-e rite held here in the second month of the old calendar — now 1–14 March. The temple dates the rite's beginning to 752, the same year the Great Buddha was consecrated, and names its founder as Jitchu, chief disciple of Todai-ji's first abbot.

The rite is a repentance offered to the Eleven-headed Kannon, and the monks do not perform it for themselves. In the temple's words, it addresses the state's spiritual ills so as to "guarantee the welfare of the people." Eleven monks, the Rengyoshu, carry it out, with about thirty people involved in total.

⇒ Every year, eleven men spend a fortnight atoning on behalf of everyone in the country, whether or not anyone is watching. ⚑ It is widely said that the rite has never once been missed since 752. The temple's own pages do not make that claim, so treat it as widely repeated but not confirmed by the temple.

Official site, English edition — Shuni-e ceremony page · checked 2026-08-15

What happens in it

Two parts of the rite are famous. In the dattan, a great pine torch is swung about inside the inner sanctum: open fire, on purpose, indoors, in a wooden National Treasure. The other is the drawing of water. Sacred water is fetched after midnight on the twelfth day and offered to Kannon, and this gives the rite its popular name, Omizutori ("the water-drawing").

⚑ The hall you are standing on burned down in 1667, during the ceremony, and was rebuilt two years later. ⇒ The fire risk the rite runs did destroy the building once, and the rite carried on.

Official site, English edition — Shuni-e ceremony page · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08) for the 1667 fire

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Allow about twenty minutes; there is a terrace and a view, and nothing to tour inside. Two things to look at:

1. Take the stone stairway up, rather than the ramp, and turn round at the top. The platform faces west over the basin: the Great Buddha Hall's roof below, the city, and the hills beyond. It is the only high, open view in the whole park, and it costs nothing.

2. Look for scorch marks from the torches. The pine torches are carried along this terrace every March, so the building is still in working use for the rite and is not kept as a museum piece. ⚠ Tripods are not allowed on or around the terrace, and the temple asks for quiet at night.

Official site, English edition · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

Links
Official site (English) Official — the Shuni-e ceremony
Nara National Museum — the Shoso-in Exhibition奈良国立博物館・正倉院展
Museum · Nara Park · the 78th exhibition, 24 October – 9 November 2026
▶
What this actually is

Behind Todai-ji stands the Shoso-in, a raised log storehouse that was the temple's treasury and is now administered by the Imperial Household Agency. It holds roughly 9,000 objects from the 8th century.

The objects are there because of a death. When Emperor Shomu, the man who ordered the Great Buddha, died, his widow Empress Komyo dedicated his personal possessions to the Buddha he had built, on five separate occasions beginning 21 June 756.

⇒ Nothing in the collection was excavated, lost or rediscovered, so it is not an archaeological collection. These are one man's belongings, given away by his wife and kept in the same building ever since: an 8th-century household, held in one place without a break for nearly thirteen centuries. There is nothing else like it anywhere in the world.

Shoso-in Exhibition official site (about) · Todai-ji official site, English edition (the repository) · checked 2026-08-15

Why it is open now, and only now

The Shoso-in repository is not a museum and has no opening hours. Its imperial seal is ceremonially broken once a year, each autumn, so the treasures can be inventoried, and the exhibition shows a changing handful of them during those weeks.

⇒ You are here during the few weeks of the year when the storehouse is officially open, and the objects you see will go back inside and be sealed again. A different selection appears each year, so a given object may not be shown again for decades.

Shoso-in Exhibition official site (about) · checked 2026-08-15

The Silk Road claim, corrected

The collection is famous as a Silk Road treasury, and it does hold objects from Tang China, Unified Silla, Sassanid Persia and Southeast Asia. But the exhibition's own figure is worth knowing before you go in: about 95 percent of the objects were made in 8th-century Japan.

⇒ ⚑ The Japanese-made 95 percent is the more interesting part. It is the everyday objects people used in 8th-century Japan: furniture, weapons, medicine, musical instruments, masks, textiles and paperwork. The foreign pieces matter because of what surrounds them; they are the exceptions, and they show how far the trade reached. A room of only Persian glass would tell you nothing about Nara.

Shoso-in Exhibition official site (about) · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

⚠ Expect crowds: the exhibition is the museum's busiest fortnight of the year. Three things to look at:

1. Look at the ordinary objects as well as the spectacular ones. The queue forms around the famous pieces, such as an inlaid lute or a glass bowl. The documents, the medicine labels, the used brushes and the repair records are what make the collection unique, because no other 8th-century archive of ordinary things survives intact anywhere.

2. Finds from Isonokami Shrine, visited yesterday, are held in this museum. These are the objects excavated from Isonokami's forbidden precinct in 1874. ⚠ They are held here but may not be on display: the Shoso-in show occupies the special galleries, and the permanent collection rotates.

3. The Buddhist sculpture hall is closed. The museum's permanent hall of roughly a hundred statues from Asuka to Kamakura shut for renovation on 14 September 2026 and stays shut until about spring 2028, so the exhibition ticket no longer includes it. In its place, the small Bronze Ware Hall beside the new wing, free during the exhibition, shows a selection of the museum's own statues on its upper floor.

Shoso-in Exhibition official site · Nara National Museum notice of 2026-03-09 on the hall closure · checked 2026-09-04

Links
Exhibition official site Nara National Museum (English) Wikipedia — the Shoso-in
Kasuga Taisha春日大社
Shinto shrine · Nara · buildings from 768 · the Fujiwara clan shrine
▶
What it is

The shrine of the Fujiwara — the family that supplied the imperial house with wives and the government with ministers for centuries. Its buildings were raised on this site in 768, by the statesman Fujiwara-no-Nagate, as ordained by Empress Shotoku. Four sanctuaries are National Treasures and twenty-seven further buildings are Important Cultural Properties.

Official site, English edition · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

The gods were brought here from elsewhere

Kasuga's four deities did not originate in Nara. Takemikazuchi came from Kashima, in what is now Ibaraki — the far side of the country. Futsunushi came from Katori in Chiba. Amenokoyane and Himegami came from Hiraoka, in Osaka.

⇒ ⚑ Compare this with Omiwa, the shrine you saw yesterday on the Yamanobe-no-michi. At Omiwa the god is the mountain and always was; the shrine grew up where the deity already stood. At Kasuga, a political family moving to a new capital brought its gods with it from four directions and installed them at the edge of the new city. Omiwa is a place that had a god; Kasuga is a god that was given a place. Both are Shinto shrines, forty minutes apart. The difference lies in who did the founding: at Omiwa the landscape itself, at Kasuga a clan with its own interests.

Official site, English edition (enshrined deities and their origins) · checked 2026-08-15

A shrine that is rebuilt rather than preserved

The sanctuaries are taken down and rebuilt to the same design roughly every twenty years — the shikinen zotai — and this has now been done sixty times.

⇒ Kasuga and Todai-ji, two hundred metres downhill, have different ideas of what a building is. Todai-ji and Horyu-ji are valued for their old timber, which is also how the West thinks about heritage. Kasuga's sanctuaries are valued for being correct, and the wood is replaced to keep them so. What carries on is the form and the craft, while the wood is new. Neither approach is a lesser version of the other; they are two answers to the same question, and you can see both in one afternoon.

Official site, English edition · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08) for the sixty rebuildings

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

You walk to the shrine through the forest, with deer roaming loose the whole way. Three things to look at:

1. The lanterns, every one of them a donation. There are about three thousand: stone lanterns along the approach, bronze ones hung along the corridors. Each was given by somebody, and the older ones carry the donor's name and date. ⚑ The shrine says it is believed that more than six in ten of all surviving lanterns from the Muromachi period or earlier are here. Read a few inscriptions instead of only photographing the row: together they are a fifteenth-century list of donors that you can walk through.

2. The dark room. Inside the corridors is the Fujinami-no-ya (藤浪之屋), an Important Cultural Property that was the priests' duty room until the Edo period. The shrine now keeps it dark, with dozens of bronze hanging lanterns lit inside, to recreate the lantern festival. ⚠ The room lies inside the inner corridor, on the route of the shrine's special worship (tokubetsu sanpai) through the inner corridor.

⇒ The festival the room recreates is the Mantoro (万燈籠), when every lantern at the shrine is lit. It is held at two festivals, on three evenings a year: Setsubun in February, and 14 and 15 August. Until the end of the Edo period all the lanterns were lit every night, and the Tokugawa shogunate paid for the oil. Outside these festivals the lanterns are normally unlit, so this room is the only chance to see lanterns burning, which is what all three thousand were made for.

3. The forest behind the shrine, which gives it its setting. Hunting and logging on Mount Mikasa were prohibited from the 9th century because the mountain is sacred to the shrine. The result is a primeval forest inside a city, inscribed by UNESCO along with the buildings. ⇒ The trees were protected for religious reasons, a thousand years before nature conservation existed as an idea.

Official site, English edition (deities, lanterns, Mount Mikasa, the primeval forest) · Official site, precinct guide (Fujinami-no-ya, the special-worship route, the Mantoro on Setsubun and 14 and 15 August, the six-in-ten Muromachi share) · annual events calendar (節分万燈籠, 中元万燈籠) · Japan Tourism Agency multilingual explanation database, "Fujinami no Ya" (nightly lighting until the end of the Edo period, paid for by the shogunate) · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (English) Official site (Japanese) Wikipedia
Naramachiならまち
Historic quarter · Nara · the merchant town built on a temple's grounds
▶
What it is

A district of lanes and wooden townhouses with lattice fronts, south of Sarusawa Pond, developed as a commercial town from the Edo period through the Meiji. The quarter is public streets, so there is no gate, no opening hours and no ticket; each shop keeps its own hours.

Nara City tourism association · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

What it is standing on

Naramachi grew inside the former grounds of Gango-ji, one of the seven great temples of the Nara capital. Gango-ji was itself the first proper Buddhist temple ever built in Japan, moved here from Asuka when the capital moved.

⇒ The streets stand on land that a shrinking temple gave up. As Gango-ji lost patronage and land, merchants built on that ground, so the lanes you walk fill the space the monastery once occupied. ⚑ Gango-ji's own website shows an estimated plan of the ancient temple, and the Nara City tourism association publishes a reconstructed map of Naramachi in the Nara period, when the temple's grounds were at their largest.

Nara City tourism association · checked 2026-08-15 · Gango-ji official site, "Gango-ji and Naramachi" (the estimated plan) · Nara City tourism association, reconstructed old map page · checked 2026-10-17

Gango-ji itself — the temple that came from Asuka

Gango-ji began as Hoko-ji, also called Asuka-dera, which the temple calls Japan's first full-scale temple compound (garan). It was the Soga clan's own temple in Asuka. When the capital moved to Heijo-kyo (Nara), it was rebuilt here on a new site, became a state temple, and took the name Gango-ji.

Most of that great temple is gone. The present temple grounds keep part of its monks' quarters and lecture hall. Its two World Heritage buildings, the Gokuraku-do and the Zenshitsu, are both National Treasures, and both were made from the old monks' living quarters. Most of the old temple lies buried under the streets and houses of Naramachi.

⚑ Look at the roofs. On the north and west slopes of the Gokuraku-do, and the east part of the Zenshitsu's south slope, the round and flat tiles overlap each other, unlike an ordinary tiled roof. The temple says this is an old tile style handed down from the Asuka period and the founding of Hoko-ji. When the buildings were taken apart for repair, it was found that old tiles still fit for use had been gathered and reused on them. The Nara City tourism association states that tiles from the Asuka period survive on the Gokuraku-do roof. ⇒ Some of the tiles on these roofs are older than the city of Nara.

Gango-ji official site (Japanese): top page · About · building descriptions ("元興寺の瓦", Gokuraku-do, Zenshitsu) · temple grounds page · English edition · Nara City tourism association, Naramachi feature (Asuka-period tiles) · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Allow an hour to an hour and a half at the end of the day, including dinner. Three things to look at:

1. The red monkeys. Small red cloth figures hang from the eaves all over the quarter. They are migawari-zaru, "substitute monkeys", which take on misfortune meant for the household. One is hung for each family member, with a wish written on the back. ⇒ Look up as you walk: the number hanging outside a house tells you how many people they were hung for.

2. Narrow fronts, deep buildings. These townhouses are machiya, and their shape comes from being taxed on the width of the street frontage. So a house is one or two rooms wide and very deep, with courtyards set into it for light. ⇒ Step into any shop in a machiya and walk to the back to feel how deep it goes.

3. The Mochiidono arcade runs into the quarter. Its own website calls it the shopping street "said to be the oldest in Nara"; that is the arcade's own claim, and it covers Nara only. ⚑ The arcade is also the place to eat the Nara specialities: persimmon-leaf sushi, tea gruel and Yamato vegetables.

Nara City tourism association (migawari-zaru, machiya, Mochiidono) · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

Links
Nara City tourism (Japanese) Wikipedia — Gango-ji

Sunday 1 November — Kurama to Kibune, and Sanjusangen-do

North of Kyoto · a mountain temple that left its school, a walk over the ridge, the head shrine of water — then 1,001 standing Kannon in the city
Kurama-dera鞍馬寺
Mountain temple · north of Kyoto · founded 770 · head temple of its own school since 1949
▶
What it is

A temple built up the side of a mountain above Kurama village. You enter through a gate at the bottom and climb on foot. It is the head temple of Kurama-kokyo, a school the temple founded itself in the 1940s.

Official site (Japanese): history and teaching pages · checked 2026-08-15

Founded by a student of the man whose temple you saw on Thursday 29 October

The temple's own history begins with a link to a temple you have already visited. Gantei, who founded Kurama-dera in 770, is described as "a high disciple of the venerable Ganjin of Toshodai-ji in Nara". Ganjin was the blind Chinese monk who spent twelve years and five failed crossings getting to Japan, and you walked through his temple, Toshodai-ji, on 29 October.

In the founding story, Gantei climbed the mountain "guided by a dream revelation and a white horse" on the fourth night of the first month. In 796 almost the same thing happened again. Fujiwara no Isendo, the official in charge of building To-ji, came up the same mountain with the same supernatural guidance, intending to enshrine Kannon. A second dream told him "Bishamonten and Kannon are at root one and the same", so both were installed.

⇒ ⚑ The statement that Bishamonten and Kannon are one and the same is the temple's founding doctrine, and the teaching described below follows from it. Two deities that other traditions keep separate were declared here, in the 8th century, to be aspects of one thing.

Official site (Japanese), history page — quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

What is actually worshipped here, and it is not a buddha

The object of devotion is Sonten, which the temple defines in its own words as "the universal life and cosmic energy that produces all existence in this world, beginning with humanity." Sonten has three aspects, and the temple sets them out as a table:

  • Love — the spirit of the moon — Senju Kannon
  • Light — the spirit of the sun — Bishamonten
  • Power — the spirit-king of the earth — Goho Mao-son

The temple's summary phrase, written up around the grounds, is: "All things exist in Sonten."

⇒ This is closer to a single account of the universe than to a set of gods. The temple presents the three named figures as three faces of one energy. ⚑ The third, Goho Mao-son, is the strangest: the temple's tradition holds that this being came to this mountain from Venus. That belief is the reason Kurama has a reputation for the occult, and the temple's own doctrine does not entirely disown that reputation.

Official site (Japanese), teaching page — quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

A temple that resigned from its school — and it was not the only one

For most of its history Kurama-dera was a Tendai temple. Then, in 1947, its first modern abbot gathered its accumulated traditions under a new name — Kurama-kokyo — and in 1949 the temple became the head temple of that school.

⇒ 🔴 Compare Kurama-dera's 1949 break with Horyu-ji, which you visited on Thursday 29 October. Horyu-ji left the Hosso school in 1950 to found a school centred on Prince Shotoku. Kurama-dera left Tendai in 1949. Two of the oldest temples in the country left their parent schools within twelve months of each other, and the timing has a cause. The postwar settlement had just dismantled state control of religion, so the head-temple relationships that the old system had fixed in place had suddenly become optional. ⚑ The dates come from each temple's own pages; the explanation is general history and is marked as such.

Official site (Japanese), history page (1947 and 1949, quoted and translated) · Horyu-ji official site for 1950 · the postwar context is general history, not a temple claim

The boy who trained with the tengu

The temple's history states it plainly: Minamoto no Yoshitsune — as a child, Ushiwakamaru — entered Kurama-dera at about seven years old. He "studied Buddhism by day" at a hall above Yuki Shrine, and "at night, at Sojo-ga-tani, was taught the art of war by the tengu."

⇒ Yoshitsune became the most celebrated battlefield commander in Japanese history, and was then hunted to death by his own brother. The story that he learned to fight from mountain demons on this hillside is the founding text of half of Japanese heroic literature. Sojo-ga-tani is a real place with a signpost, on the path you will walk over the ridge.

Official site (Japanese), history page — quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

You see this temple by climbing it: the temple charges a mountain-entry fee at the gate, and the walk up is the visit. Four things to look for:

1. The cable railway is run by the temple. The temple states that it is the only railway in Japan licensed under the Railway Business Act that is operated by a religious body. A religious body running a licensed railway is a real oddity. ⚠ The cable car's top station is above Yuki Shrine, so riding it skips the switchback approach and the shrine on it. Walking up is the harder choice here, and also the better one.

2. The stone mandala in the courtyard outside the main hall. A geometric pattern is set into the pavement in front of the Hondo Kondo, with a triangle at its centre where people stand. The temple holds that the mountain's energy is concentrated at that point. ⇒ Whatever you make of that, you will see a queue of people taking turns to stand on a paving stone, and it is worth knowing why they do it.

3. The treasure hall, two minutes above the main hall, and go early. It has three floors. The third floor is the room for Buddhist statues. It holds a National Treasure Bishamonten triad, a standing Sho-Kannon and a Tobatsu Bishamonten (both Important Cultural Properties), and a row of other standing Bishamonten. The temple suggests you sit on the tatami in that quiet room and take your time with them. The second floor shows the temple's paintings, calligraphy, crafts and archaeological finds. It also has a room for the poets Yosano Tekkan and Yosano Akiko, who taught poetry to a former abbot and often came to Kurama. Items given to the temple include Akiko's manuscript of her modern-Japanese translation of The Tale of Genji, and her study, Tohakutei, has been moved to the garden in front of the hall. The first floor is a natural-history room for the mountain. A fern, a moth, a land snail and two mushrooms were first found in Japan here or carry the name Kurama. ⚠ The treasure hall keeps shorter hours than the temple and closes on Tuesdays, so do not leave it until late in the day.

4. The roots. On the path over the ridge, at the Kinone-michi, the cedar roots run across the surface of the path rather than under it. The temple explains that the sandstone here was hardened when molten rock pushed up into it, so the roots cannot grow down and spread over the ground you walk on instead. ⇒ It is the most photographed stretch of the walk, and the reason the temple asks you not to go on in the wet or the dark.

Official site (Japanese): precinct, treasure hall and route pages · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08) · the cable-railway wording is the temple's own, 山内案内 §(6): 「鉄道事業法による許可を受けた鉄道としては唯一、宗教法人が運営しているケーブル」 · checked 2026-08-22 · treasure-hall floors: the temple's treasure-hall page (霊宝殿) · the roots: 山内案内 §(36) · both checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (Japanese) Official — the teaching Wikipedia
Yuki Shrine由岐神社
Shinto shrine · on the Kurama-dera approach · moved here 940
▶
What it is, and why it is on this mountain

The guardian (tutelary) shrine of Kurama village. It stands on the switchback path a few minutes above Kurama-dera's gate, so you pass through it on the way up.

The shrine did not start here. It was moved out of the capital to this mountain in 940, by imperial order. ⇒ ⚑ The reason was defence. Kurama is due north of Kyoto, and the north was believed to be the direction from which misfortune entered a city. Putting a shrine on this slope was a deliberate act of spiritual protection for the capital below, the same thinking that put Enryaku-ji on Mount Hiei, to the north-east.

Official site (Japanese) · Kyoto City official tourism register · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

The festival is the move, re-enacted every year

The Kurama Fire Festival, one of the three great fire festivals of Japan, is this shrine's annual rite, held on the night of 22 October. Villagers carry huge burning pine torches up the same approach you are walking.

⇒ The festival commemorates the 940 procession itself, the night the deity was brought up from the city by torchlight. It re-enacts that arrival on the same ground once a year, and has done so for eleven centuries. ⚑ On 1 November you miss it by ten days.

Official site (Japanese) · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free, with no gate of its own; allow ten to fifteen minutes as you pass. Three things to look for:

1. Walk through the worship hall itself. The hall was rebuilt in 1607 by Toyotomi Hideyori and is an Important Cultural Property. It is a wari-haiden, literally a "split worship hall": two halves with the path running through the middle of the building. The path goes straight through the hall instead of stopping at it or going round it. There are very few halls like this.

2. The lion-dogs. The guardian pair are an Important Cultural Property, and the shrine's own site calls them the only ones in Japan shown holding a child. ⚠ Several independent accounts say the designated pair is on deposit at the Kyoto National Museum, and that the pair beside the hall is a faithful copy made in 1966. Neither the shrine's own site nor Kyoto City's register (which confirms only the designation) says this, so treat the deposit as unconfirmed. Either way, look at what is under the paws before you walk on.

3. The great cedar, the shrine's sacred tree. The shrine gives it as about 800 years old and about 53 m tall, and it is a designated Kyoto City Natural Monument. People have long called it Osugi-san, a familiar name meaning "the great cedar", and the shrine says that a wish made to it wholeheartedly comes true. ⚠ The shrine calls the whole mountain the place where the boy Yoshitsune trained, but it tells no story that links him to this tree.

Official site (Japanese) · Kyoto City official tourism register · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08) · the cedar's age, height, designation and the wish: the shrine's About page (由岐神社とは), checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (Japanese)
Kurama to Kibune Trail鞍馬山〜貴船
Mountain path · about 3.9 km · 60–90 minutes · the temple's own road, not a public trail
▶
🔴 It is not a hiking trail. It is part of the temple

The crossing from Kurama to Kibune is the temple's own route over the ridge, and the temple has a name for it: the Shugyō-no-michi, the road of ascetic practice. It begins behind the main hall you have just climbed to and ends at the Nishi-mon gate at Kibune, and every step of it is inside Kurama-dera's precinct.

⇒ ⚑ Because the whole path is inside the temple precinct, the walk is lined with halls and shrines instead of trail markers. The path is itself part of the temple, and it ends by letting you out through a gate on the far side.

Kurama-dera official site (Japanese), the precinct and access pages · route recorded in the planning file, checked 2026-08-08

The route, in the order you meet it

From the main hall, going over and down. The mountain path begins at the small gate beyond the treasure hall.

  • Ikitsugi-no-mizu (息つぎの水), "the water for catching your breath". A spring. The temple says the boy Ushiwakamaru (the young Yoshitsune) hurried alone along this path at midnight to his lessons with the tengu, and stopped here to drink.
  • Byobu-zaka Jizō-dō (屏風坂の地蔵堂), a small hall to Jizō on the slope.
  • Sekurabe-ishi (背比べ石), "the height-measuring stone". When Ushiwakamaru learned that the Taira were his father's enemies, he left Kurama to seek the protection of Fujiwara no Hidehira at Hiraizumi, in the far north. Tradition says that before he went, sad to leave, he measured his height against this stone.
  • Kinone-michi (木の根道), the stretch where the cedar roots lace the surface of the path. The temple explains that the sandstone here was hardened when molten rock pushed up into it, so the roots cannot grow down. Ushiwakamaru is also said to have trained on this stretch.
  • Ōsugi Gongen (大杉権現社), a shrine to the great cedar. The temple calls the area around it a meditation ground, known as a place where the energy of Gohō Maō-son is strong. ⚑ It stands on a short side path. The path past it rejoins the main path at the Fudō-dō.
  • Fudō-dō (不動堂). It holds a figure of Fudō Myōō that the temple says Saichō carved himself, bowing three times at every cut, while he was praying to found the Tendai school.
  • Yoshitsune-dō (義経堂). Yoshitsune died a violent death in the north, and his spirit is said to have come back to Kurama. He is worshipped here as Shanaō-son. 🔴 The temple says this spot is Sōjō-ga-tani (僧正ガ谷), the place where Ushiwakamaru learned the art of war from the tengu. So you reach Sōjō-ga-tani after Ōsugi Gongen and the Fudō-dō, a little before the Maō-den.
  • Maō-den (魔王殿), the inner sanctuary. Since ancient times the rocks here have been worshipped as the seat where Gohō Maō-son came down to earth.
  • then the descent to the Nishi-mon gate, and Kibune

Kurama-dera official site (Japanese), precinct guide (山内案内) §(32)–(40), quoted and translated · checked 2026-10-17

The numbers, and they are for the crossing as a whole

About 3.9 km · roughly 60 to 90 minutes · about 370 m of descent on the Kibune side.

⚠ The 60 to 90 minutes covers the whole crossing. The day plan shows the walk as two parts, over the ridge and then down into Kibune, but the time is not divided between them.

Planning record, from the temple's own route description · checked 2026-08-08

🔴 Roots and stone underfoot: walk it dry and in daylight

It is a forest path of roots and stone, and the temple's own guidance is not to push on after rain or in failing light.

⇒ ⚑ So walk it from the Kurama side in the morning, in dry weather, and come down into Kibune in the afternoon with plenty of daylight left. Walking it the other way round, late in the day, would put the root path under your feet as the light goes, which is what the temple advises against.

Kurama-dera official site (Japanese) · checked 2026-08-08

Closure notices you may see do not cover this path

The whole route was verified open on 30 July 2026. ⚠ The closure notices that circulate for this mountain are for the 582 m summit ridge beyond the temple's route, a different path that this walk does not use.

The temple posts path closures on its own front page, so check that page once more close to the trip. A three-day closure of the lower switchback approach, for hazardous trees, ran 5–7 August 2026 and did not affect the route over the ridge.

Kurama-dera official site (Japanese), front-page notices · checked 2026-08-08

Links
Official site (Japanese) Precinct guide Entry and mountain-access page Access
Kifune Shrine貴船神社
Shinto shrine · Kibune ravine · head shrine of some 2,000 water shrines
▶
What it is

In its own words, "the head shrine of approximately 2,000 water shrines nationwide", enshrining "the water deity, the source of the life of all things." It stands in a cedar ravine at the head of the stream that feeds Kyoto's water. Building work is recorded here from around 686.

Official site (Japanese and English editions) · checked 2026-08-15

Why a shrine to water is a shrine to the state

The founding legend, from the shrine's own English page: about 1,600 years ago Tamayori-hime, mother of the first emperor, declared (quoted directly) "I am Tamayori-hime. E'er I control the rain and the winds to make the land prosper and the soil fertile." She travelled by boat up the rivers and founded the shrine at the place the water came from.

⇒ In a rice economy, rain decided the harvest, and the harvest was the tax base. So emperors petitioned this shrine directly, and horses were offered here in droughts and in floods: a black horse to call rain, a white one to stop it. ⚑ That practice is widely held to be the origin of the ema, the small painted wooden tablet hung at every shrine in Japan. An ema was a picture of a horse, offered when a live horse was not affordable. ⚠ The horse offerings are on the shrine's own pages. The ema origin is a widely repeated claim that the shrine's English page does not itself make, and it is marked here as such.

Official site, English edition (the founding quotation, the imperial petitions, the horses) · the ema derivation is a common attribution, not sourced from the shrine

The other reputation, which is real

Kifune is also, historically, a site of ushi-no-koku-mairi: a shrine visit at the hour of the ox, 2 a.m., to nail a straw effigy to a sacred tree. The Noh play Kanawa is set in this ravine; in it, a woman comes here to ask to be turned into a demon.

⇒ ⚑ The curses follow from the same belief as the rain petitions. People bring their worst requests to a shrine they believe answers requests with real force. The romantic Kibune of summer dining platforms and the Kibune of the 2 a.m. nail are the same place, and knowing about the second makes the ravine feel very different in the dark under the cedars.

Reference works and the Noh repertoire · ⚠ not a claim the shrine makes about itself

The middle shrine, and the goddess who stayed to make matches

The middle one of Kifune's three shrines is Yui-no-yashiro (結社). The shrine describes it as famous for matchmaking. Its goddess is Iwanaga-hime, elder sister of Konohana-sakuya-hime.

The shrine tells her story this way. Ninigi, great-grandfather of Emperor Jimmu, asked to marry Konohana-sakuya-hime. Her father offered him Iwanaga-hime as well, but Ninigi wanted only the younger sister. Iwanaga-hime was deeply ashamed, and settled here saying: "I will stay here and give people good matches."

⚑ The shrine adds a second story. In the Heian period the poet Izumi Shikibu came here, unhappy because her husband's feelings had changed, and offered a poem with her prayer. Her wish was granted, and from then on Kifune was called "the shrine of love". One of the shrine's votive tablets shows Izumi Shikibu.

Official site (Japanese), shrine guide (境内案内) and prayer pages, quoted and translated · English edition for the name Yui no Yashiro · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free, open every day. Kifune Shrine is three shrines along the ravine, and the order you visit them in matters. Coming down off the mountain you meet them in reverse: Okunomiya (the inner shrine) first, then the middle shrine, then the main shrine. That is the opposite of the traditional order, and it saves walking back the way you came. Three things to look for:

1. Float a fortune on the spring at the main shrine. Water wells out of the stone wall in the precinct, and the paper fortunes sold here are blank. The writing appears when the paper touches the water. ⇒ It is a novelty, and it also fits the shrine's belief: at a shrine whose deity is water, the water reveals the message.

2. The stone boat at the innermost shrine. Beside the Okunomiya hall lies a boat-shaped mound of stones. It is said to cover the boat Tamayori-hime arrived in, in the founding legend.

3. What is under the innermost hall. The hall is built over a dragon hole, counted among the three great ones in Japan. Nobody is permitted to look into it, and the hall exists to cover it. ⇒ ⚑ A shrine hall is usually thought of as a house for a god; this one is also a lid.

Official site (Japanese and English editions) · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08) for the three-shrine round and the stone boat

Links
Official site (English) Official site (Japanese) Wikipedia
Sanjusangen-do (Rengeo-in)蓮華王院 三十三間堂
Buddhist hall · Kyoto · founded 1164 · present building 1266 · 1,001 Kannon
▶
What it is

A wooden hall 120 m long, containing 1,001 statues of Kannon: a thousand life-size standing figures ranked in ten tiers, with one large seated Kannon at the centre. The building and everything in it are National Treasures.

Its name is an architectural measurement. Sanjusan-gen means thirty-three bays, the spaces between the pillars of the inner sanctuary. Thirty-three is the number of forms Kannon is said to take in order to reach different people. ⇒ The hall's length was set by that number: it was built to have thirty-three bays, the number of Kannon's forms.

Kyoto City official tourism register · English Wikipedia · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

Who built it, and what they wanted from it

It was founded in 1164 at the wish of the retired emperor Go-Shirakawa and paid for by Taira no Kiyomori — the warlord who was in the process of taking over the country.

⇒ ⚑ The pairing sums up the politics of the age. Go-Shirakawa was an emperor who had abdicated so that he could hold power without the ceremonial duties of the throne. Kiyomori was the first warrior to run the state outright. A retired emperor with authority but no money and a soldier with money but no legitimacy together built the largest devotional object in the country. Within twenty years the Taira had been wiped out by Minamoto no Yoshitsune, the boy who trained at Kurama-dera, where you were this morning.

Kyoto City official tourism register · English Wikipedia · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

The fire, and what was carried out of it

The hall burned in 1249. Of the thousand standing Kannon, 124 survived — carried out of a burning building — and the remaining 876 were commissioned by the retired emperor Go-Saga and made by three rival sculptural workshops over sixteen years. The present building was completed in 1266.

⇒ So the ranks in front of you are two sets of statues: 12th-century survivors standing among 13th-century replacements. The replacements were a national, industrial-scale effort with a deadline. ⚑ The central seated Kannon was carved by Tankei, son of Unkei. Unkei's workshop made the guardians at Todai-ji's Great South Gate, which you walked under on 31 October. You see the father's workshop at Nara and the son's work here.

English Wikipedia (the 124 survivals, Go-Saga's 876, the three workshops, sixteen years) · reference works for Tankei's parentage

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

One long room, which you walk from one end to the other. It is dim, and photography is not allowed inside. Four things to look for:

1. Look for the older statues among the newer. 124 of the thousand predate the 1249 fire. The 12th-century figures differ subtly from the 13th-century ones beside them, in the face and in the fall of the drapery. ⇒ You are looking for survivors in a crowd: nobody sorted them afterwards, so they still stand mixed in among the replacements.

2. The twenty-eight attendants at the front, and the two at the ends. The guardians lined up along the front are National Treasures with Hindu origins: deities taken into Buddhism on its way east, who arrived here with their names translated. At the two ends stand Fujin and Raijin, the gods of wind and thunder. ⇒ These are the most individual faces in the building: unidealised old men with visible tendons.

3. It is said that no two of the thousand faces are the same. Test it on a few instead of taking it on trust. The tradition is that a visitor can find the face of someone they have lost among them, which is why people study the faces so carefully.

4. Go outside to the west veranda. For 255 years in the Edo period it was the site of the toshiya: archers shot the full 120 m length of the building under the eaves, competing on how many arrows they could land in twenty-four hours. ⇒ Stand at one end and look down it. The record holders shot for a full day and night, and the roof beams still carry arrow damage.

English Wikipedia (the 28 attendants and their origins, Fujin and Raijin, the toshiya's 255 years) · Kyoto City official tourism register · the trip's own verified record (2026-08-08)

Links
Kyoto City tourism register Wikipedia

Monday 2 November — the Kamo shrines and Daitoku-ji

Kyoto, south to north then west · one family's two shrines, the street its priests lived in, and the Zen temple the warlords bought into · the day is held together by a single stream
Tadasu-no-Mori糺の森
Forest · Kyoto · the wooded approach to Shimogamo Shrine
▶
What it is

A broadleaf forest in the middle of Kyoto, a city of 1.4 million, on the point of land where two rivers meet. You walk its full length to reach Shimogamo Shrine. The foundation that conserves it describes it as a forest that has lived on since the Jomon period. It counts about six hundred trees between two and six hundred years old: zelkova, hackberry and Aphananthe, the species that made up the ancient woodland of this plain.

Tadasu-no-Mori Foundation (Japanese) · checked 2026-08-15

How much of it is gone

The foundation gives two numbers in the same sentence. The forest today covers about 36,000 tsubo. By tradition it once covered 1,500,000.

⇒ The forest you walk through is roughly one fortieth of its former size. It feels like a large city park. It is what is left of a much bigger forest.

⚠ Be careful with the word "primeval": the foundation itself avoids calling the forest that. What it says is that "the same vegetation as the primeval forest of around the 3rd century BCE is transmitted to the present". That means the same mix of species grows here today; the trees themselves are not untouched survivors. ⚑ The foundation draws this distinction itself, and it is worth keeping in mind.

Tadasu-no-Mori Foundation (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

The stream you walk beside is the one in the myth

Two small streams run through the forest. One of them, the Semi-no-ogawa, appears in the 8th-century Fudoki account of the shrine's origin, in the passage that explains how the stream got its name: "Though narrow, it is a clear river of Ishikawa," he said. Therefore it is called the Semi-no-ogawa of Ishikawa.

⇒ In the myth, this is the stream where a vermilion arrow came floating down to Tamayorihime. That event led to the birth of the god enshrined at Kamigamo Shrine, which you visit this afternoon. ⚑ The origin myth of both Kamo shrines is set in a real place, and it is a stream you can put your hand in on your morning walk through the forest.

Shimogamo Shrine's own mythology page, quoting the Yamashiro Fudoki (Japanese), translated · Tadasu-no-Mori Foundation for the stream names

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free, unfenced, and open at all hours. The forest is the approach path to the shrine, so walking it is no detour. Three things to look at:

1. People lived here in the Yayoi period. Digs in and around the forest have turned up ancient pottery and Yayoi-period dwelling remains. ⇒ People were living here before there was a shrine, a capital, or a written language in Japan.

2. Learn to recognise three trees. Zelkova, hackberry and Aphananthe are the main species in the forest. They show what the Yamashiro plain looked like before it was farmed.

3. The reconstructed hut is in the forest. A scholarly reconstruction of Kamo no Chomei's ten-foot-square hut, the dwelling he describes in the Hojoki, stands in the paid viewing area on the former temple site inside the forest. ⚠ Nearly every guidebook still places the hut at Kawai Shrine. Those guidebooks are out of date.

Shimogamo Shrine official site (the excavations, the hut's actual location) · Tadasu-no-Mori Foundation · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Tadasu-no-Mori Foundation (Japanese) Wikipedia — Shimogamo
Kawai Shrine河合神社
Auxiliary shrine of Shimogamo · in Tadasu-no-Mori · where Kamo no Chomei grew up
▶
What it is

A small shrine at the southern end of the forest. Its own signs call it "the foremost beauty-deity in Japan." It enshrines Tamayorihime, and the shrine gives the reason plainly: because she was beautiful as a jewel, she is revered as the deity of beauty.

⚠ Its founding date is unknown. The shrine publishes no year, no founder and no circumstance. It says only that it has been worshipped as an auxiliary shrine "from of old." Guidebooks that give a founding date have supplied one themselves.

Shimogamo Shrine official site (Japanese) — the only published account · checked 2026-08-15

The essayist grew up on this spot

Kamo no Chomei, author of the Hojoki, spent his childhood here as the son of this shrine's senior priest. The Hojoki is counted as one of the three great essay collections in Japanese literature.

⇒ ⚑ His childhood here is the reason to stop at this shrine. The Hojoki opens with the flow of a river and the impermanence of everything people build. Its author ended his life in a ten-foot hut that he could take apart and carry away, after he failed to inherit the priesthood of this shrine. His withdrawal from the world followed a specific disappointment, and that disappointment happened here.

⚠ The reconstruction of his hut is NOT at this shrine. It stands in the paid area further into the forest, so do not look for it in this precinct.

Shimogamo Shrine official site (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

⚠ One thing the shrine's own pages disagree about

Kawai Shrine's page calls Tamayorihime the mother of Emperor Jinmu. The history page of Shimogamo, its parent shrine, calls her the daughter of Kamo Taketsunumi and the mother of the god enshrined at Kamigamo. These are two different figures with the same name, from two different traditions, merged into one deity.

⇒ ⚑ Both statements are on the same official website. The shrine has left the two traditions side by side without reconciling them. Noticing the contradiction tells you more about how these family trees of the gods were put together than a single tidy version would.

Shimogamo Shrine official site (Japanese) — both pages, read 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free. ⚠ It closes half an hour earlier than the main shrine. Your route today runs south to north, so you reach Kawai first, in the morning, and its earlier closing does not affect you.

The mirror ema. The votive tablets here are shaped like a hand-mirror with a face printed on them. You are meant to make up the face with your own cosmetics, then write your wish on the back. The stated idea is that you become beautiful by polishing the inside as well as the outside. ⇒ The rack holds hundreds of them, each made up by a different person with whatever was in their bag. No other shrine on this trip has votive tablets so personal to the people who left them.

Shimogamo Shrine official site (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official page (Japanese) Wikipedia — Kamo no Chomei
Shimogamo Shrine賀茂御祖神社（下鴨神社）
Shinto shrine · Kyoto · founding date unknown · World Heritage
▶
What it is

The southern of the two Kamo shrines, at the confluence of the Kamo and Takano rivers. Its formal name means the shrine of the Kamo ancestors, and that is literal: it enshrines Kamo Taketsunumi in the west hall and Tamayorihime in the east — the grandfather and the mother of the god enshrined at Kamigamo, which you reach this afternoon.

Official site (Japanese) · checked 2026-08-15 · ⚠ no English edition: the site's own "English" link leads to a page that was never filled

The shrine says it does not know when it was founded — and its reasoning is better than a date

Its own history page states: "The exact founding of this shrine is unknown, but because there is a record that the sacred fence was repaired in the 7th year of Emperor Sujin, it is thought to have been worshipped from an older age than that."

⇒ ⚑ The shrine dates itself from a repair. You cannot repair a fence that was never built, so a record of a repair proves the shrine already existed before that record. How much earlier is unknown. Most old shrines simply claim to be ancient. This one declines to give a founding year and instead publishes the reasoning by which its age is worked out, which is much rarer.

The earliest firm written evidence of its size is also indirect. An entry in the 8th-century national chronicle records that in 698 an order was issued to police the Kamo Festival because so many spectators were gathering. ⇒ A crowd-control order is the first firm evidence that the shrine was already a major institution.

Official site (Japanese), history page, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

How a shrine changes who pays for it

Most shrines say nothing about where their money came from. Shimogamo does: "over sixty estates throughout the country were donated and supported the shrine. As the Kamakura and Muromachi periods and then the age of wars came on, contact with the estates gradually broke off; in their place the faith of the common people came to support the shrine."

⇒ Those two sentences, written by the shrine itself, sum up the social history of Japanese religious institutions. The estates granted by the aristocracy were lost as the aristocracy lost power, and the shrine survived by turning itself into something ordinary people paid for. Every amulet counter at a Japanese shrine today is a result of that change.

Official site (Japanese), history page, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

The rebuilding cycle — and the shrine's admission that it no longer means what it meant

Shimogamo rebuilds its halls on a fixed cycle, counted from a first rebuilding in 1036. The shrine notes drily that "owing to wars, famines and disasters it sometimes stretched to thirty or fifty years." The interval is now 21 years, for a practical reason: cypress bark and plain wood stay sound for a little over twenty years. The cycle is also how the building crafts are passed on.

⇒ 🔴 The shrine then admits that the cycle has changed: "it is not possible to renew everything. The original rebuilding was a transfer renewing all buildings and furnishings except the sacred object, but the present basic policy is repair of damaged parts."

⚑ Protecting the buildings as cultural property changed the ritual. Two of the halls are National Treasures and fifty-three more buildings are Important Cultural Properties. You cannot demolish a National Treasure every twenty-one years, even if the rite requires it.
