The law that protects the buildings now overrides the practice that produced them, and the shrine says so in public. ⇒ Compare Kasuga Taisha in Nara, which you visited on Sat 31 Oct: it has carried out its rebuilding sixty times.

Official site (Japanese), shikinen sengu page, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The precinct is free. A separate paid area holds the old kitchen and the reconstructed hut. Four things to look at:

1. The seven zodiac shrines beside the main hall: find the one for your birth year. They look like a single row of small shrines, but there are seven, in three groups, each covering particular birth years of the twelve-animal cycle. ⇒ You are meant to find yours and pray at that one. Most visitors photograph the row and pray at whichever is nearest.

2. The pond behind a famous sweet. Water wells up around the Mitarashi pond, which is counted among the seven wonders of Kamo. ⚑ Dumplings were shaped to look like its rising bubbles, and this is held to be the origin of mitarashi-dango, a sweet now sold in every station in Japan.

3. The joined tree, on the left of the small shrine near the entrance. Two trunks grown into one, with a young tree sprouting at the root. It is counted among the seven wonders of Kyoto. The present tree is the fourth generation, and each new tree is said to have sprung up somewhere in the Tadasu-no-Mori forest you have just walked through.

4. The shrine of seals, right beside the main hall. A small sanctuary for seals, the carved stamps used to sign documents. The shrine's explanation: seals were treated as precious at court and in temples, and "this was gradually deified." ⇒ An office tool has become an object of worship. It is a strange thing to find at the centre of a World Heritage site, and almost nobody stops at it.

Official site (Japanese), precinct pages, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (Japanese) Wikipedia
Kamigamo Shrine賀茂別雷神社（上賀茂神社）
Shinto shrine · Kyoto · buildings from 677 · World Heritage
▶
What it is

The northern of the two Kamo shrines. It stands on a triangular slope where two streams meet, facing a mountain. It has the best English information of any place on this trip: its own website has a full English edition that is longer, and says more about individual objects, than its Japanese pages.

Official site, English edition · checked 2026-08-15

Where the god came from, and why the festival looks the way it does

The myth begins in the Tadasu-no-Mori forest you walked through this morning. Tamayorihime was purifying herself in the river when a vermilion arrow came floating downstream. She took it home, enshrined it, and became pregnant by its power. When her son came of age, his grandfather assembled the gods for a feast and asked the boy to point out his father among the guests. The boy answered that his father was in the heavens, and ascended there himself with a clap of thunder.

His mother prayed to see him once more. He appeared in a dream and gave instructions for the preparations that would bring him back: sacred garments, decorations of aoi and katsura, and a ritual involving horses. When these were carried out he descended, as an adult, onto the mountain behind this shrine.

⇒ 🔴 This legend is the basis of the Aoi Festival. The aoi leaves, the katsura branches and the horses of the festival are all things the god asked for in his instructions. The shrine calls the Aoi Festival one of Japan's three imperial festivals (the other two are Kasuga's and Iwashimizu's). It is a set of directions from a dream that is still being followed.

⚑ The arrow is also identified, which English retellings usually leave out. The older chronicle names it as the thunder deity of a shrine in the neighbouring district. So the god's father is identified.

Official site, English edition (the myth, quoted) · Shimogamo's citation of the Yamashiro Fudoki for the arrow's identity, translated · the shrine's own Kamo-sai page for the 1884 designation as one of "Japan's three imperial festivals", alongside Kasuga Taisha's Kasuga-sai and Iwashimizu Hachimangu's Iwashimizu-sai · checked 2026-08-22

The documentary founding — and a date the shrine gives two ways

The shrine's history states that in 677 "the Kamo Jingu was built by the province of Yamashiro, and the foundation of the shrine buildings — scarcely altered to this day — was laid."

⇒ ⚑ Shimogamo gives the same year for its own rebuilding in cypress bark and tile. The province had halls built at both Kamo shrines in the same year, and each shrine records the event without mentioning the other. It was a single act of the provincial government.

⚠ Kamigamo's own chronology page gives 678 instead of 677. Both years are on the shrine's website; this almanac does not pick one.

⚠ The shrine does not say why the province built it in that year. It gives a myth and a construction date, but no motive. It does explain why the shrine mattered after the capital moved here in 794: the north-east was the unlucky direction, called the demon gate, and the shrine stood to the north-east of the capital.

Official site, English edition and Japanese history page · Shimogamo official site for its own 677 · checked 2026-08-15

Yin and yang, and the fact that you can point at it

From the Heian period the Kamo family were noted practitioners of onmyodo, "the way of yin and yang" — used at court to purify, to identify lucky and unlucky directions, and to predict. The shrine states that the philosophy of balance between contrasting forces "is represented in various ways on the shrine grounds."

⇒ 🔴 Three of those objects are described in this entry: two in the viewing list and one in the garden section. It is rare to be able to point at a doctrine; usually all you get is an explanation on a signboard.

Official site, English edition, quoted · checked 2026-08-15

Every unifier in Japanese history, each with a specific act

The shrine's English history reads as a list of the powerful men who honoured this shrine: Emperor Kanmu made a pilgrimage after moving the capital in 794. Minamoto no Yoritomo bestowed protection. Oda Nobunaga provided a horse for the shrine's horse-race ritual. Toyotomi Hideyoshi sponsored one of the rebuildings. Tokugawa Ieyasu made a pilgrimage in 1600, the year of Sekigahara. And Emperor Meiji came in 1868 "to inform the shrine deity that governing power in Japan had been returned to the emperor."

⇒ ⚑ Over more than ten centuries, each man who took power in Japan honoured this shrine with something specific. The last entry is the most striking: a restored emperor formally telling a god that the government had changed hands.

Official site, English edition, quoted · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The precinct is free and open. The inner court in front of the National Treasure halls needs a separate guided admission. Five things to look at:

1. Count the pine needles on the two sand cones. Straight ahead as you pass the second torii stand two conical heaps of sand, shaped to represent the sacred mountain. The left cone has three needles set in its tip; the right has two. Odd and even: yang and yin. ⇒ This is the most useful thing to do at this shrine. The yin-yang idea is not explained on a plaque here. You see it by counting the needles on two piles of sand.

2. The two guardians are not a matching pair, and that is deliberate. Modern shrines put two identical komainu at the gate. Here it is a komainu on the left, silver, for the moon and yin; and a Chinese lion on the right, gold, for the sun and yang. The shrine notes this combination is the older tradition.

3. Do not cross the arched vermilion bridge. It stands in front of the gate and looks like the way in. It is used only by priests and designated participants during rites, so take one of the bridges beside it. ⚑ Without knowing this, most visitors would try to walk over it within thirty seconds of arriving.

4. The ema rack at the small shrine across the stream. This sub-shrine is dedicated to the mother of the god in the main hall, and it is the most important of the twenty-four subsidiary shrines. The priests at the main sanctuary do not begin their prayers until they hear the call from the priest at this sub-shrine. The mother's rite goes first. ⚑ Its votive tablets carry an image of Murasaki Shikibu and the poem she wrote here, on a pilgrimage to pray for a good marriage: "Oh, songbird! / While I remain here / Waiting for your call / In the forests of Kataoka / My garments grow wet with morning dew."

5. ⚠ The sacred horse will not be there. A live white horse is kept at the shrine and shown to visitors on Sundays and public holidays only. You are here on a Monday, so you will not see it.

Official site, English edition — grounds and sanctuaries pages, quoted · checked 2026-08-15

The garden on the east side, by Nakane Kinsaku

On the east side of the grounds is the Shokei-en, a garden laid out along a winding stream in a style popular in the Heian period. The shrine says it was made in 1960 by the landscape architect Nakane Kinsaku (1917–1995), to celebrate the birth of the present emperor, Naruhito. ⚑ Nakane also rebuilt the dry garden at Korin-in in Daitoku-ji, which you see later on 2 November.

Two things in it are worth finding:

1. The tree in the middle. It is about 300 years old and grows as several trunks from one root, so it is believed to bless families with happiness.

2. The boulder in the corner. It looks like two halves fused into one stone, and is called the Onmyoseki, "yin and yang stone", or the wishing stone. Visitors put a hand on each half, make a wish, then pray at the small Kamo Yamaguchi shrine nearby. ⇒ The Japan Tourism Agency's text on the shrine names this stone as the third object on the grounds that shows the yin-yang idea, after the two guardians and the sand cones.

Each April the garden holds a poetry event on the stream. It re-creates a poetry contest held in 1182 by Kamo Shigeyasu, the shrine's head priest at the time and a poet himself. Poets in Heian court dress sit along the bank while small boats carry cups of sake down the stream to them.

Official site, English edition, grounds page (Shokei-en), quoted · Japan Tourism Agency multilingual database (R3-00072) for the stone as the third yin-yang object · Kyoto Shunju (Korin-in page) for Nakane's Korin-in garden · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (English) Official — the grounds Wikipedia
Kamigamo Shake-machi上賀茂 社家町
Preservation district · Kyoto · the street the shrine's priests lived in · 2.7 hectares
▶
What it is

A lane of walled houses running east from the shrine along a stream, protected as a national preservation district. Kyoto City's own statutory plan describes what formed here: a townscape built since the Muromachi period as the residential quarter of the shrine's priests.

⚑ The plan also says what made the settlement unusual: up to the Meiji Restoration it was "a settlement of a special character in which the shrine's priests and farmers dwelt together." ⇒ Priests and farmers lived side by side here, which is why this kind of settlement has its own word, shake-machi.

Kyoto City statutory preservation plan (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

It is the same water as the shrine

The stream running past these houses is the stream that flows through the shrine precinct. Inside the grounds it is the Nara-no-ogawa. The moment it leaves the precinct it changes its name to the Myojin-gawa (明神川) and runs east past the priests' houses.

⇒ 🔴 The best fact about the place is in the city's planning document, and on no signboard:

"In particular, it is rare that a solidarity is formed through the water — water drawn from the stream into the front-garden ponds is kept pure, and is returned to the same stream."

⚑ Each house takes shrine water into its garden pond and must return it clean, because the next house downstream takes in the same water. A house that dirtied its pond would be dirtying its neighbour's. ⇒ What holds the community together is a practical rule: the water flows from house to house, so each house depends on the one upstream.

Kyoto City statutory preservation plan (Japanese), §3, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free, outside only, about five hundred metres of lane. ⚠ The houses stand far back in their plots, so what you see from the lane is walls, gates, bridges and treetops; the house fronts are hidden. The city's plan lists five features, and they make a good checklist for the walk:

1. The bridges over the stream. There are three kinds: earthen, plank and stone. The bridges are protected, and so are the stone-built banks. Each house has its own crossing. ⇒ Count the types as you go: the mix shows who could afford what, and when.

2. The earthen walls along the water.

3. The gates. Because the houses stand so far back, the gate was the main architectural statement each family could make.

4. The gable ends. The main houses are single-storey with tiled roofs, and the distinctive ones are entered from the gable end rather than the eaves side, with an ornament on the gable wall. The city's own repair standards name two forms of that ornament:

  • Inoko-sasu (豕扠首): a frame of slanting struts on the gable face, topped by a bearing block that carries the ridge beam, against a white plaster wall.
  • Tsuka-nuki (束貫): a grid of short upright posts and horizontal tie-beams on the gable face.

⚑ The city's plan notes that the ornament varies a little from house to house, while the layout of each property is almost the same. ⇒ Compare the gables as you walk past the houses. ⚠ Other official descriptions of the district word the ornament differently. These two forms are the ones in the city's own repair standards.

5. The green over the walls — the garden trees, which are the only part of the private world you are given.

⚑ The city counts 52 buildings in the district, of which about 63 percent are traditional. ⇒ Roughly a third of what you walk past is not historic, which is worth remembering before you photograph the lane as if all of it were.

Kyoto City statutory preservation plan (Japanese), §2 and §3, quoted and translated · Agency for Cultural Affairs heritage database · checked 2026-08-15 · the two gable forms: the plan's repair-standards table (表－3), and the stream's name: §1, both checked 2026-10-17

Links
Kyoto City preservation plan (Japanese) National preservation districts council (Japanese)
Daitoku-ji大徳寺
Rinzai Zen head temple · Kyoto · founded 1315 (or 1326 — see below)
▶
What it is

A walled compound of lanes and some two dozen sub-temples in northern Kyoto, head temple of its own branch of Rinzai Zen. Most of it is closed most of the time; what you do is walk the gravel lanes between walls and enter whichever sub-temples happen to be open. ⚠ It publishes nothing in English.

Official site (Japanese) · checked 2026-08-15

⚠ The temple gives two founding dates, and both are its own

Its official site says it was founded in 1315 by the Zen master Shuho Myocho. But the exhibition mounted for its anniversary — co-organised by the temple itself — says 1326, and calls 2026 the temple's 700th year.

⇒ ⚑ The arithmetic shows which date the anniversary uses: 1326 plus 700 is 2026, and 1315 cannot give a 700th anniversary this year. Both dates come from Daitoku-ji itself, and there is nothing to choose between them, so both are given here. (A third figure is implied elsewhere on the temple's site, which dates the founder's 300th memorial to 1636.)

Official site (Japanese) for 1315 · the 700th-anniversary exhibition site, co-organised by Daitoku-ji, for 1326 · both read 2026-08-15

It began as a hut, and the warlords made it

The official account is that it started as a small hermitage, which grew because of its founder's personal reputation for austere Zen. It was raised in status when two emperors became its patrons and it was styled "the peerless Zen garden of this realm." ⇒ It was not founded by the state; it was a hut that attracted emperors.

It was then laid waste in the Onin War and restored by the monk Ikkyu. The temple's account goes on to explain how the compound you walk through today came to look as it does:

"In the Momoyama period Toyotomi Hideyoshi conducted Oda Nobunaga's funeral here, built Soken-in to pray for Nobunaga's enlightenment, and together with it donated temple lands; taking that as the opening, the Sengoku warlords built sub-temples one after another and it reached its greatest prosperity."

⇒ 🔴 The compound took its shape because of one funeral. After Hideyoshi held his memorial for Nobunaga here, the warlords followed his lead and each paid for a sub-temple. The sub-temples along the lanes amount to a list of the Sengoku warlords who paid for them.

Official site (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Zen and tea, and the gate that helped kill a man

Daitoku-ji is the temple most closely tied to the link between Zen and the tea ceremony. All three tea-ceremony schools descended from Sen no Rikyu are formal supporters of its anniversary exhibition.

The tie is built into the temple itself. The main gate's first storey was donated by a renga poet who was Ikkyu's lay disciple; the second storey was added by Rikyu himself and named Kinmokaku. The temple then says, in careful wording, that there is a famous story: installing an image of Rikyu in that upper storey drew Hideyoshi's anger and became "one cause" of Rikyu's forced suicide.

⇒ ⚑ Note that the temple says "one cause", and stops short of "the cause". It is more careful than most guidebooks, which present the statue as the whole explanation. In the story, the offence was that Hideyoshi would have had to walk beneath a statue of a commoner tea master to enter the gate.

Official site (Japanese), quoted and translated · exhibition site for the three Sen houses' participation · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The lanes are free and open. Each sub-temple charges its own fee and keeps its own opening days. ⚠ The abbot's quarters, the National Treasure gate and the famous dry garden are in the main compound, which is not normally open to the public at all. The main compound's annual airing of treasures is currently suspended for restoration, so do not expect to see any of them. Four things to look at:

1. Walk the main axis and read it as a plan. From the imperial messenger's gate the buildings run in a straight line north: main gate, buddha hall, dharma hall, abbot's quarters. ⇒ A complete seven-hall Zen monastery laid out on one line is rarer than any single building in it. You can walk the whole layout in a few minutes, even with every door shut.

2. Stand under the main gate and think about the second storey. You cannot go up. The room above your head is the one whose statue of Rikyu helped cost him his life. ⚑ On 2 November, the day you stand there, that statue is on public display for the first time ever, in Tokyo, in the anniversary exhibition. ⚠ The statue on display is a 19th-century replacement for the original.

3. The dragon on the dharma hall ceiling. The dharma hall was rebuilt on its present site in 1636, for the 300th memorial of the founder, by Inaba Masakatsu and his son Masanori, lords of Odawara Castle. The dragon on its ceiling was painted by Kano Tan'yu at thirty-five. It is known as "the finest crying dragon in the land": clap your hands directly beneath it and a crackling echo, like a cry, rings round the hall. Dragons were painted on temple ceilings as water gods, to protect the building from fire. For the same reason the hall's walls carry a wave-pattern lattice (nami-renji). ⚠ The dharma hall is in the main compound, which is normally shut, so you will see it only from outside and will not hear the dragon.

4. Look at the gates as you pass, as well as the temples behind them. Each sub-temple's gate was somebody's donation, so the compound's social history can be read from the lane. That matters more here than at most temples, because the lane is most of what you will see.

Official site (Japanese) and the Rinzai sect council's head-temple page, quoted and translated · anniversary exhibition site · checked 2026-08-15 · the dharma hall's 1636 rebuilding: Rinzai sect council page, checked 2026-10-17 · the crying dragon, the fire belief and the wave lattice: nippon.com guide article (general reference), checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (Japanese) Rinzai sect council — Daitoku-ji (Japanese) Wikipedia
Soken-in総見院
Sub-temple of Daitoku-ji · built 1583 · Nobunaga's memorial temple
▶
What it is, and it is a political instrument

Built by Hideyoshi in 1583, the first anniversary of Oda Nobunaga's death, to pray for his enlightenment. Its founding abbot was Kokei Sochin — who was also Sen no Rikyu's own master in Zen.

⇒ 🔴 The organisation that runs its public openings states the political purpose outright, which is unusually blunt for a temple description: this is "a historically most important temple, built so that Hideyoshi could seize the initiative amid the succession struggle after Nobunaga's death."

⚑ Nobunaga was killed in the Honno-ji Incident with no settled succession. Hideyoshi was one of several generals with a claim. By staging the dead lord's funeral and building him a temple, Hideyoshi presented himself in public as the heir. The devotion was real, and it was also a political tool.

Kyoto Shunju (the opening's operator, Japanese), quoted and translated · Daitoku-ji's own history for the funeral and the land donation · checked 2026-08-15

Most of it is gone, and not because of a war

By tradition, a magnificent complex once stood on a vast precinct here. Much of it was lost in the Meiji-period persecution of Buddhism.

⇒ ⚑ The temple came through the Sengoku wars and the Edo period intact, and was then wrecked by a 19th-century government policy. The policy was the separation of Buddhism from Shinto, the same one that removed the Buddhist temples attached to the shrines you have visited over the past week. The most destructive event in its history was a government decision.

Kyoto Shunju (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

⚑ 2 November falls within its autumn opening period and is not one of its closed days. This matters because the sub-temple is shut for most of the year. ⚠ Cash only. The organisation running the opening warns that closed days can be added at short notice for temple services. Four things to look at:

1. The wall by the gate is two walls. The gate and the earthen wall survive from the 1583 foundation. The wall is a rare type called a "parent-and-child wall": a second wall built inside the first. ⇒ You can see it free from the lane. It is the oldest thing on the site, and almost nobody notices it.

2. The seated statue of Nobunaga in the main hall. Dedicated by Hideyoshi, it is an Important Cultural Property, life-size at about 115 cm, and was carved by a sculptor of the Kei school. That is the workshop lineage of Unkei and Tankei, whose work you saw at Todai-ji on Sat 31 Oct and at Sanjusangen-do on Sun 1 Nov. ⚑ The opening organisation describes it this way: "its blazing gaze conveys Nobunaga's likeness well." ⇒ The statue is 115 cm tall because he is shown seated; at that height it is life-size. It is a portrait of a specific man, made by people who could have known what he looked like.

3. The family graves. Seven markers stand together — Nobunaga, his principal wife, his daughter, a concubine and others. ⇒ The man who came closest to unifying Japan by force is buried in a small enclosure in a side temple, with his household.

4. Three tea rooms, and a record of who used them. The opening organisation says three tea rooms stand side by side here. It adds that a record survives of Hideyoshi setting up his own tea seat in this temple's hall during his Great Daitoku-ji Tea Gathering. Kyoto City's tourism site illustrates the autumn 2026 opening with a photo of one of the rooms, Juan-seki (寿安席). A general reference work names all three:

  • Juan-seki (寿安席): given by Yamaguchi Gendo, a businessman of the Meiji to Showa periods.
  • Koun-ken (香雲軒): built to the taste of Sokuchusai, 13th head of the Omotesenke school of tea.
  • Hoan (龐庵): its name board was written by Jimyosai, 14th head of Omotesenke.

⇒ Hideyoshi built this temple to establish his claim as Nobunaga's heir, so his tea gathering here was a political event as well as a cultural one.

Kyoto Shunju (the opening's operator, Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15 · the tea rooms: Kyoto Shunju and the Kyoto City tourism site's autumn-opening page, checked 2026-10-17 · the three names and their makers: Japanese Wikipedia (general reference), checked 2026-10-17

Links
Opening operator (Japanese) Wikipedia — Oda Nobunaga
Korin-in興臨院
Sub-temple of Daitoku-ji · founded in the 1520s · named after a dead man
▶
What it is

A Muromachi-period sub-temple of Daitoku-ji, founded in the 1520s by Hatakeyama Yoshifusa, military governor of Noto. A detail that is easy to miss: the temple is named after the founder's own posthumous Buddhist name. The building is his memorial and also carries his name.

It fell into ruin when his family declined. In 1581 Maeda Toshiie, later one of the Five Elders of Hideyoshi's government, had the roof re-thatched. From then on it served as the family temple of the Maeda, lords of Kaga, as well as of the Hatakeyama.

The temple's painted sliding doors were lost in the upheaval at the end of the Edo period and the Meiji Restoration. They have been replaced with ink paintings by the modern painter Muraishi Beisai. The main hall was taken apart and rebuilt in a full repair, finished in 1978.

Opening operator, Japanese and English editions, quoted · checked 2026-08-15 · the Five Elders: the operator's Japanese page, checked 2026-10-17 · the lost paintings and the 1978 repair: Waraku web, a Shogakukan magazine site (general reference), checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

⚑ 2 November falls within its autumn opening period and is not a closed day. ⚠ Cash only. ⚠ Do not expect autumn colour: the opening organisation's own page says the leaves around the main hall turn from late November, three weeks after your visit. Three things to look at:

1. Look at the height of the roof, the building's main feature. The opening organisation says so directly: its distinguishing feature is that the roof is lower than in the early-modern halls built from the Momoyama period onward, and that this gives the building stability and grace. ⇒ 🔴 You will see a dozen abbot's halls in Kyoto on this trip, and they will run together in memory. At Korin-in you can see what changed: stand back, note how low the roof line is, and compare every hall you enter afterwards against it.

2. The karamon gate. It has a curved gable and a cypress-bark roof, with wave-form lattice windows and cusped windows. These are features of Zen architecture, and of the Muromachi period specifically. ⚑ The Japanese and English pages about it differ on whether the gate has its own cultural-property designation or is included in the main hall's; the Japanese wording is the precise one.

3. The dry garden, and what you are looking at. Stone groupings in white sand representing the isles of the immortals. ⚠ It is not an original Muromachi garden. It was reconstructed from documentary evidence by Nakane Kinsaku during the main hall's dismantling repair. The operator calls Nakane "the Kobori Enshu of the Showa era". ⇒ ⚑ You saw his work earlier today: Nakane Kinsaku also made the Shokei-en garden at Kamigamo Shrine, in 1960. A 20th-century landscape architect designed two of today's gardens, which is worth remembering when you are told a garden is ancient.

Opening operator, Japanese and English editions, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Opening operator (English) Opening operator (Japanese)

Tuesday 3 November — Saga Toriimoto and the Takao valley

North-west of Kyoto · a pilgrim road, a hamlet that was once a resort, and three temples strung along one river · this is where Japanese esoteric Buddhism was founded
Saga Toriimoto嵯峨鳥居本
Preservation district · Kyoto · 600 m of the old Atago pilgrim road · protected 1979
▶
What it is

Six hundred metres of street climbing the old highway toward Mount Atago, protected as a national preservation district since 1979. Its name means "at the foot of the shrine gate". The great vermilion torii at the top of the street is the first gate of Atago Shrine, which stands still some way up the mountain.

Kyoto City statutory preservation plan (Japanese) · Japan Tourism Agency multilingual database, English edition · checked 2026-08-15 · the length, about 600 m along the Atago road: the plan, re-checked 2026-10-17

Farming village and temple-gate town, and where you can see each

Kyoto City's own plan gives the sequence. The settlement began around the end of the Muromachi period, based on farming, forestry and fishing. In the mid-Edo period, as more and more pilgrims went up to Atago, it took on the additional character of a temple-gate town, and teahouses appeared among the farmhouses.

⇒ 🔴 You can still see the village's two characters, farming village and temple-gate town, in the buildings, and the plan says so outright. The district divides into an upper and a lower half at Adashino Nenbutsu-ji, in the middle. The upper half, nearer the torii, is thatched farmhouse-type buildings. The lower half is townhouse-type, with lattice fronts and slatted "insect-cage" windows in the upper storey.

⚑ As you walk uphill, the architecture changes from town to countryside at a fixed point. The street shows the village's economic history in the order the buildings went up: the service trade at the bottom, near the traffic, and farming at the top. The dividing point is Adashino Nenbutsu-ji, a temple full of the dead.

Kyoto City statutory preservation plan (Japanese), §1 and §3, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Why anyone was walking up here at all

Atago Shrine, at the top of the mountain, is the shrine of fire prevention: people prayed to its god so that their wooden city would not burn. In a capital built almost entirely of timber, that made it an important cult. Kyoto City's plan describes how this farming settlement also took on the character of a temple-gate town for the Atago pilgrimage in the mid-Edo period.

⇒ ⚑ Kyoto still knows the charm from this mountain. A foreword in the bulletin of the national Fire and Disaster Management Agency says that, to people in Kyoto, "beware of fire" means this paper charm from Atago, printed 火迺要慎, the one seen on the kitchen walls of Kyoto townhouses in dramas set in the city. The street grew up on the traffic of people walking up Mount Atago to pray against fire.

Kyoto City tourism register, Kiyotaki entry (Japanese), for Atago as the fire-prevention shrine · Kyoto City preservation plan (Japanese) · Fire and Disaster Management Agency, foreword to its bulletin (Japanese), for the kitchen charm · all checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free, and open at all hours, since it is a public street. About half an hour on foot up from the Arashiyama end. ⚠ Only about half the buildings in the district are designated as traditional, so roughly every other building you photograph is not historic. Four things to look at:

1. Look at the roofs and find the boundary. Thatch above, tile below, with the change at Adashino Nenbutsu-ji in the middle. ⇒ Once you have found that boundary, the layout of the whole street makes sense.

2. The upper storeys of the lower half. Look for mushiko windows, narrow slatted openings in a plastered half-storey. The buildings are chū-nikai, "middle second floor": the upper level was kept low and was not a full storey, because a full storey facing a road a lord might use was considered presumptuous.

3. The hedges and the trees are protected too. The preservation order covers hedges and trees as well as the buildings. That is why the street looks continuous, with no modern gaps between the preserved houses.

4. The preservation hall, just past Adashino Nenbutsu-ji. It is an early-Meiji merchant house that the city opened as a small museum about the street. ⚠ The day's plan has you pass before it opens, so you will see it from the outside only. From the street, look at what the government commentary points out about the house: fine lattice on the ground floor, and an upper storey with white plaster walls and slatted mushiko windows.

Kyoto City preservation plan and preservation-hall page (Japanese) · Japan Tourism Agency multilingual database, English (the house's front), re-checked 2026-10-17 · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Government commentary (English) Kyoto City preservation plan (Japanese) Adashino Nenbutsu-ji (English)
Kiyotaki清滝
Hamlet · Kyoto · the foot of Mount Atago · once a railway resort
▶
What it is

A small village in a river gorge, where the pilgrim road reaches the mountain. Kyoto City's own description is brief and exact: "Kiyotaki opened as the post-station of the Atago pilgrimage." The village grew up because pilgrims needed somewhere to sleep before the climb.

Kyoto City tourism register (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Four economies in one hamlet

Read in order, the four stages below make one of the more complete small histories you will walk through:

1. A service settlement for mountain ascetics. The village began as the starting point of the climb for yamabushi, the mountain ascetics. People who served these practitioners and their lodges settled at the foot of the mountain.

2. An Edo post-town. Belief in Atago's warrior-protecting Jizo spread among lords and samurai across the country. The retainers who escorted daimyo on pilgrimage built houses here, and the village filled with inns and teahouses.

3. 🔴 A modern leisure resort. Few visitors expect this part. In 1929 two lines opened for pilgrims to Atago: a railway from Arashiyama to this village, and a cable car from here up the mountain. An amusement park, a hotel and a ski ground followed on top. A Kyoto University study of the village says the mountain was then called "paradise on earth".

4. Then it stopped. During the Second World War the lines were shut, together with the resort, so that their steel could be handed over for the war effort. The Kyoto University study adds that the railway was classed as a "non-essential, non-urgent line" and was never reopened after the war. The inns and teahouses along the road then closed one after another. ⚠ General reference works give the year of closure as 1944.

⇒ ⚑ A ski resort with its own railway was shut down by the war, and the hamlet has been quiet ever since. Kiyotaki did modernise, and fast, and then lost it all.

Kyoto City tourism register (Japanese) · Kyoto City culture bureau, Saga and Kiyotaki walking map (Japanese), for the 1929 lines, the resort and the wartime closure · Kyoto University study of Kiyotaki (Japanese) for "paradise on earth", the "non-essential" class and the closures · stages 1 and 2, and the year 1944: Japanese-language general reference works · checked 2026-10-17

Basho revised a poem about this place while he was dying

The verse attached to Kiyotaki is:

Kiyotaki ya / nami ni chirikomu / ao-matsuba
"Kiyotaki — green pine needles scattering down into the waves."

An earlier version of the Kiyotaki verse read "Kiyotaki — no dust upon the waves; the summer moon." ⇒ Basho revised it on his sickbed in Osaka three days before he died, in 1694, apparently to avoid re-using an image he had just used in another late poem.

⚑ Basho, with three days to live, was changing a word in a poem about a river gorge he would not see again. ⚠ Two stones carrying the verse are said to stand in the village and downstream, but only informal sources say so; look for them, but do not count on finding them.

Japanese classical commentary via an academic host, translated · Kyoto City culture bureau, Saga and Kiyotaki walking map (Japanese), which says that Basho and Yosano Akiko wrote here, checked 2026-10-17 · ⚠ the verse stones: no official source found; informal sources only · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free, with no gates. You reach Kiyotaki in the morning, at the bottom of the gorge, and walk on up the river to Jingo-ji. Three things to look at:

1. From Kiyotaki on, the day follows the Kiyotaki River, and its gorge has two names. The river runs past all three temples you visit today and on down to this village. Upstream of the village the gorge is called Kin'un-kei; downstream it is Kinrei-kei. ⇒ The clay discs that visitors throw from Jingo-ji's western terrace land in the upper gorge, in the same water that runs through this village.

2. The vermilion bridge. The Wataruzaru-bashi ("the bridge the monkeys cross") is the village's landmark. The Kinrei and Seiryu bridges are nearby.

3. The road tunnel is a railway tunnel. The prefectural road that short-cuts the pass runs through a converted single-track tunnel of the railway abolished in 1944. ⚠ It is narrow and poorly suited to walking. You may walk through it, but the recommended walking route is over the pass from Toriimoto. ⇒ ⚑ The road into the village runs on the old trackbed, which explains its width.

Kyoto City tourism register (Japanese) for the river, the gorge names and the bridges, re-checked 2026-10-17 · Kyoto City culture bureau walking map (Japanese) for the tunnel's railway origin and its conversion to a road, checked 2026-10-17 · Japanese-language reference works for the single track, the year 1944 and the walking advice, checked 2026-08-15

Links
Kyoto City tourism register (Japanese) Wikipedia — Matsuo Basho
Jingo-ji高雄山 神護寺
Shingon temple · Takao · two temples merged in 824 · where Japanese esoteric Buddhism was founded
▶
What it is

A mountain temple reached by a long stone stair up from the river, holding sixteen National Treasures. ⚠ Its website prints, in English, the words "Sorry, Japanese only."

Official site (Japanese) · Takao preservation association · checked 2026-08-15

Two temples merged into one, and why each was built

Jingan-ji, "the temple of the deity's vow", was built in 781 by the statesman Wake no Kiyomaro, after he sought an oracle at the Usa Hachiman shrine and was told: "Copy the whole Buddhist canon, make Buddhist images, recite the sutra, build a temple compound, and pray for the peace of ten thousand generations." The temple's name comes straight from that instruction.

At about the same time Kiyomaro built Takaosan-ji on this mountain. The temple's own account is careful about its purpose: "rather than a mere clan mortuary temple, it is thought to have been built as a training hall for monks aiming at mountain austerities, dissatisfied with the urban Buddhism of Nara until then."

⇒ 🔴 Building a training hall for mountain austerities explains why the temple is on a mountain. By then Nara Buddhism, which you saw in and around Nara on 29–31 October, had become an institution of the capital: endowed, political, and practised indoors in the city. Takaosan-ji was built for monks who wanted to get away from all that and train on a mountain. In 824 the two temples were merged, the name became Jingo-ji, and the whole temple was handed to Kukai.

Official site (Japanese), history pages, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 Saicho and Kukai both worked here — and in 812 Saicho was initiated by Kukai

Two men founded the schools that would dominate Japanese Buddhism for the next thousand years: Saicho, who founded Tendai, and Kukai, who founded Shingon. Both worked here, one after the other, under the same family's patronage.

In 802 Kiyomaro's sons invited Saicho to lecture on the Lotus Sutra here, as a memorial for their aunt. The temple calls those lectures "the first utterance of Heian Buddhism." After returning from China in 805, Saicho came back and opened Japan's first esoteric initiation altar in this temple. Kukai arrived in 809, and in 810 he performed here the first state-protection rite he ever conducted.

⇒ 🔴🔴 In 812 came the event that makes this temple unique: Saicho received esoteric initiation from Kukai, here. Saicho was the senior man, already famous and already the founder of a school, and he knelt to the younger Kukai. Seventeen of Saicho's own disciples followed him a few months later.

⚑ The register of those initiated survives, in Kukai's own handwriting, and it is a National Treasure. Saicho's name is written at the head of the list. The temple points out another name on it: Taihan, the disciple who left Saicho for Kukai. His defection became one of the causes of the break between the two men, which was permanent. ⇒ One sheet of paper records both the high point of their collaboration and one cause of its end. Kukai wrote it as a working memo, so the temple notes that it also shows his free, unconstrained hand.

⚠ The register is shown only in early May, so you will not see it.

Official site (Japanese), history and treasures pages, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

A monk who wrote a rule against violence into his own temple

The temple was revived in the 12th century by Mongaku, a monk who had strong-armed the retired emperor Go-Shirakawa into funding it. His forty-five article oath of 1185 survives as a National Treasure, carrying Go-Shirakawa's own palm-print in vermilion at head and foot.

Article one: the monks shall be of one mind. Article two: they shall not select by high or low birth. Then comes the article on protest, which is worth reading twice:

"When there is a grave suit, lead the monks and petition the court… if no judgment is granted, stand in the palace court and spend your whole life doing so. Even if you die without returning to the temple, you shall never, by private force, plan battle and settle it by victory or defeat, nor flatter officials and give bribes."

⇒ ⚑ Mongaku wrote his oath in the century when armed monks from the great temples were a standing threat to the capital. He had personally coerced an emperor, yet he made it permanent law that his successors must never use force or bribery, and must instead stand and wait until they died. Go-Shirakawa, the emperor he had coerced, pressed his palm on the document in red.

Official site (Japanese), treasures page, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The temple is up a long stone stair from the river, and the precinct is spread over terraces. Five things to look at:

1. 🔴 The Daishi-do, open during its one week of the year. Most of the temple's buildings have burned more than once. The temple traces the Daishi-do to the Noryo-bo, the quarters where Kukai lived. The present building dates from the Momoyama period, and it has a roof of wooden shingles. It is laid out like a house: four bays on its left side and five on its right, an asymmetry no ritual hall would have. ⚑ Inside is a plank-carved image of Kukai, carved in 1302 as a copy of an older image in Shikoku. The temple notes that nobody knows where that older image is now. ⇒ The hall is opened 1–7 November, and you are there on 3 November. Plank-carved figures are rare in Japan, and this is one of the few days a year when this one can be seen.

2. The main hall dates from 1935, and its patron is worth knowing. The vermilion Kondo (main hall), the pagoda above it and several other buildings were all donated in the 1930s by a single lay devotee. ⇒ Most of what looks ancient here is about ninety years old. The temple's age is in its objects and its history; most of its buildings are recent.

3. The Yakushi statue in the main hall (National Treasure): look at the face. The temple's own description is unusually vivid: "a piercing, fixed gaze; a thick nose-ridge and fleshy nostrils; lips boldly thrust forward and clamped." Its explanation: "such an uncanny physiognomy, which raises dread in the worshipper, may be a product of the presence of ascetic mountain practitioners." ⇒ The statue looks severe because the men who commissioned it were severe.

4. Throw clay discs at the western end. From the garden at the far west of the precinct, above the gorge, visitors throw small unglazed earthenware discs out into the ravine to carry misfortune away. ⚠ The claim that this temple invented the practice is widely repeated but rests on informal sources. The practice itself and its purpose are properly documented.

5. The Jingo-ji statue that Narita-san was founded around. In 939, during a rebellion, a Fudo image carved by Kukai was carried east from this temple to subdue the rebellion. Afterwards the image "would not move", and a temple was founded around it where it stood. ⇒ That temple is Narita-san, today one of the most visited in Japan. It began with a statue from this mountain.

Official site (Japanese), treasures and history pages, quoted and translated · Agency for Cultural Affairs heritage database for the Daishi-do's dimensions · Takao preservation association for the disc-throwing site · checked 2026-08-15 · the Daishi-do's Momoyama date and shingle roof: Kyoto Prefectural Board of Education, list of repair works (Japanese), and its origin in Kukai's quarters: the temple's page on the plank-carved image, both checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (Japanese) Takao preservation association (English) Wikipedia
Saimyo-ji槇尾山 西明寺
Shingon temple · Makinoo · founded c. 824–834 · independent since 1290
▶
What it is

The middle one of the day's three temples (Jingo-ji, Saimyo-ji, Kozan-ji), small and quiet, reached over a vermilion bridge and up two flights of steps under maples. It was founded in the 824–834 period by Chisen, a senior disciple of Kukai, as a branch temple of Jingo-ji.

Official site (Japanese) · checked 2026-08-15 · no English edition

Founded for a different purpose from Jingo-ji

Jingo-ji was where the esoteric teachings were passed on. Saimyo-ji was opened as a kairitsu dojo, a training hall for the monastic precepts (the rules governing how a monk lives). Its own site still describes it that way: "a mountain temple that still values the quiet environment young monks need for practice."

⇒ ⚑ So the valley was organised by function. Jingo-ji handled the doctrine and the initiations; Saimyo-ji, twenty minutes down the road, handled the discipline. The monastic complex was spread across the valley in separate temples, and walking from one to the other is like walking between departments.

Saimyo-ji stayed a dependency of Jingo-ji for 466 years. In 1290 a retired emperor granted it its own temple title, and it became independent of Jingo-ji. It burned in the wars of the 1560s and was restored in 1602 by a vinaya master (a teacher of monastic discipline) of the early-Edo precepts revival, so in 1602 it was again doing the job it was founded for. ⚑ The nine-article monastic rule from that restoration still hangs as a wooden tablet in the guest hall.

Official site (Japanese), history page, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Small; half an hour is enough. Five things to look at, and the first is on the way in:

1. The stone pillar on the left of the steps. It is carved with four characters meaning "the outer aspect of the great boundary", a marker declaring that the ground above this point is pure. ⇒ The sacred ground begins at this pillar, before you reach any gate.

2. The main hall's plan is unusual for its sect. It was built in 1700, donated by the mother of the fifth Tokugawa shogun, and made entirely of zelkova wood. It is seven bays by four, divided into three along its depth: the centre is the inner sanctuary, and the flanking bays serve as the outer sanctuary. The temple states plainly that this is "an unusual plan for the main hall of a Shingon temple." ⇒ You can see from the doorway that the hall does not follow the usual Shingon layout.

3. The main image, and an attribution the temple's own pages disagree on. A standing Shakyamuni of the Seiryo-ji type: one of a line of copies of an image said to have been carved during the Buddha's lifetime, which is why the face and drapery look unlike other buddhas. ⚠ The temple's own pages contradict each other on whether it is by Unkei or only attributed to him. This entry calls it attributed, the more cautious of the two claims.

4. Open the doors on the east side and look at the moss garden. In the temple's own words, the view from inside the hall is "like two framed hanging scrolls": a white-walled storehouse on the left and, on the right, a stone pagoda set on a moss-covered natural boulder.

5. The old tree that named the place. On the right as you walk back toward the gate stands a 700-year-old umbrella pine. It is said to be the origin of the place-name Makinoo, which is the name of this mountain and of the temple's address.

⚑ One more curiosity on the way out, beside the pond: a miniature Ashoka pillar carved in 1912 by a Fukushima stonemason. Unlike the Indian original, it has four horses on it.

Official site (Japanese), history, architecture and walking-tour pages, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (Japanese) Takao preservation association (English)
Kozan-ji栂尾山 高山寺
Shingon-lineage temple · Toganoo · refounded 1206 by Myoe · World Heritage
▶
What it is

The topmost of the day's three temples (Jingo-ji, Saimyo-ji, Kozan-ji), in cedar woods, with one surviving medieval building and an archive of over twelve thousand items. ⚑ Its own English site notes that everyone pronounces the name "Kozanji" but that the official pronunciation is "Kosanji."

Kozan-ji's name comes from a plaque granted by a retired emperor in 1206 reading "the temple of the high mountain the rising sun first illumines", a phrase from the Avatamsaka Sutra, the text this temple was refounded to study.

Official site, English edition and Japanese edition · checked 2026-08-15

Myoe, the monk who refounded it

Myoe (1173–1232), the monk who refounded the temple, is one of the strangest figures in Japanese religious history, and the temple does not tone him down.

⚑ Myoe lost both parents at eight and was tonsured under Mongaku at Jingo-ji, the first of today's three temples. ⇒ So Kozan-ji, the last temple of the day, was refounded by a monk raised at Jingo-ji, and as an adult he went back and rebuilt Jingo-ji.

The temple's own biography sums Myoe up: "In his youth he cut off his right ear out of the will to seek the Way, and out of longing for Shakyamuni twice planned a journey to India." He never went. He kept his worked-out itinerary (the daily distances, the arrival dates) in a sutra bag for the rest of his life, with a note in the margin reading "Ah, ah — how I long to go."

Myoe cut off his ear in front of a particular painting, which survives here as a National Treasure and carries his own handwriting, including the words "the honoured mother of the earless monk" and a poem: "Think of me together with pity, O Buddha — there is none who knows me but you." ⇒ Orphaned at eight, he took Shakyamuni as his father and this painted figure as his mother, and wrote so on the painting.

Official site (Japanese), biography page written by a named Kyoto University academic, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Myoe's teaching, and a deathbed the temple says embodied it

Myoe's teaching is called gonmitsu — his own fusion of Kegon doctrine with Shingon esoteric practice, two systems normally kept apart.

⇒ 🔴 The temple makes an extraordinary claim about how Myoe died. He arranged the room himself, placing a Maitreya image, hanging a specific mandala and chanting two particular mantras. The temple's own verdict is that "the scene of his death was precisely gonmitsu made concrete." The Kegon objects and the esoteric objects stood side by side around a dying man who had spent his life arguing that they belonged together.

Myoe's maxim, which the temple puts first and which survives on a wooden tablet, is aru beki yo wa. The temple's own English is "do what is appropriate," explained as ask yourself how you should behave given the time and the circumstances, and act accordingly. ⚑ Seven syllables and no metaphysics, from the man who cut off his own ear.

Official site (Japanese and English editions), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

The animal scrolls — and where they actually are

This temple owns the Choju-jinbutsu-giga, four scrolls of rabbits, frogs and monkeys behaving like people, which are universally described as the ancestor of Japanese comics.

⚠ 🔴 You will not see the original Choju-jinbutsu-giga scrolls here. They are on deposit at the national museums in Tokyo and Kyoto, though the temple still owns them. The temple's own English page says of the most famous scroll that "a reproduction of this scroll is displayed at Sekisuiin." ⇒ The scroll on display here is a copy, and the temple says so.

The temple is clear on one point worth remembering: the artist of the Choju-jinbutsu-giga is unknown. The traditional attribution is doubted, other names have been proposed, and the temple's page states flatly: "None is confirmed; the artist is unknown." The scrolls are thought to continue a tradition of impromptu caricature drawn by monks.

Official site, Japanese page (dating and authorship, quoted) and English page (the reproduction) · Japanese-language reference works for the museum deposits · checked 2026-08-15

The tea claim, and the temple's own correction of its monument

Kozan-ji is "known as the place where tea was first made in Japan." The story is that Eisai brought tea seeds from China and gave them to Myoe, who planted them here and recommended tea to the monks because it dispelled the sleepiness that hinders practice. Tea from here was later called honcha, "true tea", and all other tea hicha, "not tea." Tea growing spread from here to Uji.

⇒ ⚑ The temple then contradicts its own monument. A stone here reads "the oldest tea garden in Japan", but the temple's own page states that the oldest garden was actually across the river, and that the stele stands on the site of a former monastic hall. The temple tells you its own landmark is in the wrong place.

Official site (Japanese), tea garden page, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Wooded, steep in places, and quiet. Four things to look at:

1. Sekisui-in, the one building left from Myoe's time, which has a more complicated history than it looks. ⚠ The temple's English page calls it a nobleman's residence in the Kamakura style. Its Japanese page tells a different and better story. The building began as the East Sutra Repository. The original hall called Sekisui-in was destroyed by a flood in 1228, and the repository then took over the name. It later became a combined repository and shrine hall, and in 1889 it was moved to its present site and altered into residential form. ⇒ The temple's own summary: "changing name, changing role, changing place, it survives as the sole relic of Myoe's era." Where the two official pages disagree, this entry follows the Japanese one.

2. Sit down on the south side, in the spot the temple describes. "Step back from the veranda and sit on the tatami, and the landscape is cropped like a picture frame by the pillars, the shutters and the broad veranda." ⇒ The temple is telling you how to use the building, and the framed view only works from that spot.

3. Count the stones on the front approach. Where the great gate once stood, the path has seventeen square slabs laid in a row under dappled light. The temple itself points this out as a designed effect.

4. ⚠ Two things you cannot see. The tea house is normally closed. And the seated statue of Myoe in the founder's hall is shown at the tea offering on 8 November, five days after your visit on 3 November.

⚑ One last thing about the temple's archive. Among its National Treasures are two Chinese books that survive nowhere else in the world: they were lost in China and preserved only here. ⇒ These two Chinese texts still exist because a temple on a Kyoto hillside kept them.

Official site, Japanese pages (Sekisui-in's history, the approach, the archive) and English pages · checked 2026-08-15 · Sekisui-in's history re-checked on the Japanese About page, 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (English) Official site (Japanese) Wikipedia

Wednesday 4 November — Daigo-ji, Tofuku-ji and the Fushimi Inari loop

South-east Kyoto · a garden a dying man designed for one party, the oldest wooden building in the prefecture, a Zen gate and a 1939 garden, then ten thousand torii up a mountain · the climb to Kami-Daigo only if the temple has lifted its closure
Sanbo-in三宝院
Head priests' residence · Daigo-ji · founded 1115 · garden laid out by Hideyoshi in 1598
▶
What it is

Sanbo-in is a residence: the house the head priests of Daigo-ji lived in. The temple's own page dates it exactly: "Sanboin temple was built in 1115 by Shokaku, the 14th archbishop of Daigoji Temple." Almost every building in it is an Important Cultural Property, and two are National Treasures: the main drawing room (the Omote-shoin) and the gate (the Kara-mon). Daigo-ji's English site calls the Omote-shoin "one of the most iconic buildings of Medieval Japan."

Daigo-ji official site, English grounds page and English Sanboin page; Japanese Sanboin page for the 1115 date · checked 2026-08-15

The garden was designed by a dying man for a single afternoon

The garden you look at from the drawing room was designed all at once, for one occasion. Toyotomi Hideyoshi drew it himself in 1598 (the temple's Japanese page says he "personally made the basic design"), and he made it for one event: the flower-viewing party held here on the fifteenth day of the third month of 1598.

The scale of that party is recorded on the temple's own archive pages. Seven hundred cherry trees were dug up and replanted along the approach, brought in from the home provinces around the capital and from Yoshino. The abbot of Sanbo-in, Gien, hosted the party and wrote it up in his diary, calling it "a great rejoicing for the temple" and "a satisfaction of my own person." Hideyoshi's wife, his consort Yodo, his son Hideyori and Maeda Toshiie were all there.

⇒ 🔴 Hideyoshi died five months later. The party was in the third month, and he died on 18 August 1598, aged 62. The garden was not finished in his lifetime; Gien carried on the work after him. ⚑ You are looking at the last thing Japan's most powerful man designed, and he never saw it finished.

⚑ This room links to two other days of your trip. The cherry trees came from Yoshino, the ridge you walk on 5 November. And on 2 November you stood in Soken-in, the sub-temple Hideyoshi built in 1583 as Nobunaga's memorial temple: the same man securing his power, fifteen years before this party. Hideyoshi buried his lord in Kyoto, and later threw the largest party in the country in a garden he drew himself.

Daigo-ji official archive feature on the Daigo flower-viewing (Japanese), for the date, the seven hundred trees, their origin, Gien's diary and Hideyoshi's death; Daigo-ji Japanese Sanboin page for the design attribution; Daigo-ji official annual-events page for his age at death · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 And there was going to be a second party, in this season, and he died first

The temple ties this garden to the season you are visiting in, on a page it wrote about autumn. After the flower-viewing, Hideyoshi was looking forward to an autumn "Daigo maple-hunt." He died that summer. The temple's page ends with its own conclusion: Daigo-ji's autumn colour may be the keepsake Hideyoshi left to later generations.

⇒ 🔴 You are here on 4 November, in the season of the maple party he did not live to hold, standing in the garden he built for the flower party he did hold. Among all its autumn views, the temple singles out this garden, because the maples and Hideyoshi's design are seen together as one picture, and that is only possible in autumn.

Daigo-ji official annual-events page, "the maple-hunt Hideyoshi never had" (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

The drawing room is a theatre, and the seating is graded by rank

The Omote-shoin is a National Treasure, built as a working reception room. It faces the garden along its whole length. The temple describes a veranda with a balustrade and an izumi-dono, a water pavilion, built into the south-west corner. This is the shinden-zukuri layout of a Heian aristocrat's mansion, used here for a priest's residence.

It has three rooms (lower, middle and upper), and their purpose is the interesting part. The lower room has a second name: the "raised-stage room." When its tatami are lifted, it becomes a Noh stage. The middle and upper rooms are built a step higher, and the temple says plainly why: so that Noh and kyogen can be watched from above.

⇒ ⚑ The whole room works as an auditorium. The floor of the lower room converts to a stage, and the two higher rooms are tiered seating, where the level you sat on showed your rank.

🔴 The sliding-door paintings show a route you walked on 2 November. The Important Cultural Property fusuma here show the Aoi Festival procession travelling from Shimogamo Shrine to Kamigamo Shrine, the two shrines you walked between that day.

Daigo-ji official Japanese Sanboin page, for the three rooms, the raised-stage room, the tiering for viewing, the water pavilion and the subject of the fusuma; English Sanboin page for the shinden-zukuri note · checked 2026-08-15

The gate that opened for almost nobody

The Kara-mon is the other National Treasure. The temple's English page describes it as "the special gate which opened the doors only when the Imperial messengers visited. It was entirely black-lacquered and employed four large chrysanthemum and paulownia motifs plated with gold." The chrysanthemum is the imperial crest and the paulownia is Hideyoshi's, so both houses appear on one door. A restoration finished in July 2010, after about a year and a half of work, returned the gate to its black-and-gold state.

Daigo-ji official site, English Sanboin page (the Kara-mon description) and Japanese Sanboin page (the 2010 restoration) · checked 2026-08-15

What is actually in the garden — including a stone that was a trophy

The temple names the garden's pieces individually, and three of them are worth knowing before you look:

🔴 The Fujito Stone. This is the group of stones at the centre of the garden, which the temple says represents the Amida triad. It is called "the finest stone under heaven", and the name has nothing to do with Buddhism: it comes from the fact that it was handed down through successive warlords. ⇒ ⚑ The men who ran Japan passed this rock between them as a token of power, and it ended up in the middle of Hideyoshi's garden. Like the cherry trees and an entire hall in the lower precinct, much of this garden was brought here from elsewhere.

🔴 The three Kamo stones, in front of the pond, are three oddly shaped rocks that together depict a river. The temple gives each one a meaning: the left is the Kamo River running fast, the middle is the water lying still, and the right is the water breaking and scattering apart. ⇒ It is the same river whose myth, shrines and priests' houses you followed on 2 November, shown here as three stones for three states of moving water.

Turtle Island and Crane Island. Turtle Island is shaped like a shell and covered by a thick-trunked five-needle pine said to be over six hundred years old; the temple says the island expresses stillness. Crane Island is immediately to the west. There, the stone bridge on the left is the crane's neck, and the island expresses a bird about to take off. ⇒ The two islands are a matched pair: one at rest, one about to fly.

Also here: the Toyokuni Daimyojin shrine, which enshrines Hideyoshi himself. It was built in gratitude for his restoration of this residence and garden, his repair of the five-storey pagoda and his rebuilding of the main hall. There is also a three-tiered waterfall in the south-east corner, and the teahouse Chinryu-tei. Its entrance is unusual: a "noble's door" that you walk through without stooping, instead of the usual small hatch you crawl through.

Daigo-ji official Japanese Sanboin page, per-feature descriptions of the Fujito Stone, the three Kamo stones, Turtle and Crane Islands, the Toyokuni shrine, the waterfall and the teahouse · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The visit is an indoor circuit through a residence: matted rooms, a corridor, and a garden you look at from the veranda without walking in it. Interiors are generally not photographed. Six things to look for:

1. The Kara-mon, before you go in: count the crests. Black lacquer, gold, and four large crests of chrysanthemum and paulownia. ⇒ The gate shows who the guest was (the emperor's messengers) and who the patron was (Hideyoshi), and it stayed shut to everyone else.

2. In the Omote-shoin, look at the floor levels before you look at the garden. Find where the lower room ends and the middle and upper rooms step up. ⇒ The low floor is the stage, the raised rooms are the seats, and which level you were allowed to sit on depended on who you were.

3. The sliding doors in the Omote-shoin. The procession painted on them runs from Shimogamo to Kamigamo, the same route you walked on 2 November.

4. Then the garden: go to the middle first. The group of stones at the centre is the Fujito Stone. ⇒ It was handed from warlord to warlord before it came here, so it has travelled further than anything else in the garden.

5. In front of the pond, find the three stones and read them left to right: fast water, still water, water shattering. ⇒ The three rocks stand for the Kamo River, the river you walked along on 2 November.

6. The two pine islands. The island under the huge pine is the turtle. That tree is said to be over six hundred years old, which would make it at least 170 years older than the garden around it, laid out in 1598. The island next to it is the crane, and the stone bridge is the crane's neck.

Daigo-ji official Japanese Sanboin page for the room levels, the fusuma subject and the four garden features; English Sanboin page for the Kara-mon · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official Sanboin page (English) Official Sanboin page (Japanese) Official page on the autumn colour Hideyoshi missed (Japanese) Official grounds overview (English)
Daigo-ji — the lower precinct醍醐寺（下醍醐・伽藍）
Shingon head temple · south-east Kyoto · World Heritage 1994 · the oldest wooden building in Kyoto Prefecture
▶
What it is

The lower precinct is the flat, wooded lower half of a temple that runs from a suburban street up to a mountain top. Daigo-ji's English site calls this part "the largest area of the Daigoji temple" and names what is in it: "its magnificent Main Hall and its 38-metres Five-storied Pagoda, the oldest wooden structure of all Kyoto built in 951." By its own count the temple holds more than 150,000 preserved cultural items, and it was inscribed as World Heritage in December 1994.

Daigo-ji official site, English Explore page and English front page; official history page for the 1994 inscription · checked 2026-08-15

The temple is named after a taste

The strangest fact about the temple is its name, and it is on the temple's own pages. Daigo is a Buddhist word for the last and finest stage of refining milk, the clarified essence, used as a metaphor for the highest teaching. The temple is named after it because of what happened at a spring on the mountain above.

The temple's own founding account says that in 874 the monk Shobo, known after his death as Rigen Daishi, was led to a sacred spring by a manifestation of the local deity Yokoo Daimyojin. He built a small hall there housing two Kannon images, Juntei and Nyoirin. The temple's archive feature adds the scene. He had been praying for a place to establish esoteric teaching, and saw five-coloured cloud rising to the south-east of the capital. On the summit, by the water, an old white-haired man was drinking. He said the water had the taste of daigo, and vanished.

⇒ ⚑ The spring was named after that taste, the temple took the spring's name, and the word itself means "the best thing you have ever tasted." The spring is still running and you can drink from it. It is on the summit of Kami-Daigo, at the top of the climb, so you reach it only if the temple has lifted its closure of the mountain path.

Daigo-ji official founding account (Japanese) for 874, Yokoo Daimyojin and the two Kannon; Daigo-ji official archive feature (Japanese) for the five-coloured cloud and the old man at the well · checked 2026-08-15

Two National Treasures, and one of them was brought here from another province

The Kondo, the main hall, was first raised by decree of Emperor Daigo in 926. The building standing there now came from somewhere else. The temple's page records that after the original burned, the present hall was moved from Yuasa in Kishu (modern Wakayama) on Hideyoshi's order, and completed in 1600 under his son Hideyori.

⇒ ⚑ The main hall of the lower precinct was moved here, just like the seven hundred cherry trees planted for the 1598 party. Hideyoshi restored this temple by importing whole objects: a hall from one province, and cherry trees from two others.

The five-storey pagoda is older than the Kondo and has never burned, which is why the lower precinct matters historically. Emperor Suzaku ordered it in 936 as a memorial to his father, Emperor Daigo, and it was finished in 951, under Emperor Murakami. It stands about 38 metres tall, of which roughly 13 metres is the metal spire. It is the oldest wooden building in Kyoto Prefecture, older than anything in the city centre, because the city centre burned again and again and the pagoda did not.

Daigo-ji official grounds page for the Garan (Japanese), for the Kondo's relocation from Yuasa, the 926 decree, the 1600 completion, and the pagoda's 936 commission, 951 completion, height and spire · checked 2026-08-15

Inside the pagoda is the beginning of a painting tradition, and you were at its source on 3 November

The first storey of the pagoda is painted inside. The temple's page names what is on the walls: the mandalas of the two realms, and the eight patriarchs of the Shingon lineage. Because the building dates from 951 and has never burned, those paintings are among the oldest surviving esoteric Buddhist paintings in Japan, and are treated as a source for all later ones.

⇒ 🔴 On 3 November you stood at Jingo-ji, where this lineage was passed into Japan: Kukai performed his first state rite there in 810, and Saicho received initiation from him there in 812. The eight patriarchs painted on these walls are the line of teachers that this teaching came down through. So the two days connect: Jingo-ji is where the teaching arrived, and this pagoda holds the oldest surviving picture of the people who carried it.

Daigo-ji official grounds page for the Garan (Japanese), naming the two-realm mandalas and the eight patriarchs in the first storey · checked 2026-08-15

Burned in a civil war, rebuilt for a party

The temple's own history is blunt about the gap in the middle: the Onin War of 1467–1477 destroyed most of the temple. What stands now is largely the work of Gien, the abbot who rebuilt the temple in the Momoyama period with Hideyoshi's money, and the 1598 flower-viewing was the public face of that restoration. The West Gate you walk through was rebuilt in 1605, the Soshi-do in the same year, and the Kondo, as described above, in 1600.

⇒ The pagoda is the exception: it survived the war, which is why it is so important.

Daigo-ji official history page (Japanese) for the Onin destruction and the Gien–Hideyoshi restoration; official grounds page for the 1605 gate and hall dates · checked 2026-08-15

The Reihokan treasure house, and why your visit is capped at 45 minutes

The Reihokan treasure house is a major museum in its own right. The temple states it holds over 75,000 designated National Treasures and Important Cultural Properties, and something over 100,000 items counting the undesignated. It was planned in 1930 for the 1,100-year memorial of Emperor Daigo, opened in 1935, gained three new storehouses in 1979, and in 2001 gained a large exhibition room built specifically to show the National Treasure Yakushi triad, the Heian sculpture that was brought down from the mountain. The Important Cultural Property Five Wisdom Kings were brought down from Kami-Daigo in 2014.

⚑ The day plan allows about three quarters of an hour here. The collection is far too large to see in that time, so decide what you want to see before you go in.

Daigo-ji official Reihokan page (Japanese) for the holdings count, the 1930/1935/1979/2001 chronology, the Yakushi triad room and the 2014 transfer · checked 2026-08-15

Where the autumn colour is, named by the temple itself

The temple publishes its own guide to where the autumn colour is, and it names exact spots. Two of them are in this precinct:

The approach past the Nio-mon — the temple calls it a tunnel of maples over the path in.

The Rinsen: the whole area around the pond where the Kannon-do stands, just before the ground starts rising toward Kami-Daigo. In the temple's own words, it is the most brilliant autumn spot in the lower precinct.

⚑ The Kannon-do on this pond is now where the Juntei Kannon from the burned mountain hall is kept. The temple's annual-events page states that the yearly unveiling of the Juntei Kannon, the principal image of the eleventh Saigoku pilgrimage station, is held at the Kannon-do in the lower precinct for a week centred on 18 May, because the hall that held it on the mountain burned down in 2008.

⇒ The prettiest corner of the lower temple now hosts a ceremony that used to be held at the top of the mountain.

Daigo-ji official annual-events page, "the maple-hunt Hideyoshi never had" (Japanese), for the maple tunnel and the Rinsen; Daigo-ji official annual-events page for the Juntei Kannon unveiling (Japanese), which names the lower Kannon-do and the 2008 fire · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The precinct is flat, gravelled, wooded and long: it runs a good way back from the gate toward the mountain. Six things to look for:

1. Stand where the Kondo and the pagoda are both in view. They are close together in the same open ground. ⇒ One is a hall in late-Heian style, moved here from Wakayama on Hideyoshi's order and completed in 1600; the other has stood on that spot since 951. The pagoda stood on this lawn for about 650 years before the Kondo arrived.

2. On the pagoda, look at the top third. Roughly 13 of its 38 metres is the spire alone, so a third of the height is metal. That proportion is what makes the pagoda look so tall.

3. At the pagoda's base, look for the doors of the first storey. The painted interior, with the two-realm mandalas and the eight patriarchs, is behind them. ⚠ The inside is not normally open, so you will know what is in there without seeing it.

4. In the Reihokan, find the Yakushi triad and go to it first. A whole room was built in 2001 for that one group. ⇒ By building a room for it, the temple shows that it ranks the triad first in its collection.

5. At the Rinsen, stop at the pond instead of walking straight past. The Kannon-do on the water and the maples around it are the temple's own choice for its best autumn corner, and the pond is also the last flat ground before the path up to Kami-Daigo. You climb that path only if the temple has reopened it.

6. Look for where the flat ground ends. The lower precinct runs back until the hill starts. That line is where the two halves of the temple meet. Everything you have seen so far was built by emperors and warlords; everything above it is the part a monk built first.

Daigo-ji official grounds and Reihokan pages (Japanese); official annual-events page for the Rinsen; English Explore page · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official Garan page (Japanese) Official history page (Japanese) Official page on the autumn colour (Japanese) Official page on the Juntei Kannon unveiling (Japanese) Wikipedia
Kami-Daigo上醍醐
The upper temple · the mountain above the lower precinct · founded 874 · this is the original Daigo-ji
▶
⚠ Read this first — the mountain may be shut

Daigo-ji's Japanese front page carries a standing notice: because of a danger of landslides on part of the mountain trail, entry to Kami-Daigo is prohibited for the time being. It was still there on 15 August 2026, and it has been carried continuously since at least late June.

🔴 The temple's English page says the opposite. Its Explore page reads: "After a 60 minutes' walk on the mountain trail, you can access the Kami Daigo, the upper part of the temple where the Daigoji was originally founded in 874." ⇒ This entry follows the Japanese front-page notice, because it is newer, more specific, and on the page the temple keeps up to date. The English text reads like an old description that has not been revised.

⚑ The temple's site still prints the climbing schedule below the prohibition notice, so printed hours do not mean the mountain is open. On 9 August you decided to keep the day as planned and find out at the gate. The rest of this entry assumes the mountain is open.

Daigo-ji official Japanese front page, prohibition notice, read 2026-08-15; Daigo-ji official English Explore page, read 2026-08-15 · the conflict is between two pages of the same official site and is reported rather than resolved

What it is — and it is not an annexe

Kami-Daigo is where the temple began; everything below the hill came later. Daigo-ji's own English page says it plainly: this is "the upper part of the temple where the Daigoji was originally founded in 874." The Japanese page for the mountain describes it as the retreat Shobo built on the summit, and dates the temple from its opening in 874.

⇒ 🔴 The climb takes you from the temple's later expansion, paid for by emperors and warlords, up to the hut it started as. The National Treasures at the bottom were built by an emperor fifty years after a monk came up here alone.

Daigo-ji official English Explore page and official Japanese Kami-Daigo page · checked 2026-08-15

The spring the whole temple is named after is still running

The founding story told in the lower-precinct entry ends here, at the spring itself. The mountain page says the spring was found by Shobo's spiritual power, and adds that you can still drink this sacred water today.

⚑ Very few great temples have an origin legend you can drink. The word an old man used for the taste of this water became the name of the temple, and it is one of the largest temples in Japan.

Daigo-ji official Japanese Kami-Daigo page for the spring and for its still being drinkable; official founding account for the legend · checked 2026-08-15

What stands up there

The upper precinct holds two National Treasures and more:

The Yakushi-do, a National Treasure, was founded in 913 as a votive hall of Emperor Daigo, and the temple notes it has stood through more than nine hundred years of weather. ⚠ Its National Treasure triad is no longer in it.
