The images were moved down to the Reihokan in October 2001 when that building reopened, and a new Yakushi triad was carved and installed here in April 2012. ⇒ The hall is up here, its original statues are down in the Reihokan, and the figures in the hall now are fourteen years old.

The Kiyotaki-gu haiden, a National Treasure, is a Muromachi-period building constructed kakezukuri: built out on posts over the sloping ground, the same technique used for stage-type halls.

The Godai-do was also founded in 913 by Emperor Daigo, as a hall for rites to protect the state. It burned repeatedly, and the present building dates from 1940. The temple says its bulk on the ridge still gives the feeling of a mountain esoteric temple entrusted with guarding the country.

The Nyoirin-do is an Important Cultural Property. 🔴 This is one of the first two buildings ever put up on the mountain. The temple states it was raised together with the Juntei-do when Shobo opened the mountain, and that the temple's own founding record dates this to 876. The present building is from the early 1600s.

The Kaisan-do, an Important Cultural Property, is the founder's hall, and holds Shobo himself. It began in 911, built by his disciple Kangen, the temple's first head. It burned, was rebuilt in the Kamakura period and fell into ruin, and the present hall was put up in 1606 by Toyotomi Hideyori. In the temple's own words it is the largest hall on the mountain (「山上最大のお堂」). ⇒ Hideyoshi's son rebuilt the hall of the man who found the spring, so the Toyotomi restoration reached all the way up the mountain.

⚠ The temple's own page contradicts itself in two places. Its overview line credits Hideyoshi with rebuilding both the Kaisan-do and the Nyoirin-do in 1606, while the Kaisan-do's own paragraph credits Hideyori; this entry follows the detailed paragraph. And for the Nyoirin-do the page gives an era year and a western year that do not match, while for the Kaisan-do the same era year is matched correctly. ⇒ The rebuild in the early 1600s is certain, but the exact year for the Nyoirin-do is not, and this entry leaves it open.

Daigo-ji official Japanese Kami-Daigo page, per-building designations and dates, read as raw page text · both internal inconsistencies are on that page and are reported, not smoothed · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 Hideyoshi climbed this, with Ieyasu, a year before the party

The temple records, from Gien's diary, that in 1597, one year before the flower-viewing, Toyotomi Hideyoshi and Tokugawa Ieyasu climbed up to Kami-Daigo together to inspect the place in advance.

⇒ ⚑ The two men who between them decided the next three hundred years of Japanese history walked up this path together, and one of them had only a year to live. The climb is recorded because Gien, the abbot who was about to host the party, wrote down who came to inspect it.

And the temple names what they would have seen from the top: the city of Osaka and Osaka Castle laid out below, and — with luck — the Akashi-Kaikyo bridge. ⚠ The bridge is a modern addition to that view; the city and the castle are not.

Daigo-ji official Japanese Kami-Daigo page, citing the diary of Gien for the 1597 ascent, and for the summit view · checked 2026-08-15

The other founding hall burned down — and a new image for it is being carved right now

Of the two Kannon halls Shobo established, only one is still standing. The temple's own notice records what happened to the other. In the early hours of 24 August 2008, the Juntei Kannon hall burned to the ground. The fire followed a thunderclap at 10:56 the previous night, which the temple describes as exceptionally loud and suggestive of a lightning strike. No nationally designated property was lost, but the hall itself burned completely. Its principal image, the Juntei Kannon, was put on show in the lower Kondo the next year.

The Juntei Kannon hall was the eleventh station of the Saigoku thirty-three Kannon pilgrimage, one of the oldest pilgrimage circuits in the country. The temple's 2008 notice moved worship straight away to the Nyonin-do at the foot of the climb. Its annual-events page now places the yearly unveiling at the Kannon-do in the lower precinct, on the pond you pass just before the trailhead.

🔴 The temple plans to rebuild the hall. Its annual-events page states, of the burned hall: "work toward its restoration is currently in progress." Its news announcement of the 2025 unveiling rite goes further. After the service in the middle of the unveiling week, on 18 May 2025, a first-chisel ceremony was held for a newly carved Juntei Kannon, cut by the sculptor Noda Kodo of the Yase image workshop in Kyoto. The announcement says it will be the principal image when the Kami-Daigo Juntei-do is rebuilt. People attending were invited to take a turn with the chisel themselves.

⇒ ⚑ The statue is being made before the building it belongs in. ⚠ The temple says only that restoration work is under way and that the new image is meant for the rebuilt hall. It does not say whether the hall is finished, or when it will be. These pages do not show what is standing on the site now.

⚑ The Nyonin-do, where you register to climb, was standing in for the summit's Juntei hall for centuries before the fire. Its name means "the women's hall," because women were not permitted up the mountain and worshipped the summit's buddhas from there. Its own principal image is a divided image of the Juntei Kannon from the top. ⇒ For as long as there has been a climb, the Nyonin-do has served people who could not make it, and since 2008 it has also served people who can.

Daigo-ji official notice of 1 September 2008 (Japanese) for the fire, the thunderclap, the loss and the immediate relocation; Daigo-ji official news announcement of the Juntei Kannon unveiling rite (Japanese) for the 2025 first-chisel ceremony, the sculptor and the stated purpose; Daigo-ji official Kami-Daigo page for the Nyonin-do's name, its history and its divided image · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

A sustained climb on stone steps, out and back on foot, with the halls at the top. It is both the day's hike and the day's temple. ⚠ Only if the mountain is open (see the notice at the top of this entry). Six things to look for:

1. At the Nyonin-do, before you start, look at everything this small building does. It is the registration point for the climb; the hall from which women worshipped the summit when they were not allowed up; the keeper of a divided image of the Juntei Kannon; and, since 2008, the stand-in for the summit hall that held the original. Four jobs in one building at the bottom of the path.

2. Drink at the spring. The temple says the water can still be drunk. ⇒ This spring is where the temple's name comes from, and it is the only sight on the trip you can drink.

3. At the Kiyotaki-gu haiden, walk to the downhill side and look underneath. The hall is kakezukuri: it stands on posts over the slope. You can only see how the building is held up from below it.

4. At the Yakushi-do, remember that the statues that made it famous are no longer inside. Its National Treasure triad went down to the Reihokan in 2001; the figures inside now were carved in 2012. ⇒ You will have seen the originals earlier the same day, in the Reihokan at the bottom of the hill.

5. Look for the site of the Juntei-do, without knowing what you will find there. The eleventh Saigoku station burned in one night in 2008; the temple says restoration work is in progress and a replacement image is being carved for it. ⚠ The temple's pages do not say what stands there now: bare ground, a building site, or a new hall. Whichever it is, you will be seeing the site eighteen years after the fire.

6. At the Kaisan-do, put the day together. It holds Shobo, who came up here in 874. The building around him was raised in 1606 by Hideyori, whose father climbed this same path in 1597 and designed the Sanbo-in garden you saw this morning. Both ends of the temple's history are in this one hall, and you walked between them today.

Daigo-ji official Japanese Kami-Daigo page (the halls, the spring, the kakezukuri construction, the Nyonin-do, the Yakushi triad transfer) and official notices (the Juntei hall and the new image) · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official Kami-Daigo page (Japanese) Official notice on the 2008 fire (Japanese) Official page on the Juntei Kannon and the rebuild (Japanese) Official notices index (Japanese) Official founding account (Japanese) Official grounds overview (English)
Tofuku-ji東福寺
Rinzai Zen head temple · Higashiyama · built 1236–1255 for a regent · a National Treasure gate and a 1939 garden
▶
What it is

The regent Kujō Michiie set out to build Kyoto's largest monastery and measured it against the two biggest temples of Nara: he took the "Tō" of Tōdai-ji and the "Fuku" of Kōfuku-ji for its name. Building ran for nineteen years, from 1236 to 1255. In 1243, about half-way through, Enni Ben'en (Shōichi Kokushi) was made founding abbot. The first halls served Tendai, Shingon and Zen together. The temple became purely Zen only after the fires of 1319, 1334 and 1336 and the rebuilding that followed, and by then it already ranked among the Five Mountains of Kyoto.

⚑ The temple's own account describes the medieval main hall: it held a 15-metre Shakyamuni with 7.5-metre attendants, talked of as "the new Great Buddha", and it was protected in turn by Yoshimochi, Hideyoshi and Ieyasu. The whole complex reached the Meiji era without ever being burnt in war.

🔴 Then in December 1881 the Buddha hall, the lecture hall, the abbot's hall and the kitchen burned. The present main hall was begun in 1917 and finished in 1934; the abbot's hall was rebuilt in 1890, and the kitchen in 1910. ⇒ What you see is a mix of two periods: medieval Zen buildings that escaped the fire, and Shōwa rebuilding beside them.

Tōfuku-ji official history page (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-09-04

🔴 What survived the fire, and what it is designated

The Sanmon is a National Treasure. It is an early-Muromachi rebuilding in a style the temple compares to Tōdai-ji's "Indian" manner. Its plaque is in the hand of the shogun Ashikaga Yoshimochi, and its upper storey was painted by Minchō and his pupils. You see it from the outside only.

The Zen hall, rebuilt in 1347, is an Important Cultural Property, and the temple calls it the oldest and largest surviving medieval meditation hall in Japan. The Tōsu beside it, the "hundred latrines", is an Important Cultural Property of the early Muromachi period and the oldest of its kind in the country. The temple notes that even the latrine was a place of practice, with its own strict etiquette. The bath house (tiles dated 1459), the Gekka-mon of 1268, the Rokuhara-mon, the thirteen-storey stone pagoda of 1343 and the Engetsu-kyō bridge of 1603 are all Important Cultural Properties.

⚑ In March 2026 the temple announced that Minchō's Five Hundred Arhats paintings had been designated a National Treasure.

Tōfuku-ji official visiting guide, precinct map section, and the news item of 27 March 2026 (Japanese) · checked 2026-09-04

The bridge and the founder's hall

The Tsūten-kyō is a roofed bridge-corridor that runs from the main hall across the Sengyokukan ravine to the founder's hall. It was first built in 1380 by Shun'oku Myōha. The maples below it are the temple's autumn view. ⚠ The temple's own hours page gives its autumn-viewing period as 14 November to 6 December, so your visit on 4 November comes before it.

The Kaisan-dō stands at the highest point of the precinct, beyond the bridge. Its inner shrine holds the image of the founder Enni, who lived in the neighbouring Fumon-ji while he watched the temple being built, and who died here in 1280 aged seventy-nine. The interior is not open. The pavilion above it holds a seated Hotei that the temple calls the origin of the Fushimi clay dolls.

Tōfuku-ji official pages for the Tsūten-kyō and the Kaisan-dō (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-09-04

🔴 The abbot's-hall gardens — the temple made him re-use everything

Four gardens surround the four sides of the Hōjō, the abbot's hall. Shigemori Mirei laid them out between 27 June and 11 November 1939, straight after he had finished measuring every old garden in Japan for his twenty-six-volume garden history. The temple says it is the only Zen abbot's hall with gardens on all four sides, and the set was registered as a national Place of Scenic Beauty in 2014.

🔴 The temple set Shigemori one condition: nothing on the site could be thrown away, and every stone had to be used again. The temple presents this as the Zen rule against waste, and as the reason the design turned out as it did. ⇒ The checkerboard on the north side is the old paving from the path between the imperial-messenger gate and the hall, re-laid as squares of stone and moss that thin out and disappear towards the ravine. The west side is a larger checkerboard based on the ancient well-field pattern. The east side shows the Plough. The south side has four rock islands of the Immortals, with moss hills for the Five Mountains of Kyoto and Mount Sumeru. There are eight motifs in all, so the temple named the gardens Hassō no niwa, after the eight phases of the Buddha's life.

⚑ The temple adds that Shigemori's first sketches had the layout reversed. Two old pines he wanted to move stayed where they were, because the monks refused the cost and the risk of killing them.

Tōfuku-ji official Hōjō garden page (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-09-04

What to look at

1. The Sanmon, the main gate, from in front. The temple's own guide to the gate says it is said to be the oldest, largest and finest Zen main gate in Japan. The present gate was rebuilt soon after the fourteenth-century fires and finished in 1425. It is a two-storey gate, five bays wide with three doorways, and a little over 22 metres to the ridge of the roof. It was designated a National Treasure in 1952. From 1969 to 1978 the Agency for Cultural Affairs took it apart completely and put it back together, the first full repair in about six hundred years. Upstairs, by the temple's account, sit a crowned Shakyamuni and the sixteen arhats, said to date from the early Muromachi period.

2. In the east garden of the Hōjō, count the seven stone columns. They stand in sand and moss as the stars of the Plough (the Big Dipper). The temple's garden page says they are foundation stones from the Tōsu, the medieval latrine building, left over when it was taken apart for repair. The temple's chief steward asked Shigemori to use them, because nothing was to be wasted. ⚑ The temple calls this the first constellation ever shown in a Japanese garden.

3. On the north side, follow the checkerboard until it disappears. The squares are regular next to the west garden, then break up, and end as single stones set one at a time toward the ravine. The temple notes that those last stones were first laid in white sand. Moss has since grown over the sand, because the stream in the ravine beside the garden keeps the air damp.

4. In the south garden, look at the long stones lying flat. Three of the four rock islands use stones about 6 metres long laid on their sides, to balance the tall upright stones. The temple says this way of using stone has almost no precedent in older gardens.

5. A count that Shigemori never wrote down. The seven columns in the east garden, the five moss hills in the south garden, and a group of three stones in the south-west corner of the west garden make seven, five, three (shichi-go-san). The temple points out that Shigemori left no written note of this, so it is only noticed by looking at the gardens and the plans.

6. From the Tsūten-kyō bridge, look down at the maples. The temple's guide says the rare three-leaved maples there, which turn golden, are said to have been brought from Song China by the founder Enni.

Tōfuku-ji official Sanmon guide (Japanese), Hōjō garden page (Japanese) and visiting guide (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official visiting guide (Japanese) Official history (Japanese) Official Hōjō garden page (Japanese) Official notices (Japanese)
Fushimi Inari Taisha伏見稲荷大社
Head shrine of Inari · Fushimi · enshrined 711 · a whole mountain of torii, open day and night
▶
What it is

Fushimi Inari is the head shrine of the roughly 30,000 Inari shrines in Japan. By the shrine's own account the deity was enshrined on Inari-yama on the first horse day of the second month of 711, and the 1,300th anniversary was celebrated in 2011. The oldest record, a fragment of the Yamashiro fudoki, tells the founding as a story. A member of the Hata clan, rich in rice, used a rice cake as an archery target. The cake turned into a white bird and flew to the peak, rice grew where it landed, and a shrine was built there.

⚑ The shrine's grounds are the whole mountain. Inari-yama is the southernmost of the thirty-six Higashiyama peaks, and the shrine puts its grounds at about 870,000 square metres including the western foot, which it compares to twenty-two baseball stadiums. The deity's rank rose from junior fifth grade in 827 to senior first rank in 942.

Fushimi Inari official "about" page, founding-legend page and FAQ (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-09-04

🔴 The torii, and why there are ten thousand of them

The shrine's own explanation: a torii is offered in thanks for a prayer that "passed". In Japanese, the word for a wish being granted and for passing through a gate is the same. The custom spread from the Edo period, and about 10,000 torii now stand along the mountain paths. The shrine adds that vermilion is a colour that wards off evil, and here it is specifically the colour of the deity's abundance. The pigment is mercury red, which was used in the past to preserve timber.

⚠ The Senbon Torii near the foot is the famous stretch, but the count of 10,000 is for the whole mountain.

Fushimi Inari official FAQ (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-09-04

🔴 The mountain burned in 1468, and the peaks were never rebuilt

On 15 March 1468, during the Ōnin war, a force on the Hosokawa side took the mountain as a stronghold overlooking the roads south of the city. Six days later the Yamana side stormed it, and the shrine's halls and priests' houses burned from about seven in the morning until past noon. Almost every earlier record was lost in the fire.

A temporary hall stood by December, and the festival was held the next year among the ruins. The rebuilt main hall, the present one and an Important Cultural Property, was dedicated on 23 November 1499. Its cost was raised by a nationwide subscription, and the shrine still has the record of it. ⇒ 🔴 The upper and middle shrines on the peaks were never rebuilt. They survive as shinseki, sacred sites where a shrine once stood. The crowds of small stone shrines around them, the otsuka, are offerings by individual believers, each carved with the name under which that believer worships the deity. The custom took off after the seven sacred sites were fixed in the Meiji era.

Fushimi Inari official history pages on the Ōnin war and the rebuilding of the main hall, and the FAQ (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-09-04

The loop

The shrine lists the peaks as San-no-mine, Ni-no-mine and Ichi-no-mine, with Ai-no-mine between them and Kōjin-ga-mine to the north. Walking round them to worship at the sacred sites is called o-yama-suru, and the shrine says pilgrims do it day and night. Kyoto City's official tourism register gives no closing time and no closing day for the grounds (worship is possible day and night), and suggests a visit of 120 minutes including the loop. The walk is about four kilometres, up to the 233-metre summit. ⚠ The shrine's rules: eat only in the designated rest areas and not while walking, and stay out of the fenced buildings.

Fushimi Inari official mountain-map page and visitor-rules page (Japanese); Kyoto City official tourism register entry · checked 2026-09-04

What to look at

1. The Senbon Torii, the "thousand torii". The shrine's map page says the custom of believers offering torii along the path to the Okusha, in prayer and in thanks, began in the Edo period, and that this is what made the Senbon Torii famous. The vermilion paint is called Inari-nuri. The shrine explains that the word for this red, ake, carries the feeling of brightness and hope.

2. The Okusha Hōhaisho, at the end of the Senbon Torii. Most people call it the Oku-no-in. It stands in a valley called Myōbu-dani and is a place to worship the mountain from a distance: the three peaks stand directly behind it. The shrine's record of the 1499 rebuilding already mentions it. The present building went up after it was damaged in 1794 (Kansei 6), and in 1975 it was moved further back and a worship space was added in front.

3. The Omokaru-ishi, behind the Okusha on the right. These are a pair of stone lanterns. You make a wish in front of them and lift the round stone on top of a lantern. ⚑ By the shrine's tradition, if it feels lighter than you expected the wish will come true, and if it feels heavier it is unlikely to.

4. The foxes. The shrine's FAQ says the fox is the messenger of the Inari deity. These messengers are invisible, like the deity, so they are revered as white, or transparent, foxes, called byakko-san. ⚑ The shrine adds that the deity itself is not a fox.

5. The Mitsurugi-sha, the sword shrine. An old map of the mountain marks this sacred site as the sword stone, or thunder stone. To its left is a well called Yakiba-no-mizu, "the water for tempering blades". A Noh play tells how the swordsmith Sanjō Munechika, ordered by the emperor to make a sword, forged the famous blade Kogitsunemaru, "little fox", on this mountain with the Inari deity's help. The shrine says this spot calls that scene to mind.

6. The peaks. Ichi-no-mine is the summit, at 233 metres above sea level. Each sacred site is worshipped under its own name: Ichi-no-mine as Suehiro Ōkami, Ni-no-mine as Aoki Ōkami and San-no-mine as Shiragiku Ōkami. At Ai-no-mine, the stone torii at the entrance is an unusual kind called a nune torii, with gable-shaped struts on each side of the central plaque. A mirror was dug up at San-no-mine in the early 1890s and is now shown at the Kyoto National Museum.

7. The view from Kōjin-ga-mine. The shrine's map says that if you walk round behind the sacred site on Kōjin-ga-mine, the view opens out. From there you can see central and northern Kyoto, which cannot be seen from the Yotsutsuji crossroads.

Fushimi Inari official mountain-map page and FAQ (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (Japanese) Official mountain map (Japanese) Official FAQ (Japanese) Kyoto City tourism register entry (Japanese)

Thursday 5 November — the Yoshino ridge

Nara Prefecture · the birthplace of mountain asceticism, a rebel emperor's capital-in-exile, and three hidden buddhas that are open on the day you arrive
Kinpusen-ji金峯山寺
Head temple of Kinpusen Shugen · Yoshino · where Shugendo began · World Heritage
▶
🔴 The three hidden buddhas are open on the day you are there

The temple's main images are three colossal Kongo Zao Gongen statues. They are hibutsu, hidden images that are normally sealed and not shown at all, so being here while they are on view is pure luck. The temple posted its autumn schedule on 10 June 2026:

The special unveiling runs 24 October to 30 November 2026. ⇒ 🔴 You are there on 5 November, in the middle of the window.

The same notice gives the reason for the unveiling: it raises funds for the major repair of the Nio-mon gate, which is itself a National Treasure. ⇒ ⚑ The temple is paying for its buildings by putting the hidden statues on show.

Kinpusen-ji official notice "Autumn 2026 special unveiling of the hidden principal images," posted 2026-06-10, dates read from the page's own text · checked 2026-08-15

What it is: where Shugendo is said to have begun

Kinpusen-ji is the head temple of its own school, and it claims to be the place where Shugendo, Japanese mountain asceticism, started. The founder is En no Gyoja, and the temple's founding story describes one specific event.

After opening sacred mountains across the country, En no Gyoja came into the Yoshino and Omine ranges and finally undertook a thousand days of unbroken practice on the summit of Sanjogatake. He asked for a form of the Buddha that could save people living in real suffering. First Shakyamuni, the Thousand-Armed Kannon and Miroku appeared, in their gentle forms. He judged that those forms could not reach violent and desperate people, and kept praying.

🔴 Then, in the temple's own words: heaven and earth roared, the great bedrock of the summit split apart, and with the thunder there welled up a buddha in a form of raging wrath. That buddha is Zao Gongen: the three gentle buddhas had thrown away their gentleness and come back angry.

The temple explains the statue's colour and its anger together. The whole body is blue-black, and the blackness is said to represent compassion and tolerance. The temple's word for this anger is jo, which it describes as anger that forgives everything all the same. The temple compares it to the anger of a loving parent.

Kinpusen-ji official Japanese site, the temple's account of its own founding and of its principal image, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 The cherry trees are here because the statue is made of cherry

Nara Prefecture puts the number of cherry trees on the mountain at about 30,000, mostly wild shiroyama-zakura. They grow in four zones that flower one after another going up the slope. The trees are here for a religious reason. The temple's founding story, repeated on three separate pages of its site, says that En no Gyoja carved the form he had seen into the wood of a mountain cherry, and enshrined it at the summit and at the foot of the mountain.

⇒ ⚑ Because the god's statue was carved from mountain cherry, the mountain cherry became a sacred tree, and people have planted cherries here as offerings ever since. The temple's pages state that the statue was carved from cherry wood. They do not spell out the link to the planting, so that link is given here as the usual tradition; the temple itself does not claim it.

⚠ You are here in November, so there will be no blossom. What you can still see is a hillside of trees planted as offerings, seen out of season.

Kinpusen-ji official Japanese site, founding account, the mountain-cherry carving stated on the school-history, principal-image and founder pages · the link to the mass plantings is flagged as tradition, not a temple claim · checked 2026-08-15. The tree count, the species and the four zones are Nara Prefecture's figures (pref.nara.lg.jp), and the prefecture hedges the number itself — "said to be about 30,000" · checked 2026-08-22

The hall, and its two links to Todai-ji in Nara

The Zao-do is a National Treasure and the second-largest pre-modern wooden building in Japan, after the Great Buddha Hall at Todai-ji. ⇒ 🔴 You stood in the Great Buddha Hall, the largest, on 31 October.

Inside are 68 pillars, none of them milled. They are natural tree trunks of pine, cedar, pear and cypress mixed together, left in their own shapes instead of being squared to match. ⇒ ⚑ In a National Treasure this large, leaving the trunks in their natural shapes was a deliberate choice. The present hall dates from 1592.

🔴 The second link to Todai-ji is a gate. About two hundred metres below the temple stands the Kane-no-Torii, the bronze gate. It is the first of four gates marking stages of ascetic progress on the way up to Omine. Legend says it was cast from the bronze left over from the Great Buddha at Todai-ji. ⚠ The temple presents this as a legend only.

Kinpusen-ji official site (the Zao-do's rank, the 68 unmilled pillars, the 1592 date, the bronze torii and its legend); Yoshino Town's official World Heritage constituent-assets page (corroborating dimensions and dating) · checked 2026-08-15

⚠ The gate is under repair, and its guardians have left the mountain

The Nio-mon, a National Treasure, is undergoing a full dismantling repair. Its two Kongo Rikishi guardians have been moved to the Nara National Museum. The statues are 5.3 metres tall, Important Cultural Properties, and dated 1338 and 1339. The temple states this is the first time they have ever left Yoshino.

⇒ ⚑ You were at the Nara National Museum on 31 October for the Shoso-in exhibition. The temple's page does not say whether the guardians were on view then, and the museum's autumn programme is mostly given over to Shoso-in, so do not assume you saw them.

⚠ The temple gives two different dates for the gate's return. Its dated Japanese repair notice gives a target of the 2028 financial year; its English page says the gate "may be covered until 2030." Both pages are the temple's own. This entry follows the dated Japanese notice, which is the more specific. Either way, expect scaffolding on the gate.

Kinpusen-ji official Japanese repair notice (2021, with the FY2028 target and the statues' move) and official English page (the 2030 figure) · the conflict is between two pages of the temple's own site and is reported, not resolved · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

A large hall at the top of the village street, with a gate below it and a bronze torii below the gate. ⚠ The gate is covered in scaffolding. Five things to look at:

1. Go in for the unveiling of the hidden statues; it is the main reason for the day. Three wrathful figures, blue-black, normally sealed, on view for five weeks this autumn. ⇒ The temple reads the black colour as mercy, so the figures are not meant as a threat.

2. Inside the hall, look at the pillars rather than the altar. Sixty-eight of them, four species, none of them squared. ⇒ Find two next to each other that are obviously different trees.

3. The four cherry trees in front of the Zao-do. Temple tradition holds that Prince Morinaga held his last banquet on this spot in 1333, before his palace fell to an army of sixty thousand. ⇒ The four trees mark the last party of a man who knew he had lost.

4. The standing statue of En no Gyoja. Unusually he is shown upright and looking ahead rather than seated on a rock in the conventional pose. ⇒ The founder of Japanese mountain religion, depicted as someone about to walk somewhere.

5. Walk down to the bronze torii and stand under it. It is the first of four gates — resolve, training, awakening, enlightenment — marking a pilgrim's stages toward Omine. ⇒ You will pass the second one later today, at Kinpu Shrine at the top of the ridge.

Kinpusen-ji official Japanese and English pages (the unveiling, the pillars, the four cherry trees and Prince Morinaga, the statue's posture, the four gates) · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official history and principal image (Japanese) Official notice of the autumn 2026 unveiling (Japanese) UNESCO listing for the Kii mountain sites
Yoshimizu Shrine吉水神社
Shrine, formerly a Shugendo lodging · Yoshino · a fugitive, an exiled emperor and a warlord's party, in one building
▶
What it is

Yoshimizu Shrine was first built as Yoshimizu-in, the residence of a senior Shugendo monk attached to Kinpusen-ji. It became a Shinto shrine only in 1875, when the Meiji government forcibly separated Buddhism from Shinto. The building was made a shrine because of what had happened inside it.

The three enshrined here are Emperor Go-Daigo, Kusunoki Masashige, and the priest who sheltered them. ⇒ The shrine's gods are the losing side of a fourteenth-century civil war.

Yoshimizu Shrine official site, history page · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 Three separate people hid, ruled or celebrated in these rooms

Few places hold three unrelated turning points in Japanese history as clearly as this building. Here is the shrine's own account of each:

1. 1185 — Yoshitsune hid here. Minamoto no Yoshitsune, Shizuka Gozen and Benkei concealed themselves at Yoshimizu-in while being hunted by Yoshitsune's brother Yoritomo. The room where he and Shizuka spent their last time together still exists, and the shrine says the weapons, tools and clothing he left behind when he disguised himself as a mountain ascetic and fled deeper into the range have been kept ever since. ⇒ ⚑ At Kurama on 1 November you read about him as a boy trained by tengu. Yoshimizu-in is where he hid as a fugitive, near the end of his life.

2. 1336 — an emperor made this his palace. When Go-Daigo left Kyoto for Yoshino, he made Yoshimizu-in the imperial palace of the Southern Court. The shrine's claim: the Southern Court's four generations and fifty-seven years began in this building, and it is the only surviving Southern Court palace. ⇒ 🔴 For half a century Japan had two emperors, and one of them governed from a monk's guest rooms on a mountain.

3. 1594 — Hideyoshi threw a party here. He came to Yoshino "to display his power to the whole realm," and made this his headquarters. The shrine's own detail: about five thousand retainers, staying roughly five days, with Tokugawa Ieyasu, Kato Kiyomasa, Maeda Toshiie and Date Masamune among them. He held poetry, tea and Noh here, repaired Go-Daigo's throne room, laid out the garden, and left behind Noh masks and gold screens.

Yoshimizu Shrine official site, history and cherry-blossom pages, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 Yoshino cherries at Hideyoshi's 1598 Daigo-ji party

Four years after the 1594 party here, Hideyoshi held another one, at Daigo-ji, in 1598. You stood in that garden on the morning of 4 November. Daigo-ji's own archive, citing the abbot Gien's diary for the second month of 1598, records that seven hundred cherry trees were transplanted for it from the home provinces and from Yoshino.

⇒ 🔴 Hideyoshi liked Yoshino so much that four years after his party here he had some of its cherry trees dug up and moved to Kyoto. ⚑ He was dead within five months of the Daigo-ji party. The 1594 party at Yoshimizu-in was the rehearsal; the 1598 party at Daigo-ji was the last thing he did.

⚠ Of the two sites, only Daigo-ji mentions the other. Daigo-ji names Yoshino as a source of the trees; nothing on Yoshimizu Shrine's own site mentions Daigo-ji at all.

Daigo-ji official archive feature on the 1598 flower-viewing (Japanese), citing Gien's diary for the trees' origin; Yoshimizu Shrine official site for the 1594 party · the one-directional nature of the connection is stated · checked 2026-08-15

⚠ The building's age — three official sources, and they do not agree

The shrine says its shoin is the oldest shoin-style building in Japan, from the beginning of the Kamakura period, and lists the features it dates by: an unusually wide, shallow alcove said to be the origin of the modern tokonoma; staggered shelves held without vertical posts; pillars with the widest chamfer known among surviving early shoin; wooden rather than metal nail-covers; and ceiling boards finished with a spear-plane.

🔴 The national cultural-property register disagrees by about two centuries. It describes the building as partly built in the first half of the fifteenth century and extended at the end of the sixteenth, and notes that the building itself first appears in written records in 1594 — the year of Hideyoshi's party. Yoshino Town's own pages sit in between, calling the Yoshitsune room Muromachi in style.

⇒ This entry follows the national register. It is the government record behind the building's designation, and the most precisely sourced of the three. ⚑ The architectural features the shrine lists are real and worth looking at; only the century they date from is in dispute.

Yoshimizu Shrine official site (the "oldest in Japan," early-Kamakura claim and the feature list); Agency for Cultural Affairs national designation record (first half of the 15th century, extended late 16th, first documented 1594); Yoshino Town official pages (Muromachi style) · all three are reported and the register is followed · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

A small shrine and an old house you walk through in stockinged feet, with the "Thousand at a Glance" viewpoint beside the car park. Five things to look at:

1. The three named rooms, in chronological order. Yoshitsune's hiding room · Go-Daigo's throne room · Hideyoshi's flower-viewing room. ⇒ Four hundred years of Japanese history in one corridor, and each room is furnished with things its occupants left behind.

2. In Hideyoshi's room, the two gold screens. One Bamboo by Kano Sansetsu, one Cherry Blossoms by Kano Eitoku — the shrine says both are from his own 1594 use of the room. ⇒ Painted cherry blossom, in a room he used because of the real ones outside.

3. The pillars and the shelves. Look for the wide chamfer on the pillar edges and the staggered shelf with nothing holding it up from below. ⇒ These are the features that the argument over the building's age is about.

4. Benkei's nail rock, beside the main hall. Two nail heads in the stone; the legend is that Benkei drove them in with his thumb to frighten off pursuers in the winter of 1185. The shrine adds that Hideyoshi, told the story in 1594, touched the rock and asked for strength. ⇒ ⚑ A warlord at the height of his power touching a rock for luck, because of a story about a monk four centuries earlier.

5. The "Thousand at a Glance" viewpoint by the car park. The panorama across the middle and upper cherry zones. Its nickname is "a glance is ten years" — folk belief holds that seeing it once adds ten years to your life. ⚠ In November you are looking at the shape of the mountain rather than the blossom.

Yoshimizu Shrine official site — history (the rooms), cherry-blossom page (the screens and the viewpoint), precinct guide (Benkei's nail rock and Hideyoshi's visit), World Heritage shoin page (the architectural features) · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (Japanese) National designation record for the shoin (Japanese) Yoshino Town World Heritage assets (Japanese)
Yoshino Mikumari Shrine吉野水分神社
Shrine · upper Yoshino · a water god who became a child god by mispronunciation · rebuilt 1604 by Hideyori
▶
🔴 The god changed job through a slip of the tongue

The shrine's deity is Ame-no-Mikumari, a god of the distribution of water: the god who decides which valley the rain runs down. For farmers that was an important role, and it is why a shrine stands this high up the ridge.

Over the centuries, the name mikumari was slurred into mikomori and then komori, a word that means minding a child. The national heritage record states this change in pronunciation plainly. As a result, the shrine is popularly called Komori-san, and people pray here for conception, safe birth and the protection of children.

⇒ ⚑ A water god became a god of children because of how the name came to be pronounced. The change came from pronunciation alone, without any merger of cults or religious debate.

Agency for Cultural Affairs Japan Heritage record, which states the phonetic drift explicitly, quoted and translated; Yoshino Town official page for the deities · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 The child prayed for here also rebuilt a hall at Daigo-ji

Yoshino Town's own page states it directly: Hideyoshi prayed here for a child, was granted his son Hideyori, and the buildings standing today were rebuilt in 1604 by Hideyori himself — described in the town's wording as the petitioned-for child.

⇒ 🔴 On the afternoon of 4 November, if the mountain was open, you stood in the Kaisan-do at the top of Kami-Daigo, which Toyotomi Hideyori rebuilt in 1606. ⚑ If you made the Kami-Daigo climb on 4 November, you will have seen two buildings Hideyori rebuilt two years apart, on 4 and 5 November, and this is the shrine where his father prayed for him to be born.

⚠ The claim appears in identical wording on the town, prefecture and local tourism pages. It is well attested, but all three pages copy one municipal text; they are not three independent records.

Yoshino Town official page, quoted and translated; the same wording appears on Nara Prefecture and local tourism listings and the shared derivation is noted · checked 2026-08-15

What is standing there

The buildings are Momoyama in style and built as three shrine halls under a single roof — main hall, offering hall, worship hall, a tower gate and a connecting corridor, all designated together as an Important Cultural Property.

⚠ The shrine's greatest object cannot be seen. A statue of Tamayori-hime held here is a National Treasure, and the local record marks it explicitly not open to the public, as is a second Important Cultural Property statue. ⇒ Do not expect to see either statue.

Yoshino Town official page (the structure and designations); local tourism association listing (the statues' non-public status) · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

A small shrine high on the ridge, reached on foot along the ridge road. Three things to look at:

1. The tower gate and the corridor, as one object. Gate, corridor and three halls are a single Important Cultural Property, all from the 1604 rebuild. ⇒ You are looking at a complete Momoyama shrine, paid for by Hideyori, whose birth was prayed for here.

2. The courtyard, and how enclosed it is. The corridor wraps around the courtyard instead of leaving it open to the ridge. ⇒ The courtyard feels like a room on the mountain.

3. The weeping cherry. Nara Prefecture's own listing names this shrine's shidare-zakura as a highlight in its own right. ⚠ The tree is bare in November, but it is the tree the shrine is known for, so it is worth finding.

Yoshino Town official page; Nara Prefecture tourism listing for the weeping cherry · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Yoshino Town official page (Japanese) National Japan Heritage record (Japanese)
Kinpu Shrine金峯神社
Shrine · Oku-senbon, the top of the ridge · recorded before 927 · the gate where the pilgrimage proper begins
▶
What it is, and how its age is documented

Kinpu Shrine is the furthest and quietest of the four places on this day's walk. It stands in Oku-senbon, the innermost zone of the mountain, and enshrines Kanayamahiko, described as the guardian deity of the whole of Kinpusen: the range running from Yoshino up to Sanjogatake.

The shrine's age is on record in writing. It is a shikinaisha, a shrine already listed in the Engishiki register compiled in 927. ⇒ Whatever else is uncertain, this shrine was important enough to be in a government list eleven hundred years ago.

Nara Prefecture's description adds the double reading of the god: venerated both as a deity who keeps living things from withering and as the golden deity of a gold-bearing mountain — the mountain's name means "the peak of gold."

Yoshino Town official page (the deity and the Engishiki listing); Nara Prefecture tourism page (the two readings of the god) · checked 2026-08-15

The Gate of Training: second of four gates to Omine

The ascetic route up to Omine is marked by four gates, in order: resolve · training · awakening · enlightenment. The first is the bronze torii below Kinpusen-ji, at the bottom of the village. The second, the Gate of Training, is here.

The temple's own pilgrimage itinerary lists the sequence as the Gate of Training, then Kinpu Shrine, then the old boundary marker beyond which women were not permitted.

⇒ 🔴 The Gate of Training marks a boundary. Below it, pilgrims and sightseers share the mountain. Above it, the route becomes the Omine ridge proper, and until modern times that part was closed to women by rule. ⚑ Today you walked from the first gate to the second. That walk took the whole day, and it is the shortest stretch of the route.

Yoshino Town official World Heritage assets page (the four gates in sequence); Kinpusen-ji official pilgrimage itinerary (the order of the training gate, the shrine and the old exclusion boundary) · checked 2026-08-15

Michinaga's buried sutra, and Yoshitsune's tower

🔴 Fujiwara no Michinaga climbed the Kinpusen range in 1007 and buried a sutra container at its summit. This shrine has kept the container since the Edo period. The Kyoto National Museum dates the burial to the eighth month of 1007, when Michinaga was forty-one. The burial site was the summit of Sanjōgatake (1,719 m), in the village of Tenkawa. ⚑ The container is said to have been dug out of the ground in 1691, during repairs to the main hall on that summit. The shrine still owns it, and it is kept on deposit at the Kyoto National Museum. It is a National Treasure, and the museum calls it the oldest sutra container in Japan with a dated inscription.

He had copied the Lotus Sutra and other texts in his own hand. The inscription says the Lotus Sutra was offered to repay the Buddha Shakyamuni and so that he would meet Maitreya, the Buddha of the future. Michinaga also wrote up the pilgrimage in his own diary, the Midō Kanpakuki.

⇒ ⚑ This is the same Michinaga whose villa his son turned into Byodo-in, which you visit on 6 November. The most powerful man of the Heian court went to the top of the range to bury sutras for the coming of the future Buddha. In 1052 his son turned the villa into Byodo-in, a model of paradise. This almanac draws the Byodo-in link itself; neither site's pages make it.

The second thing is a tower. Just below the shrine stands Yoshitsune's hiding tower, where he is said to have hidden from pursuers and, when surrounded, kicked his way out through the roof. Its other name is the Kick-Through Tower. ⇒ Earlier in the day, at Yoshimizu Shrine, you saw the room where he hid; here, at the end of the walk, is the roof he broke through.

Kyoto National Museum bulletin no. 37, article on the Michinaga sutra container (Japanese), for the 1007 date, the Sanjōgatake summit, the excavation, the shrine's ownership and the deposit, the inscription and the diary; Agency for Cultural Affairs record for the Kinpusen sutra-mound sutras (Japanese), corroborating the 1007 burial, the summit site, the 1691 excavation and the shrine's ownership; Yoshino Town official page (the hiding tower, quoted and translated); Nara Prefecture tourism page (corroborating the tower) · the Byodo-in connection is stated as an inference · tower checked 2026-08-15, Michinaga paragraphs checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Free and always open, with no gates and no staff, at the far end of a long ridge walk. Three things to look at:

1. The Gate of Training. Stand at it and look uphill. ⇒ For most of this shrine's existence, the ridge beyond the gate was the Omine ridge proper, closed to women.

2. Yoshitsune's hiding tower, below the shrine buildings. A small building you can find and see. ⇒ Look at the roof he is said to have kicked through.

3. The worship hall, which is not originally from here. Nara Prefecture notes it was moved to this site from the former Yoshino Jingu. ⚠ No date is given for the move. ⇒ Even at the top of the mountain, some of the buildings were moved here from elsewhere.

Yoshino Town and Nara Prefecture official pages · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Yoshino Town official page (Japanese) Kinpusen-ji pilgrimage route (Japanese)

Friday 6 November — Uji

South of Kyoto · a paradise built by people who believed the world was ending, the oldest surviving shrine building in Japan, and a Chinese temple that never became Japanese
Byodo-in平等院
Temple · Uji · converted from a villa in 1052 · World Heritage · the building on the ten-yen coin
▶
🔴 It was built because people thought the world had just ended

Everything about Byodo-in follows from one fact, and the temple states it directly. In 1052 the regent Fujiwara no Yorimichi took a villa from his father, Michinaga, and turned it into a temple. The temple's own English page explains why:

"In Buddhism, there is the Mappo 'end-of-the-world' theory and it was believed that the year 1052 marked the first year of the beginning of the end-of-the-world. This theory captured the heart of many aristocrats and monks, and, as a result, people became more devout in Buddhism and believed in the Buddhist Pure Land."

Mappo is the doctrine that the Buddha's teaching decays in stages, and that in the final stage no one can reach enlightenment by their own effort any more. By the calendar of the time, that final stage began in 1052. The hall went up the following year.

⇒ 🔴 The richest man in Japan built this in the first year of what he believed was the end of the world. It is a scale model of the paradise he thought he could no longer reach any other way.

Byodo-in official English site, history page, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15

The hall faces east, so paradise lies to the west across the pond

The hall's formal name is the Amida-do. "Phoenix Hall" is a nickname in use since around the beginning of the Edo period, because from the front the hall looks like a bird spreading its wings, and because there is a phoenix on each end of the roof.

🔴 The direction the hall faces comes from Buddhist doctrine. The temple's own wording: "Its front faces the east; thus, it implies that the Buddhist Pure Land is to the west from across the Aji-ike Pond." ⇒ You stand on the east bank and look west, across the water, at Amida. That is the position of someone meditating on rebirth in the Western Paradise, and the garden was laid out to put visitors in that position. The garden is the oldest Pure Land garden of its kind still surviving, and is designated a National Historic Site and Place of Scenic Beauty.

⚑ The temple adds one line about the time of day: when the sun sets behind the hall, the building's outline looks like the Pure Land itself.

Byodo-in official English site, architecture and garden pages, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15

Jocho's only surviving statue, by the temple's account, and the joined-block method behind it

The Amida inside was carved by Jocho and finished in 1053. The temple's claim, in its own words: Jocho is "claimed to have been the very best Buddhist sculptor in Japanese history, and this Amida Buddha is the only remaining statue fashioned by him still existing today." The statue is wood, covered in gold leaf over lacquer, and it is made using yosegi-zukuri — joined-block construction, "which is also known as the Jocho style."

⇒ 🔴 Joined-block construction shaped Japanese sculpture for the next two hundred years, and you have already seen the result. A statue built from many separate blocks can be split among many carvers working at the same time, instead of one man carving one log. On 1 November you stood in front of a thousand Kannon statues at Sanjusangen-do. After a fire, 876 of them were made by three rival workshops in sixteen years. ⚑ That was mass production, and this statue is where the method was perfected. ⚠ This almanac draws that connection: each temple states only its own facts, and neither links them.

The temple also names the statue's hand position (thumb and forefinger forming a circle, palms up on the knees) and gives its meaning: that Amida is willing to help and to give relief.

Byodo-in official English site, sculpture page, for Jocho, the sole survival, yosegi-zukuri and the hand gesture; the Sanjusangen-do figures are from this document's own entry for 1 November · the causal link between them is stated as an inference · checked 2026-08-15

Fifty-two figures on clouds, and a phoenix on the 2004-series ten-thousand-yen note

52 Bodhisattvas on Clouds were made for the upper walls of the hall, all by Jocho's studio in the same year, 1053. They were set in two numbered groups of 26, in a U shape around the Amida. Twenty-six of the originals are now in the Hōshōkan museum. Each has a halo and rides a flying cloud; five take the form of monks; some play instruments, some dance, some hold objects, some are simply praying. ⇒ Together they show a crowd at the moment of arrival: the retinue that comes to collect the dying, carved as if moving.

The phoenixes on the roof are replacements. Both originals were taken down because air pollution had rusted them. The pair on the roof now are the second generation, and the originals, which are National Treasures, are in the museum. ⇒ ⚑ The Phoenix of the South has been on the ten-thousand-yen note issued from 2004, and the hall itself has been on the ten-yen coin since 1951, the year the hall was designated a National Treasure. You have been carrying pictures of this building since you landed.

🔴 The temple bell links Byodo-in to Nara. The original bell was also removed because of rust, and it is also in the museum. It is one of three bells named in an old saying: "Byodo-in for the best figure, Onjo-ji for the best sound, Todai-ji for the best resonance." ⇒ You stood under the Todai-ji bell on 31 October.

Byodo-in official English site, sculpture page, for the 52 Bodhisattvas, the phoenixes, the currency and the three-bells saying · checked 2026-08-15

The first samurai seppuku is said to have happened here

In 1180, after Prince Mochihito called for the Taira to be destroyed, Minamoto no Yorimasa raised troops, was beaten in a battle at Uji, and killed himself inside this precinct. The temple's own careful wording: "It is said to have been the first Seppuku suicide by a samurai in Japanese history."

⚠ "It is said" is the temple's own hedge, and this almanac keeps it. The place where Yorimasa died is now the lawn called Ogi-no-Shiba, "the fan lawn," and his grave is on the grounds.

⇒ ⚑ Yorimasa's defeat here began a war. The Taira, who won the battle at Uji, were wiped out within five years, and the man who destroyed them was Yoshitsune. On 5 November, at Yoshino, you saw where he hid in 1185, when his own brother was hunting him. The paradise garden has a battlefield in it.

Byodo-in official English site, history page, quoted verbatim including the "it is said" · checked 2026-08-15

Two temples of different schools have run it jointly since 1670

Byodo-in is run by two temples of different schools. Jodo-in, of the Pure Land school, and Saisho-in, of the Tendai school, "began to cooperate and made a great effort to maintain Byodoin Temple". The temple dates that arrangement to 1670, and it has lasted ever since.

⇒ ⚑ It is unusual in Japanese Buddhism for two rival denominations to share custody of one building, and these two have done it for over three hundred and fifty years. The building survived by being jointly owned.

Byodo-in official English site, history page · checked 2026-08-15

The Hōshōkan museum

The temple's museum is called the Hōshōkan (鳳翔館). It opened on 1 March 2001, replacing an older treasure house built in 1965. The temple says it was the first comprehensive museum run by a religious organisation, and it is a registered museum of Kyoto Prefecture. The architect was Kuryū Akira. Most of the building is underground so that it fits the garden around the Phoenix Hall, and its glass display wall is one of the largest in Japan.

🔴 The museum holds the originals of three National Treasures: the temple bell, the pair of phoenixes from the hall's roof, and 26 of the 52 Bodhisattvas on Clouds. The museum also shows an Eleven-faced Kannon (an Important Cultural Property) and eaves tiles from the temple's first construction.

Byodo-in official museum page (Japanese and English), for the name, the opening date, the 1965 treasure house, the designer, the underground building and the collection · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The site is a pond, a hall you walk around, and a museum built mostly underground so that it does not spoil the view. Six things to look for:

1. Stand on the east bank first and do not walk round yet. The east bank is the viewpoint the garden was designed around: the hall, its reflection, the pond, and, in theory, paradise beyond them to the west. ⇒ The whole building was designed to be seen from this spot.

2. The round lattice window in the front wall. Amida's face can be seen through it from outside; the temple says so itself. ⇒ The window was designed so that people who could not come in could still see the Buddha, and be seen by him.

3. Up on the walls inside, find one Bodhisattva with an instrument and one dancing. There are 52 in all and they are numbered. Twenty-six of the originals are in the Hōshōkan museum. ⇒ Do not try to see them all. Pick two and look at how the carver showed a body floating in the air.

4. The suhama — the winding pebbled shoreline at the pond's edge. It was excavated and researched in 1990 and rebuilt as an exact recreation of the Heian original. ⇒ Most of what you see at Byodo-in is restored; the suhama is a restoration based on an excavation.

5. In the museum, the original phoenixes and the original bell. Both were brought indoors because air pollution outside was corroding them. ⇒ Compare the original phoenix with the one on the roof, and then with the one on the 2004-series ten-thousand-yen note.

6. Ogi-no-Shiba, the fan lawn, and Yorimasa's grave. ⇒ The quietest corner of the garden is where Yorimasa's war against the Taira began.

Byodo-in official English site — architecture (the lattice window), sculpture (the Bodhisattvas, phoenixes, bell), garden (the suhama and its 1990 excavation), history (Ogi-no-Shiba) · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official history (English) Official page on the sculpture (English) Official museum page (English)
Ujigami-jinja宇治上神社
Shrine · Uji · the oldest surviving shrine building in Japan · World Heritage
▶
What it is

Ujigami-jinja is a small shrine on the wooded slope across the river from Byodo-in. The national cultural-property record calls its main hall, without qualification, the oldest surviving example of shrine architecture in Japan. The Agency for Cultural Affairs' own entry on the main hall: "judging by the detailing, the left, middle and right halls were built in that order, roughly from the late eleventh century to the first half of the twelfth, and it is the oldest surviving structure of nagare-zukuri and of shrine architecture." The main hall was made a National Treasure in 1952 and is part of the World Heritage listing.

⚑ Compare Ujigami-jinja with Omiwa, which you visited on 30 October. Omiwa is called the oldest shrine in the country because it has no main hall at all and never had one. Ujigami-jinja makes the opposite claim, and both are true: Omiwa keeps the oldest way of worshipping, and Ujigami-jinja has the oldest building made for worship.

Agency for Cultural Affairs national cultural-properties database, entry for the Ujigami-jinja main hall (Japanese), quoted and translated; Kyoto Prefecture's World Heritage pages for the component listing · checked 2026-08-15, main-hall date re-checked 2026-10-17

🔴 The shrine exists because a prince killed himself to end a stalemate

The shrine's own site tells the founding story, and it ends in a death. Uji no Wakiiratsuko was the youngest son of Emperor Ojin and had been made heir. When his father died, he refused the throne in favour of his elder brother, the future Emperor Nintoku, and withdrew to a residence here in Uji.

Nintoku refused the throne in turn. In the shrine's telling, his reason was that their father had settled the succession and it was not his to change. ⇒ For three years each brother kept refusing the throne in favour of the other. In the shrine's words, the country fell into confusion.

🔴 Wakiiratsuko ended the deadlock by removing himself. He judged that things would only get worse while he lived, and he took his own life. Nintoku hurried from Naniwa and buried him with honour. The shrine's own sentence: in later times, this became the origin of this shrine.

⚑ The oldest surviving shrine building in Japan stands where a prince decided that his own existence was the problem. The three deities enshrined here are Wakiiratsuko himself, his father Ojin, and the brother he would not displace, so everyone in the quarrel is enshrined together.

⚠ Wakiiratsuko's tomb was lost for centuries. In 1889 a burial mound about fifteen minutes' walk away was formally identified as his.

Ujigami-jinja official site, history page (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

The main hall is a shelter over three small shrines

The main hall that holds the record is more than a single hall. The national record describes it as five bays by three, single-storeyed, in the nagare-zukuri style and roofed in cypress bark, with three inner shrines inside it, each a one-bay nagare-zukuri shrine of its own. Kyoto Prefecture's description adds a detail that explains this: of the three inner shrines, the left and right ones are large and the middle one is small.

⇒ 🔴 From outside you are looking at a shelter. The National Treasure is the three small buildings inside it, one for each deity, and the middle one, for the prince, is the smallest. The prefecture and the national record describe this arrangement, but neither says why the sizes differ, and the shrine's own site does not explain it either.

The worship hall in front is a separate National Treasure and about a century younger than the main hall. Its cypress was felled in the early Kamakura period, while the main hall's timber is late Heian. The worship hall is built in the style used for houses.

⚠ On the widely quoted dates: tree-ring dates in the 1050s–1060s are often given for the main hall. They come from a named study by the Nara research institute and the Uji city museum, but the study could not be found on either institution's own pages. This entry therefore uses what the national and prefectural records say: late Heian timber, and the late eleventh to early twelfth century judging by the construction details. Both datings agree the hall is the oldest; they differ on how precisely it can be dated.

Agency for Cultural Affairs database (the structure and the "oldest surviving" verdict); Kyoto Prefecture World Heritage page (the felling periods and the three halls' relative sizes) · ⚠ the dendrochronological year figures are NOT Tier-1-confirmed and are flagged rather than used · checked 2026-08-15, main-hall date re-checked 2026-10-17

Until the Meiji period this was half of one shrine

Until the Meiji era, Ujigami-jinja and Uji-jinja, immediately downhill, were one institution. Kyoto Prefecture's own description says the two together were called "Rikyu-Kamisha", the upper shrine of the detached palace. The nineteenth-century reorganisation of religion split them into two shrines.

⇒ Seen as a pair, the site makes sense: an upper and a lower shrine on one hillside, both tied to the memory of Wakiiratsuko's residence.

Kyoto Prefecture tourism authority, Yamashiro regional page (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Ujigami-jinja is small and quiet, and quick to visit. Five things to look for:

1. Look past the outer hall to the three shrines inside it. Find the two large ones and the small one between them. ⇒ That middle building is the prince's, and it is the smallest of the three.

2. The roofs, on both buildings. They are cypress bark laid in layers, in the nagare-zukuri style, where one slope of the roof runs long down over the front. ⇒ Nearly every Japanese shrine uses this form, and this is the oldest example left.

3. The worship hall in front, and how much it looks like a house. It dates from the early Kamakura period and is built in the style of an aristocrat's residence. ⇒ A house design is doing religious work here. You saw the same borrowing in the drawing room at Sanbo-in on 4 November; this example is about four centuries older. The Ujigami worship hall is built of cypress felled in the early Kamakura period, and the Sanbo-in drawing room dates from 1598.

4. Kirihara-sui, the spring on the grounds. Uji City's own page names it as one of the Seven Famous Waters of Uji. ⇒ Uji is a tea town because of water like this, and Kirihara-sui is the one still on a shrine's grounds. The city's page does not say whether the other six still exist.

5. The Kasuga sub-shrine in the precinct. It dates from the Kamakura period. ⇒ The Kasuga gods were brought to Nara from four other places, as you read on 31 October. This sub-shrine is a branch of Kasuga Taisha, built four hundred years after the Kasuga gods came to Nara.

Agency for Cultural Affairs database and Kyoto Prefecture pages (the halls and the roof form); Uji City official site (Kirihara-sui and the Seven Waters); Kyoto Prefecture (the Kasuga sub-shrine); Agency for Cultural Affairs record for the Sanbo-in Omote-shoin (1598) · checked 2026-08-15, dates for the worship-hall comparison checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official history and enshrined deities (Japanese) National cultural-properties record for the main hall (Japanese) Kyoto Prefecture World Heritage page (Japanese) Uji City page (Japanese)
Manpuku-ji黄檗山 萬福寺
Head temple of the Obaku school · Uji · founded 1661 by a Chinese monk · built Chinese and still is
▶
🔴 It is a Chinese temple, and that is not a figure of speech

There is a temple called Wanfu-si on Mount Huangbo in Fuzhou, Fujian. Its abbot, Yinyuan Longqi (Ingen in Japanese), came to Japan in 1654, aged 63, with about twenty disciples. He was granted land here, and in 1661 he founded a temple which he gave the same name as the one he had left, written with the same characters: Obaku-san Manpuku-ji.

He never went home. The temple's English page: "Instead of returning to China, he decided to found Obaku-san Manpuku-ji and ended up making Japan his permanent home."

The complex was begun in 1661 and the national record dates its provisional completion to 1679 — eighteen years of building.

Manpuku-ji official English and Japanese site (the founding, Ingen's arrival, the shared name); Agency for Cultural Affairs national database (the 1661–1679 construction) · checked 2026-08-15

Why it looks unlike a Japanese temple

The temple's own account of its buildings: they are "different from ordinary Japanese temple architecture," built in the style of late-Ming China, the country Ingen came from. The national record puts it more precisely — the layout and the halls show the influence of Chinese Buddhist architecture from the end of the Ming into the early Qing, and are valuable in the history of early-modern building for exactly that reason.

🔴 The temple is not even built of Japanese wood. The temple states that the construction timber is teak, native to South and Southeast Asia, and in its words the main hall is the only and largest historic building in Japan made of teak. The temple's English page says the teak is from Sumatra; its Japanese page says only South and Southeast Asia. Both pages are the temple's own, and this entry uses the broader statement.

Four details the temple singles out as things you will not see at other Japanese temples: balustrades in a manji-kuzushi broken-swastika pattern · round windows · arched Obaku ceilings · and peach-shaped ornaments on the doors, a charm against evil. Its own summary: no other temple in Japan preserves its founding-era appearance this completely.

Manpuku-ji official Japanese precinct pages and English overview; Agency for Cultural Affairs national database for the Ming-to-Qing assessment · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 They still chant in Chinese

The chanting is what makes Manpuku-ji a living Chinese temple today, as well as a historic building. The temple: "In the Obaku school, the liturgy and the sutras are all faithfully inherited as they were performed in China." The reading used is toon — Chinese-based pronunciation.

The temple gives its own worked example. The opening of the Heart Sutra, which in an ordinary Japanese temple is chanted "Maka hannya haramita shingyo…", is chanted here as "Poze poromi to shinkin…"

⇒ ⚑ If you hear a service, you are hearing a Chinese liturgy that has been kept up without a break in Japan for over three hundred and sixty years, by people who mostly do not speak Chinese. The chanting is sung rather than intoned, in a four-beat form with bells, drums and gongs, and the temple describes it as performed like music.

Manpuku-ji official Japanese site, the school's own description, quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

Zen has three schools in Japan, and this one was not allowed to be one until 1876

The temple's own history says the school was first called something like "the Obaku branch of the Rinzai school", and it stayed that way because of shogunate policy. Only in 1876 did it become independent and take the name Obaku-shu. The temple's own sentence: Zen in Japan is classified into three schools — Rinzai, Soto and Obaku.

⇒ 🔴 For more than two hundred years after it was founded, Obaku, the third school of Japanese Zen, was formally a sub-branch of Rinzai, the first. Its independence came from a Meiji reorganisation, with no doctrinal victory behind it.

⚑ Zen was already old in Japan when Ingen arrived. It came to Japan from China in the thirteenth century, brought by Chinese monks, and by the time Ingen landed it had been established in Japan for four hundred years. ⇒ He arrived as a foreign teacher in a country that already had the religion he was bringing. The visible difference is that this time the buildings stayed Chinese. The temple notes that its abbots were Chinese monks for thirteen successive generations.

Manpuku-ji official Japanese site, the school's own history and the abbots' succession · checked 2026-08-15

🆕 Three of its halls became National Treasures in December 2024

The designation is recent enough that most printed guides will not include it. On 18 October 2024 the Council for Cultural Affairs recommended that the Hatto, the Daiyu Hoden and the Tenno-den be raised from Important Cultural Property to National Treasure; the designation was gazetted on 9 December 2024.

The temple's own announcement repeats the reason it was given: the three halls were judged outstanding even among Important Cultural Properties, and of deep significance in cultural history. Its other twenty main buildings remain Important Cultural Properties, designated in two earlier rounds, in 1965 and 1989. That makes twenty-three designated buildings in all.

⚠ The temple's general description page still says all twenty-three are Important Cultural Properties and has not been updated; the dated announcement is the current statement.

Manpuku-ji official announcement dated 2024-10-18 (Japanese); Agency for Cultural Affairs database, National Treasure designation date 2024-12-09 · the internal inconsistency on the temple's own site is reported, not smoothed · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Manpuku-ji is a large, flat, symmetrical compound laid out on a central axis, with matching halls on the left and right. Six things to look for:

1. The paving underfoot, and the rule attached to it. A path of diamond-shaped stones runs through the precinct, in a pattern the temple says is modelled on dragon scales. A Zen monk of great power is likened to a dragon, so only the abbot may walk on the diamond-shaped stones. ⇒ Almost nobody notices this rule, though the temple states it openly on its own page.

2. The kaipan at the dining hall — a wooden fish. The temple describes it as a fish-shaped ritual implement that announces the hours of the monastery's daily observances. Next to it hangs a bronze plate struck at the morning and midday meals. ⇒ The fish is a working signal: it has announced the times of day in this compound since the seventeenth century.

3. In the main hall, count the rakan. There are eighteen. A Japanese temple would have sixteen, and the temple says eighteen matches the arrangement in Ming halls in China. ⇒ The number alone shows where the building came from.

4. The doors and windows of the main hall. Peach-carved door panels against evil; round windows on either side. ⇒ Both are Chinese features, and neither belongs to Japanese temple building.

5. The Tenno-den, the first hall you pass through. It holds the Four Heavenly Kings, Idaten, and a seated Hotei carved by a Chinese sculptor in 1663. Hotei is usually a folk figure of good luck, but here he is Miroku, the buddha of the future, himself. ⇒ The fat laughing figure you have seen in shop windows all trip is a buddha in this hall.

6. The founder's hall, reached by a path of irregular stones. The temple describes the paving as an ice-crack pattern, with no two stones the same shape. Inside is a portrait statue of Ingen made in 1663 while he was alive, for his seventieth birthday, with real hair and beard set into it. ⇒ It is the only object on the grounds modelled on a living person's face.

Manpuku-ji official Japanese precinct pages, per-building descriptions (the dragon-scale paving and its rule, the kaipan, the eighteen rakan, the peach doors and round windows, the Tenno-den's Hotei as Miroku, the founder's hall and the portrait statue) · checked 2026-08-15

What Ingen is said to have brought with him

The temple's English page gives a broad list: art, medicine, architecture, music, history, literature, printing, sencha tea and Buddhist cuisine. The Japanese page is more specific and names the ingen bean, which carries his name, plus watermelon, lotus root, moso bamboo and the wooden fish-drum.

⚠ Treat this list as cautiously as the temple does. Its Japanese page mostly says the plants and foods "are said to" have come with Ingen, so read them as tradition the temple supports but does not claim to have proved. The Chinese sutra pronunciation is different: you can hear it for yourself.

The vegetarian cuisine, fucha ryori, is here too: the name means sharing tea widely with everyone, and its etiquette is four to a table, no seniority, and nothing left uneaten.

Manpuku-ji official English overview and Japanese pages on the school and on fucha ryori · the difference in how firmly each source states the food attributions is reported · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official page on the school and its founder (Japanese) Official walk through the precinct (Japanese) Official announcement of the 2024 National Treasure designation (Japanese)

Saturday 7 November — Hiroshima

A garden that survived, and a park built on the site of a destroyed district · every figure below is quoted with the qualification its own source gives it
Shukkei-en縮景園
Daimyo garden · Hiroshima · begun 1620 · 1,370 m from the hypocentre
▶
What it is

A lord's garden begun in 1620 by Asano Nagaakira, the first Asano lord of Hiroshima. It was designed by his chief retainer Ueda Soko, a noted tea master. The garden reached its present shape in a long rebuilding between the 1780s and 1830s, and in 1804 thirty-four individual views within it were formally named. It marked its four hundredth year in 2020.

On 3 April 1758 the Horeki Great Fire (宝暦の大火), which the garden calls the worst fire in Hiroshima since the city was founded, spread into the garden and burned down many of its buildings. It broke out at about 4 p.m. in Shirakami-gumi 5-chome (today's Otemachi 3-chome), and a strong wind spread it as far as Hakushima and Ushita.

The name is usually translated as "shrunken scenery." The garden's own account traces the name to a preface by the Confucian scholar Hayashi Razan, which has a line about compressing sea and mountains into this ground and gathering the scenery into this pavilion. A second tradition holds that the garden was built as a miniature of West Lake in China. ⚠ The garden gives both explanations and does not say which is right.

Shukkei-en official site, introduction and history pages (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-08-15; history page re-read for the 1758 fire · checked 2026-10-17

6 August 1945, in the garden's own words

The garden's history page describes the bombing in its own words, quoted here as written:

"Many precious lives were lost in the atomic bombing of 6 August 1945. Numerous buildings within the garden, beginning with the Seifukan, were reduced to ruins in an instant, and great ancient trees were burned; the appearance of former days was entirely lost. However, the pond that forms the garden's foundation — Takuei Pond — along with the artificial hills and the Kokokyo bridge, though severely damaged, retained their former form."

The garden is 1,370 metres from the hypocentre, a distance the city has surveyed precisely. Restoration began in 1949. The garden reopened, still unfinished, in April 1951, and the full restoration took roughly thirty years.

⚠ A government tourism text reports something the garden's own page does not mention: a memorial to atomic-bomb victims stands near the river in the garden, built after photographs came to light showing that bodies had been buried in makeshift graves in the garden after the attack. Because this account is not on the garden's own site, treat it as reported rather than confirmed, and look for the memorial's own inscription when you are there.

Shukkei-en official site, history page (Japanese), quoted and translated; City of Hiroshima A-bombed-trees register for the 1,370 m figure; Japan Tourism Agency multilingual database for the makeshift-graves memorial, flagged as non-garden-sourced · checked 2026-08-15

Three trees that were here on the day

The City of Hiroshima keeps a register of surviving hibakujumoku — A-bombed trees — and lists exactly three in this garden, each surveyed at 1,370 metres from the hypocentre:

a ginkgo · a muku tree (Japanese hackberry) · a Japanese black pine.

The garden's own description names the same three trees as the register. It adds that the great ginkgo is said to be over two hundred years old, and that the muku is hollow inside but still comes into thick leaf.

⚑ Across the whole city, the register listed 159 A-bombed trees within about two kilometres of the hypocentre as of 1 April 2025. ⚠ A government tourism page still gives 161, a figure from 2019. The city's own figure is the more recent one and is used here.

City of Hiroshima official A-bombed-trees register (Japanese), which lists all three by species and distance; Shukkei-en official highlights page for the ginkgo's age and the hollow muku; the 159-versus-161 discrepancy between the city register and the tourism database is reported · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

A path runs in a circuit around a pond, and the walk takes well under an hour. Four things to look at:

1. The Kokokyo bridge: cross it. This arched stone bridge across the middle of the pond dates from the 1780s rebuilding. In the garden's own words, its construction is "elegant and at the same time sturdy — it withstood even the blast of the atomic bomb." ⇒ It is the one structure in the garden that was still standing after the bombing in 1945.

2. Find the three A-bombed trees: the ginkgo, the muku and the black pine. ⇒ They were standing here, 1,370 metres from the hypocentre, at 8:15 on the morning of 6 August 1945. The hollow muku is the easiest of the three to identify.

3. Takuei Pond and its islands. The pond is the garden's foundation, and it is one of the parts of the garden that kept its form through the blast. Its many small islands are generally read as the islands of the Seto Inland Sea. That reading of the islands comes from a government tourism text, not from the garden itself.

4. Chozenkyo. The garden names this as the one spot where the original designer Ueda Soko's intent was respected and left untouched through every later renovation. ⇒ The view here is roughly four hundred years old, in a garden that was otherwise rebuilt twice: once after the fire of 1758 and once after the bomb.
