Shukkei-en official highlights and history pages (Japanese), for the bridge's survival, the trees, and Chozenkyo; Japan Tourism Agency database for the island reading, attributed · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (Japanese) City register of A-bombed trees (Japanese) Government commentary (English)
Hiroshima Peace Memorial Park平和記念公園
Urban park · Hiroshima · designed 1949 by Kenzo Tange · built on a destroyed district
▶
What was here before

The park occupies the head of a delta island, where the Motoyasu River branches off from the Ota River. The city's own account of what stood here: the Nakajima district, which "benefited from river transport and the Saigoku Kaido passing through it, and had been one of Hiroshima's leading entertainment districts since early modern times." The same page continues: "the atomic bombing instantly annihilated the area, close as it was to the hypocentre, reducing it to scorched earth."

⇒ The park was laid out on the site of a commercial district that was destroyed in 1945 and never rebuilt.

City of Hiroshima official history exhibition, "How the Peace Memorial Park came to be" (Japanese), quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

The law, and why it was legally unusual

The park exists because of the Hiroshima Peace Memorial City Construction Law, promulgated on 6 August 1949. The law passed both houses of the Diet unanimously in May 1949, and then needed one more step that almost no Japanese law needs.

🔴 Because it was a special law applying to a single local government, the constitution required a local referendum. The law was promulgated after receiving over ninety per cent support from Hiroshima's voters. ⇒ ⚑ The people of Hiroshima voted on whether their city should become a peace memorial city, and that vote is the legal foundation of the park you are standing in.

Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum official English history timeline, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15

The competition, and Tange's axis

The design was chosen by open competition, announced in April 1949. By the deadline of 20 July, 145 proposals had been submitted. The winner was the group led by Kenzo Tange, then at the University of Tokyo. Construction ran from 1950 to 1955, and the park opened to the public on 1 April 1956. It covers 122,100 square metres and holds thirty-five memorial monuments.

🔴 The most important thing to know before you walk in: the park is laid out along a straight line of sight. In the city's own words, it works to preserve "the north-south axial viewscape from beneath the Main Building of the Peace Memorial Museum, across the Cenotaph for the A-bomb Victims, to the Atomic Bomb Dome." The city's history page calls this line "Tange's axis," the name it is generally known by.

⇒ 🔴 The view along this line is protected by law. Since 4 January 2022 the city has enforced rules on landscape and building heights so that, from the fixed viewing point beneath the Museum's Main Building, no buildings are visible behind the Dome. ⚑ Here the law protects a view.

⚑ One more design fact: the railings of the Peace Bridge and the West Peace Bridge, the two bridges at the park's southern entrance, were designed by the sculptor Isamu Noguchi.

City of Hiroshima archival exhibition (the competition, the 145 entries, "Tange's axis") and the city's landscape-planning page (the protected viewscape and its 2022 date), both Japanese, translated; the city park page for the area, monument count and opening date · checked 2026-08-15

The Cenotaph, its inscription, and the question about it

The Cenotaph was built on 6 August 1952, designed by Kenzo Tange. Its arched shape is based on an ancient haniwa clay house. The city explains that the choice came from a wish to shelter the dead from rain and dew.

The inscription, in the city's own published English: "Let all the souls here rest in peace; For we shall not repeat the evil."

The stone chest beneath the arch holds the Register of the Names of the Fallen. The city's foundation notes that it records the dead regardless of nationality, and that names are added when a relative applies. 🔴 At the dedication on 6 August 2026 the register held 353,639 names in 132 volumes, plus one further volume for the unidentified. ⇒ ⚑ In 2026, eighty-one years after the bombing, the register is still growing: in 2015 it held 297,684 names in 108 volumes.

⚠ The inscription has been argued over since it was set, because it does not say who "we" are. The city's answer, in its published FAQ, is that the inscription is a vow by all people. The city says the vow carries the heart of Hiroshima, which endures past sorrow, overcomes hatred, and prays for the coexistence of all humankind. The city has put up explanatory panels in eight languages in front of the Cenotaph to explain that reading. The dispute over the wording is real and long-running, but the city's pages explain the meaning without describing the dispute, so only the city's position is given here.

Hiroshima Peace Culture Foundation page on the Cenotaph (English, for the design, the inscription's official translation and the register); City of Hiroshima FAQ (Japanese) for the haniwa explanation and the city's reading of the inscription; City of Hiroshima FAQ updated 2026-08-12 for the current register figures · checked 2026-08-15

The Children's Peace Monument and the paper cranes

Sadako Sasaki was two years old when she was exposed to the bomb. She had no visible injuries and grew up strong and healthy. In the autumn of 1954, in her sixth year of elementary school, she fell ill, and in February 1955 she was diagnosed with leukaemia. She believed that folding paper cranes would help her recover, and she kept folding them to the end. She died on 25 October 1955, after eight months of illness.

Her classmates then put out a national call to "build a monument to mourn all the children who died from the atomic bombing." Students in more than 3,100 schools in Japan and in nine other countries supported it. The monument was completed on 5 May 1958, Children's Day. It is a bronze statue nine metres high, designed by Kazuo Kikuchi.

On top of the three-legged pedestal stands a bronze girl holding up a gold-coloured paper crane. Figures of a boy and a girl hang on opposite sides of the pedestal. The stone beneath carries the words: "This is our cry. This is our prayer. For building peace in this world."

⚑ The City of Hiroshima traces the link between paper cranes and peace back to Sadako. About ten million cranes are offered at the monument every year. A golden crane given by the Nobel physicist Hideki Yukawa first hung as a wind chime beneath a bell inside the tower. The bell and the crane are now in the first-floor lobby of the Museum's East Building.

City of Hiroshima official English page "Paper Cranes and Children's Peace Monument" (Sadako's story and the yearly crane figure); Hiroshima Peace Culture Foundation Peace Park guide, monument 16 (English) for the monument itself · checked 2026-10-17

The Monument in Memory of the Korean Victims of the A-bomb

According to the Hiroshima Peace Culture Foundation, Japan annexed Korea in 1910. Many Koreans who had lost their livelihoods were forced to come to Japan to find work, and to cover its wartime labour shortage Japan also brought many Koreans to work through forced service or conscription. About three million Koreans were living in Japan at the end of the war, and it is said that tens of thousands of them suffered the atomic bombing in Hiroshima.

The monument was completed on 10 April 1970. It stands on a base shaped like a turtle and carries the epigraph "Souls of the dead ride to heaven on the backs of turtles." Two dragons are carved into the crown on top of the column.

⚑ It was first built outside the park, at the western end of Honkawa Bridge, near the place where the body of Prince Lee, a member of the Korean royal family, was found after the bombing. Pressure grew over the years to move it inside the park, and after the city and all the parties concerned reached agreement it was moved into the park in July 1999.

Hiroshima Peace Culture Foundation Peace Park guide, monument 11 (English) · checked 2026-10-17

The Peace Bell

The Peace Bell is a temple bell in a domed belfry on four pillars, completed on 20 September 1964. The group that built it described it as a symbol of Hiroshima's wish for a world without nuclear weapons or war. The bell was cast by Masahiko Katori, a Living National Treasure.

Three details on the bell: its surface carries a world map without national borders, standing for "one world." The spot where the wooden beam strikes it shows the atomic energy symbol, as a hope for the abolition of atomic and hydrogen bombs. On the opposite side is a mirror, to reflect the heart of the person who rings the bell.

Lotus grows in the pond around the belfry and flowers around 6 August each year. ⚑ The Foundation's page adds that after the bombing, lotus leaves were laid on people's burns to ease the pain. In 1996 the sounds of the park on 6 August, including this bell, were chosen among the Environment Agency's hundred Japanese soundscapes worth preserving.

Hiroshima Peace Culture Foundation Peace Park guide, monument 8 (English) · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Open ground, accessible at all hours, with no admission charge. Four things to look at:

1. Stand beneath the Museum's Main Building and look north. Do not look from the side or from the bridge. ⇒ From there the Museum, the Cenotaph and the Dome line up. This is the viewing point the city protects by law, and it is the only place where the arch of the Cenotaph frames the Dome.

2. At the Cenotaph, read the inscription, then look through the arch. ⇒ The arch is built to frame the view beyond it, with the Dome at the far end.

3. The Rest House: more important than it looks. The park's tourist-information building is the former Fuel Hall, 170 metres from the hypocentre. The city describes it as the building nearest ground zero known to have had a survivor inside. Fire damage remains in its basement and blast damage on its upper floors. ⇒ You will probably walk past it looking for a toilet or a map. It is one of six A-bombed buildings designated together as a national Historic Site in February 2024.

4. The A-bombed Chinese parasol trees near the Museum's East Building. They are registered hibakujumoku (A-bombed trees), moved here from the site of the former Central Post Office. ⇒ They were alive in the city on the morning of 6 August 1945, and they now stand in a park where everything else was built after the bombing.

City of Hiroshima landscape page (the protected vantage point); City of Hiroshima Special Historic Site page (the Rest House at 170 m and the 2024 Historic Site designation); City of Hiroshima A-bombed-trees register (the parasol trees) · checked 2026-08-15

Links
City park page (Japanese) Peace Culture Foundation on the Cenotaph (English) City page on the protected viewscape (Japanese)
Atomic Bomb Dome原爆ドーム
Ruin · Hiroshima · built 1915 · World Heritage 1996 · Special Historic Site 2025
▶
What the building was

The building was an exhibition hall. Construction began in 1914 and it was completed in 1915 as the Hiroshima Prefectural Products Exhibition Hall, designed by the Czech architect Jan Letzel. It was a three-storey brick building, with a five-storey stairwell at the centre carrying an elliptical dome. It was renamed in 1921, and again in 1933 as the Hiroshima Prefectural Industrial Promotion Hall.

The hall sold and displayed the prefecture's products and held art exhibitions and trade fairs. From 1944 it housed wartime government offices.

City of Hiroshima official pages on the Dome and its Special Historic Site designation (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-08-15

What happened, and why the frame is still there

At 8:15 on the morning of 6 August 1945, the bomb exploded approximately 600 metres above the centre of the city. The hall stood approximately 160 metres from the point directly below the explosion. Blast and heat gutted it; fire spread from the ceiling downward and burned it out. Everyone inside, staff of the civil engineering office and of several timber companies, died instantly.

The city explains why the skeleton of the building survived when the rest of the district did not: the blast struck it almost vertically, from nearly overhead, rather than from the side. ⇒ Being so close to the point below the explosion is what left the frame standing.

⚠ The figures below are quoted with the city's own qualifications and are not rounded. The city states the population at the time was approximately 350,000: residents, military personnel, people brought in from surrounding towns to demolish buildings, and people from Japan's colonies, some of them conscripted labourers. On the dead: "The exact number of deaths from the atomic bombing is still unknown. Estimates place the number of dead by the end of December 1945, when the acute effects of radiation poisoning had largely subsided, at roughly 140,000." And: roughly 50 per cent of those within 1.2 kilometres of the hypocentre are estimated to have died that day; closer to the hypocentre, 80 to 100 per cent died.

City of Hiroshima official Dome page (the time, altitude, distance and the vertical-blast explanation) and City of Hiroshima official English FAQ (the population and casualty figures, quoted verbatim including the statement that the exact number is unknown) · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 It was nearly demolished, and a schoolgirl's diary changed that

Keeping the ruin was not the obvious choice. Many in the city wanted it taken down as an unbearable reminder. The city credits the change of mind to the diary of Kajiyama Hiroko, who was exposed to the bomb at the age of one and died of leukaemia fifteen years later. Her diary read:

"Only that pitiful Industrial Promotion Hall will, forever, keep appealing to future generations about the dreadfulness of the atomic bomb."

The city began a structural survey in 1965. The city council resolved to preserve the building in July 1966, and fundraising and the first preservation works followed in 1967. ⇒ There have been five preservation campaigns in all: 1967, 1989–90, 2002–03, 2015–16 and 2020–21. The city re-surveys the structure roughly every three years. Each campaign used the minimum reinforcement needed to keep the ruin in the condition the bomb left it in.

City of Hiroshima official Dome page (Japanese), the diary quoted and translated, and the five campaigns; UNESCO's own listing corroborates the conservation approach · checked 2026-08-15

Two designations: World Heritage in 1996, Special Historic Site in 2025

World Heritage, December 1996. Getting there took a change in Japanese law. When the city petitioned in 1992, the national government held back: a nomination needed protection under Japanese law first, and the historic-site rules then reached only as far as the mid-Meiji era, so the Dome was too recent to qualify. A citizens' campaign gathered over 1.65 million signatures, and the Diet passed supporting resolutions in 1994. The criteria were revised in March 1995 to reach as far as the end of the war. The Dome was designated a Historic Site in June 1995 and nominated in September 1995.

UNESCO inscribed it under criterion (vi), describing it as "a stark and powerful symbol of the achievement of world peace for more than half a century following the unleashing of the most destructive force ever created by humankind."

🆕 On 18 September 2025 the Dome became a national Special Historic Site, Japan's highest tier of historic-site protection. The city's own wording says what makes this exceptional: it is the first site dating from the Meiji era or later, anywhere in the country, to receive that designation. As of June 2026 there are sixty-six Special Historic Sites in Japan.

⇒ ⚑ In 1992 the Dome was refused protection for being too modern. It is now the first site from the Meiji era or later to be placed in the highest category the country has.

City of Hiroshima official Dome page (the 1992–1996 sequence and the law change) and its Special Historic Site page (the 2025-09-18 designation, the first-since-Meiji claim and the 66-site count), both Japanese, translated; UNESCO World Heritage Centre for the criterion (vi) wording · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

⚠ You cannot go in. There is no public access inside the fence; the ruin is seen from outside only. Three things to look at:

1. The low walls around the base are not a boundary wall. The city notes that they are the building's own walls, which stood to the third floor before most of the second and third storeys collapsed. ⇒ The low walls trace the outline of the rooms.

2. The dome itself, seen from across the Motoyasu River. The elliptical frame sat on top of the five-storey central stairwell and is the only part of that structure still standing. ⇒ The building's present name, Atomic Bomb Dome, comes from this frame, and nobody chose that name.

3. Stand still and take your bearings. The bomb exploded roughly 600 metres up, about 160 metres from where you are standing. ⇒ Everything within several hundred metres of this spot was destroyed at 8:15 on the morning of 6 August 1945. The Peace Memorial Park you walked through on the way here was built on one of the destroyed districts.

City of Hiroshima official Dome page and park page, for the wall remnants, the dome structure, the distances and the no-entry rule · checked 2026-08-15

Links
City page on the Dome (Japanese) City page on the 2025 Special Historic Site designation (Japanese) UNESCO listing (English)
National Peace Memorial Hall国立広島原爆死没者追悼平和祈念館
National memorial · Hiroshima · opened 2002 · Kenzo Tange · not the museum
▶
⚠ First — this is not the Peace Memorial Museum, and the two are constantly confused

The Hall and the Museum are separate institutions about 180 metres apart in the same park, run by different bodies. The Hall explains the difference in its own FAQ:

"The Peace Memorial Museum primarily uses materials — materials about the bombing, possessions which belonged to the victims — to convey the realities of the bombing and the necessity of peace, whereas the Peace Memorial Hall primarily uses the thoughts and words of the victims — diaries and memoirs of the victims and bereaved families."

⇒ ⚑ The Museum holds objects. The Hall holds writing.

The Museum is run by the City of Hiroshima. The Hall is a national institution, built by the national government under the Atomic Bomb Survivors' Assistance Act of 1994 and run for the health ministry. It opened on 1 August 2002. Admission is free. Like the Museum, the Hall was designed by Kenzo Tange.

National Peace Memorial Hall official English FAQ and "About" pages, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 The Hall of Remembrance: one tile for each estimated death

A ramp takes you down to the Hall of Remembrance, a circular room whose wall carries a 360-degree panorama of Hiroshima after the bombing, as seen from the hypocentre. The panorama is based on a photograph taken by the US military in October 1945.

🔴 The panorama is made of tiles, and the number of tiles is deliberate. In the Hall's own description, it is made of roughly 140,000 tiles, the number of people estimated to have died by the end of 1945.

Beneath the panorama are the names of the 226 towns that existed at the time of the bombing, each placed in the direction where it actually lay. The room is 55 metres around, 17.25 metres across and 8 metres high. At its centre a monument shows the time 8:15 and has running water. The Hall's stated reason for the water: to console those who died that day without the water they craved.

National Peace Memorial Hall official English floor guide, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15

The walls contain soil from the time of the bombing

Two details of the building are easy to walk past:

The ramp down to the Hall of Remembrance turns counter-clockwise. The Hall says the direction is deliberate: you are walking back in time, past six panels explaining how the bombing came about.

🔴 The countless black specks in the walls of the ramp and of the Hall of Remembrance are soil. Earth from the layer of ground dating from the time of the bombing was dug up while the Hall was being built and mixed into the wall material. ⇒ ⚑ The walls of the memorial contain soil from the city of 1945.

National Peace Memorial Hall official English floor guide · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The Hall is underground, free and quiet, and you can stay as long as you like. Four things to look at:

1. On the way down the ramp, look at the wall beside you rather than ahead. The black flecks are soil dug up from the 1945 layer. ⇒ They are close enough to touch, and almost nobody notices them.

2. In the Hall of Remembrance, walk all the way round, all 55 metres. ⇒ You are walking around a photograph of the view from the hypocentre, made of one tile for each estimated death.

3. Find your bearings from the town names beneath the panorama. Each of the 226 towns sits in the direction where it actually lay. ⇒ The room is a map as well as a picture.

4. The Victims' Information Area. Portraits and names contributed by bereaved families are shown on a screen and can be searched at terminals. ⇒ The Hall says the area is there to convey lives lost indiscriminately, regardless of age, gender or profession. A search lets you look up one particular person among them. The library holds over 140,000 memoirs, with a selection translated into more than twenty languages.

National Peace Memorial Hall official English floor guide and library pages · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official floor guide (English) Official FAQ, incl. the difference from the Museum (English)
Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum広島平和記念資料館
Museum · Hiroshima · opened 1955 · Kenzo Tange · Japan's first post-war building designated an Important Cultural Property
▶
What it says it is for

The museum's own statement of purpose, in its own English: "A single atomic bomb indiscriminately killed tens of thousands of people, profoundly disrupting and altering the lives of the survivors. Through belongings left by the victims, A-bombed artifacts, testimonies of A-bomb survivors and related materials, the Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum conveys to the world the horrors and the inhumane nature of nuclear weapons."

Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum official English homepage, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15

It began with one man collecting rubble

The museum's own timeline starts before the museum building existed. In September 1949 an "A-bomb Reference Material Display Room" opened in the Hiroshima Central Public Hall. It was the first public display of atomic bomb materials, and it included debris collected by the local geologist Shogo Nagaoka.

Construction of the museum itself began in February 1951, and it opened on 24 August 1955 with Nagaoka as its first director. At the same time, a citizens' volunteer group began a public drive to collect A-bombed artefacts.

⇒ ⚑ The collection was gathered by the city's own residents, from their own possessions, starting four years after the bombing. The first director was a geologist who had been picking up stones.

Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum official English "Museum History" timeline, quoted and paraphrased · checked 2026-08-15

The building, and why it is itself a designated object

The Main Building is reinforced concrete, raised on piloti: free-standing columns that leave the ground floor open. It was completed on 6 August 1955. In the museum's own assessment, the building "became the first post-war architectural structure highly appraised internationally" and is "recognized as a starting point of Tange's career."

🔴 On 5 July 2006 it became the first building constructed after the Second World War in Japan to be designated an Important Cultural Property.

The East Building followed in 1994. It was made deliberately heavier and plainer in appearance, with stone-faced posts and free-standing walls, because it was designed to support the Main Building rather than compete with it. Both buildings were renovated in a project that began in 2014: the East Building reopened on 26 April 2017 and the Main Building on 25 April 2019.

⇒ ⚑ The open ground floor is what makes Tange's axis work: you look under the building, between the columns, to the Cenotaph and the Dome.

Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum official English pages on the facilities, renovations and history · checked 2026-08-15

How the exhibition is arranged

The museum organises the permanent display into six areas across two floors: an introductory exhibit on the city before the bombing and the life that was lost · Hiroshima on August 6, entered through the Main Building, covering the devastation and the damage from radiation · Victims and Survivors · a gallery on the construction of the park itself · the dangers of nuclear weapons · and Hiroshima's history, war, reconstruction and after.

⚑ The order of the areas is deliberate: first the city as it was, then the morning of the bombing, then the people, then the weapon, then what came after.

Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum official English permanent-exhibition index · checked 2026-08-15

Five objects in the permanent exhibition

1. The stone steps with a human shadow (area 2, "Human Shadow Etched in Stone"). The steps stood at the entrance of the Sumitomo Bank's Hiroshima branch, 260 metres from the hypocentre. A person sitting on them, waiting for the bank to open, is thought to have died on the spot. The heat turned the steps whitish, and the stone under the person stayed dark, like a shadow. Several families have suggested that the person may have been one of their own.

2. A schoolgirl's uniform (area 2, "Children Killed in the Bombing"). Nobuko Shoda, aged 14, was at a building-demolition worksite 1,200 metres from the hypocentre. Her parents found her the next day, badly burned. She was taken home, and she died on 10 August.

3. A tricycle and a metal helmet (area 3, "Cries of the Soul"). Shinichi Tetsutani, aged three years and eleven months, was riding this tricycle when the bomb exploded, 1,500 metres from the hypocentre. He was badly injured and burned, and he died that night. The helmet shown with it is one his father put on Shinichi's body.

4. A lunch box (area 3, "Cries of the Soul"). Shigeru Orimen, aged 13, a first-year junior high school student, died at his building-demolition worksite 600 metres from the hypocentre. His mother found the scorched lunch box beneath his remains. It held rice mixed with barley, soy beans and fried shredded potato. In the museum's words, he left home looking forward to this lunch but never got to eat it.

5. A wristwatch (area 3, "Cries of the Soul"). Toshiko Nagawa, aged 22, was trapped under her collapsed house, 1,200 metres from the hypocentre, and the fires reached it. Her father could not get back at once, and it was dark when he found her remains. The watch was found under her arm. The watch stopped at 1 p.m. The museum believes that is about when the fire reached her.

Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum official English permanent-exhibition pages, one page per object (sections 2-2-3, 2-2-5, 3-1-1 and 3-1-2) · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

⚠ This is the heaviest visit of the trip. Plan nothing demanding straight after it. Four things to look at:

1. Before going in, stand under the Main Building between the columns and look north. ⇒ This is the starting point of Tange's axis, and the reason the building stands on columns.

2. Take the introductory exhibit slowly rather than hurrying on to the main sequence. It shows the city and the way of life that existed before the bombing. ⇒ Everything after it means more once you have seen what was there first, and that is the museum's own reason for putting it first.

3. In the Main Building, the exhibition is built around belongings rather than statistics. ⇒ The museum's stated method is to tell the story through objects people owned. The Memorial Hall, by contrast, tells it through what people wrote.

4. Look at the Main Building itself on the way out. It dates from 1955 and is an Important Cultural Property, the first post-war building in the country to be designated one. ⇒ The building is part of what you came to see.

Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum official English pages, exhibition index and facilities outline · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official museum history (English) City page on the damage and casualty figures (English)

Sunday 8 November — Miyajima and Mount Misen

A shrine built over the sea, a hall Hideyoshi never finished, and a mountain whose eternal flame hall burned down in May 2026
Itsukushima Shrine厳島神社
Shrine · Miyajima · established 593 · rebuilt 1168 by Taira no Kiyomori · World Heritage
▶
⚠ What is under scaffolding on 8 November

Read this before the history: it decides what you will actually see on 8 November. The shrine's own construction table lists:

⚠ The East Corridor is under structural reinforcement, 10 April 2024 to December 2026. That window contains 8 November.

⚠ The five-storey pagoda is under restoration, 20 January 2025 to 20 November 2027.

✅ The Great Torii is clear. Its roof re-thatching, painting and partial repair ran 17 June 2019 to December 2022 and is finished — the scaffolding that covered it for three years is gone.

✅ The Nagabashi bridge repair ran to March 2026 and should also be done.

Itsukushima Shrine official construction-status page (Japanese), read row by row from the page's own table · checked 2026-08-15

What it is, and the crow that chose the site

The shrine dates itself to 593, the year Empress Suiko came to the throne. Its own founding account is specific. A divine command came to Saeki no Kuramoto, who governed the island. Led by a sacred crow the goddess had brought from the Plain of High Heaven, he circled the island's bays with the deities and chose the place where the seawater ebbs and flows as the site for the shrine.

Taira no Kiyomori built the present shrine in 1168 in shinden-zukuri, which the shrine's own English page describes as "an architectural style in which residences of the nobility were built in those days." ⇒ ⚑ He used the house style of a court noble for a god's shrine, and raised it on posts in the sea. Retired emperors visited: Go-Shirakawa in 1174, and Takakura twice in 1180. They brought the culture of Kyoto with them.

⚠ One very common explanation does not come from the shrine. Guidebooks say the buildings stand over water because the whole island was too sacred for commoners to build on. The shrine's own pages do not say that. They say that the island is a sacred island, that the deity "went to great trouble, circling the island in search of a place to build the shrine," and that the chosen site is the tidal one. ⇒ The shrine certainly stands over the water, but the usual reason given for that is not the shrine's own claim.

Itsukushima Shrine official Japanese and English history pages and its auxiliary-shrines page, quoted and translated · the absence of the "too sacred to build on" causal claim from the shrine's own text is stated explicitly · checked 2026-08-15

What is worshipped

The shrine worships three goddesses, Ichikishimahime, Tagorihime and Tagitsuhime. They are the Munakata deities, born of the oath between Amaterasu and Susanoo. In the shrine's own English, they are "revered as deities that ensure the well-being of the imperial family, guard the nation and protect seafarers."

Itsukushima Shrine official English history page, quoted verbatim · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 A battle was fought here in 1555, and the shrine was ritually washed afterwards

In 1555, Mori Motonari lured Sue Harukata onto this narrow island and destroyed a far larger force — accounts give roughly four to five thousand against twenty to thirty thousand.

🔴 What Motonari did after the battle is the part to remember. He had the war dead carried off the island to the mainland for burial, had the shrine grounds ritually washed, and had the blood-soaked soil scraped away and removed from the island.

⇒ ⚑ After winning the battle, Motonari worked to remove every trace of it from this ground. The island's prohibitions on death are usually described as an old taboo, and his clean-up shows what enforcing that taboo looked like in practice. The shrine's own pages do not confirm the taboo's history or the date often quoted for it, so both are left out here.

Miyajima Ropeway operator's official history page (Japanese), quoted and translated; the battle's outcome is corroborated in general reference sources · the birth-and-death taboo is flagged as not shrine-sourced · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The visit is a one-way boardwalk circuit over the water, and you keep your shoes on. Five things to look at:

1. Look down at the corridor floor. The planks are deliberately gapped. The shrine's own visitor notice says the gaps are there to weaken the water pressure at high tide. The planks are also laid double, with protective boards over the originals. ⇒ The protective boards are why you keep your shoes on in a place where you would normally take them off.

2. The Great Torii, which simply stands on the seabed. It is camphor wood, in the four-legged ryobu form. The present gate dates from 1875 and is an Important Cultural Property. ⇒ It is held in place by its own weight and is not fixed into the seabed. The shrine's own pages give the designation and the date; the description of it standing by its own weight comes from other sources. If the tide is out, walk out to its base.

3. The Noh stage. The national cultural-property record dates it to 1680. It is an Important Cultural Property, with its gable end facing the front. ⇒ Because it is built over the sea, the usual resonating jar cannot go under the floor. Instead, the floor itself is built to carry the sound of the performers' feet. The claim that it is "the only one in Japan built over the sea" is widely repeated but is not on the shrine's own pages.

4. The Takabutai, the raised stage in front of the main shrine. It is a National Treasure, used for the court music and dance that Kiyomori brought from Kyoto. ⇒ Picture it in use and the mansion style makes sense: this is a stage in a nobleman's garden, and the garden is the sea.

5. The Mirror Pond in the east corridor area, if the tide is low. The pool appears only when the water withdraws. ⇒ Some of the shrine's features exist for only half of each day.

Itsukushima Shrine official visitor and cultural-property pages (the gapped flooring, the National Treasure list, the Takabutai); Agency for Cultural Affairs national database (the Noh stage's 1680 date and the torii's 1875 date and designation) · the unsourced superlatives are flagged · checked 2026-08-15

The Treasure Hall

The Treasure Hall (宝物館) stands directly opposite the exit of the shrine's corridors. It was completed in April 1934 and is a Registered Tangible Cultural Property. It displays part of the shrine's collection of treasures and works of art.

⚑ The building is concrete made to look like wood. In the shrine's words, it was designed to suit the shrine buildings, and it was the first building in Japan where concrete was coated with lacquer to give it the look of a wooden building.

Itsukushima Shrine official cultural-property page (Japanese), Treasure Hall entry, translated · checked 2026-10-17

The Heike Nokyo: the sutra scrolls Kiyomori gave the shrine

The government's interpretive text calls it perhaps the most important of the shrine's treasures: the Heike Nokyo (平家納経), a set of 33 richly decorated sutra scrolls that the Taira clan, led by Taira no Kiyomori, presented to the shrine in 1164. The set is a National Treasure. It contains the whole Lotus Sutra, the Amida Sutra and the Heart Sutra, all copied by hand by members of the Taira clan. A dedication in Kiyomori's own hand comes with them.

The scrolls survive complete, together with the box that held them and the net bag used to carry the box. ⚠ What the shrine displays are reproductions. The scrolls on show are copies, the box on show is a 1925 reproduction, and the original box is shown only on special occasions. The bag is a reproduction too: it is made of open netting, so the gold and silver decoration on the copper box could be seen while it was carried.

⚠ The 2019 government texts that describe these copies do not say which building shows them.

Japan Tourism Agency multilingual database, three 2019 interpretive texts for Itsukushima Shrine (the Heike Nokyo, its box and its net bag), English · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official history (English) Official construction status (Japanese) Official cultural-property list (Japanese) UNESCO listing (English)
Senjokaku & Toyokuni Shrine千畳閣・豊国神社
Sutra hall · Miyajima · begun 1587 by Hideyoshi · never finished
▶
🔴 The hall was never finished because Hideyoshi died

Itsukushima Shrine's own account tells the whole story in two sentences:

"Toyotomi Hideyoshi, wishing to have a thousand fascicles of sutra chanted, made his vow in 1587 and ordered Ankokuji Ekei to build this great sutra hall. After Hideyoshi's death, the ceiling boarding and the building's exterior fittings were left unfinished, and it remains so to this day."

⇒ 🔴 There is no ceiling. There is no front wall. You walk into a vast tiled roof standing on bare pillars, open to the weather on every side, because in 1598 the work stopped and was never resumed.

🔴 This is the third place on this trip linked to Hideyoshi's last years and his death in 1598. On 5 November, at Yoshino, you stood where he held his 1594 party. On 4 November, at Daigo-ji, you stood in the garden he designed for his 1598 party, and read that he was dead five months later. ⇒ ⚑ Senjokaku is the other end of the same story: a building he ordered, abandoned when he died and the parties stopped. The ceiling here and the Daigo-ji garden were both left unfinished for the same reason.

Itsukushima Shrine official auxiliary-shrines page (Japanese), quoted and translated; the Daigo-ji and Yoshino dates are from this document's own entries for 4 and 5 November · checked 2026-08-15

Who ordered it, and who it is dedicated to now

The builder named in the shrine's record is Ankokuji Ekei, a monk and diplomat in Hideyoshi's service. He is a known historical figure, and he was executed after the battle of Sekigahara in 1600.

The hall became a Shinto shrine in 1872, in the Meiji separation of Buddhism and Shinto, and was renamed Toyokuni Shrine, enshrining the deified spirit of Hideyoshi himself. Kato Kiyomasa was added in 1918.

⇒ ⚑ A Buddhist sutra hall that was never completed is now a Shinto shrine to the man who failed to complete it.

⚠ Two official sources give different purposes for the hall, and neither cites the other. The shrine says it was built for chanting the thousand-fascicle sutra. The ropeway operator's own history says Hideyoshi built it during his campaign toward Kyushu, to console the spirits of warriors who had died in battle and to pray for peace and prosperity. ⇒ The two purposes fit together, since a memorial sutra hall serves both. The emphasis differs, so both are given here.

Itsukushima Shrine official auxiliary-shrines page and Miyajima Ropeway official history page, both Japanese, both quoted · the divergence in stated purpose is reported, not resolved · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Senjokaku is a single enormous room up a flight of steps from the shrine, and you take your shoes off to enter. ⚠ The five-storey pagoda beside it is under restoration until November 2027. Four things to look at:

1. Look up first. There is no ceiling, so you look straight into the roof structure, at rafters that were meant to be hidden. ⇒ You can see how the building is put together because nobody finished covering it.

2. Then look out. With no front wall, the hall frames the shrine, the torii and the sea on one side and the pagoda on the other. ⇒ You get that view only because the front wall was never built.

3. The floor and the scale. The shrine's own measurements describe a hall thirteen bays across the front and fifteen across the back. Its nickname, Senjokaku, means "the thousand-mat pavilion." ⇒ Stand in one corner and look diagonally.

4. The pillars, and the double camphor-wood eaves running round the outside. ⇒ This is carpentry on the grand Momoyama scale, built with the same ambition as Hideyoshi's party at Daigo-ji.

Itsukushima Shrine official auxiliary-shrines page (Japanese), for the dimensions, the camphor eaves and the pagoda's date; the shrine's construction table for the pagoda's restoration window · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official page on the shrine's sub-shrines, incl. Senjokaku (Japanese) Ropeway operator's island history (Japanese)
Daisho-in大聖院
Shingon temple · Miyajima · founded 806 · its flame hall burned down in May 2026
▶
🔴🔴 The eternal flame hall burned down on 20 May 2026

Read this first: it changes what you will find on the mountain. The temple's own notice, posted on 20 May 2026, the day of the fire, says:

"Regrettably, the Misen Reikado and the Misen reception office were completely destroyed by fire, but fortunately there were no injuries, and the fire did not spread into the mountain forest."

A follow-up notice from the temple on 28 May 2026 reported the fire debris cleared with the help of volunteers, and stated the temple's intention: "From here on, we intend to consult repeatedly with all concerned parties toward the reconstruction of the Reikado, and to proceed one step at a time." A donation appeal for the rebuild is open.

⚠ As of 15 August 2026 no rebuild has begun and no completion date has been announced. Press reports at the time of the fire stated that the flame itself had been kept safe at a separate location, and noted that the same hall burned in 2005 and was rebuilt in 2006. The 2005 fire and 2006 rebuild are not from the temple's own pages.

⇒ 🔴 This matters beyond one building. The flame in that hall is said to have burned since Kobo Daishi's fire ritual here in 806. The temple states that it is the source flame of the Flame of Peace in the Hiroshima Peace Memorial Park, which you saw on 7 November. ⚑ The flame links the Peace Park you saw on 7 November with this mountain on 8 November, and the hall at the mountain end is now a cleared site.

Daisho-in official news page (Japanese), notices dated 2026-05-20 and 2026-05-28, quoted and translated, read 2026-08-15; the temple's own history page for the flame's origin and the stated link to the Hiroshima Peace Flame; the flame's removal to safety and the 2005 precedent are from press reporting and are flagged as not temple-sourced

What it is

Daisho-in is a head temple of the Omuro branch of Shingon, whose own head temple is Ninna-ji in Kyoto. In its own words, it is the temple with the deepest history of any on Miyajima (「宮島にある寺院で最も歴史が深い」). Its own account: after Kukai returned from Tang China he crossed to Miyajima and undertook ascetic training on Mount Misen; the temple was founded in 806.

🔴 Before the Meiji separation of Buddhism and Shinto, this temple ran Itsukushima Shrine. It served as the shrine's administrative Buddhist temple, managing its rites, and had twelve sub-temples. Two festivals still held at the shrine originated here: the Tamatori Ennen-sai (玉取延年祭), held on the eighteenth day of the seventh month of the old lunar calendar, and the Chinka-sai (鎮火祭, "fire-calming festival") on New Year's Eve. ⇒ ⚑ The shrine by the water and the temple up the slope were one institution. They are separate today because of a nineteenth-century government policy.

Daisho-in was made a prayer hall by decree of Emperor Toba. Emperor Meiji lodged here in 1885, and Hideyoshi held a tea ceremony here.

Daisho-in official site, "about" page (Japanese), translated · the 「最も歴史が深い」 line is the temple's own wording, re-read 2026-08-21 · checked 2026-08-21; re-read for the names of the two festivals · checked 2026-10-17

What is enshrined, and one of them belonged to Hideyoshi

The temple enshrines Kobo Daishi, Kukai himself. It also enshrines Sanki Daigongen, the guardian deity of Misen, whom the temple says Kukai invoked and who is said to command the tengu of the mountain. The third is an Eleven-Faced Kannon attributed to Gyoki. The temple describes this Kannon explicitly as the original Buddhist form of the deity of Itsukushima Shrine, the doctrine that made shrine and temple one thing.

🔴 The Namikiri Fudo Myo-o ("the wave-cutting Fudo") was Hideyoshi's own personal protective Buddha, carried aboard his flagship to pray for safety at sea and victory. It is now the principal image of the Imperial Prayer Hall, surrounded by a thousand miniature Fudo figures.

⇒ ⚑ Hideyoshi appears three times on this island: in a statue he sailed with, a hall he never finished, and a tea ceremony.

Daisho-in official site, "about" page and its dedicated pages on the Namikiri Fudo and the Kannon-do (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-08-15

The Seven Wonders of Misen — and two of them no longer exist

The temple keeps a list of seven marvels of the mountain, and the temple is open about their present condition:

1. The never-extinguished flame of the Reikado hall. The hall burned down in May 2026; press reports say the flame itself was kept safe elsewhere. 2. Kobo Daishi's staff, which is said to have taken root and become a red plum that will not bloom when misfortune threatens the mountain. 3. The Mandala Rock, a huge slab said to bear characters carved by Kukai. 4. The Dragon Lantern Cedar, from which mysterious lights out at sea were best seen. 5. The Tide Rock, a hole at around five hundred metres of altitude whose water rises and falls with the tide and contains salt. 6. The Drizzle Cherry, which dripped dew like passing rain on clear days. 7. The sound of wooden clappers heard at night with nobody there, attributed to tengu.

⚠ The temple states plainly that the Drizzle Cherry and the Dragon Lantern Cedar have withered away and only stumps remain. ⇒ Two of the seven wonders are now gone, and the temple says so itself. ⚠ The Mandala Rock is separately reported closed, because a typhoon brought a large tree down across the path.

Daisho-in official page on the Seven Wonders (Japanese), including its own statement that two have withered; Miyajima Ropeway official history page for the Mandala Rock closure · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Daisho-in is built up a steep hillside and is full of things to touch and turn. Five things to look for:

1. The great sutra cylinders. They hold the six hundred fascicles of the Great Perfection of Wisdom sutra, which the temple says were brought back from India by Sanzo Hoshi (三蔵法師). The temple's own claim: touching them as you pray brings blessings without limit.

2. The prayer wheels. Rows of dozens of metal prayer wheels run along the central handrail of several staircases in the grounds, including the staircase in front of the Maniden hall. They are inscribed with the Heart Sutra. The temple states the merit precisely: turning one once earns the merit of reciting one full copy of the Heart Sutra.

3. The Kaidan-meguri, beneath the main hall. The temple describes it as true darkness. You walk it with your left hand on the wall, chanting, and come out at the Eleven-Faced Kannon. ⇒ It is short and completely dark, and it is the most unusual few minutes on the island.

4. The Henjokutsu cave. A hall enshrining the principal images of all eighty-eight temples of the Shikoku pilgrimage, with sand from each temple underfoot. ⇒ The room condenses the twelve-hundred-kilometre Shikoku pilgrimage into a walk of a few minutes.

5. The five hundred rakan on the slope. Five hundred stone arhats, no two faces alike. ⇒ Climb up among them between the halls instead of taking a photograph from the path.

Daisho-in official pages on the sutra cylinders, prayer wheels, Kaidan-meguri, Henjokutsu and the rakan garden (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-08-15; the prayer wheels' place on the staircase handrails from the Japan Tourism Agency multilingual database ("Daishoin: Prayer Wheels", 2019) and the sutra cylinders' origin from the temple's grounds-map entry · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official page on the temple (Japanese) Official news, incl. the 2026 fire notices (Japanese) Official page on the Seven Wonders (Japanese)
Mount Misen弥山
Sacred mountain · Miyajima · 535 m · primeval forest, World Heritage since 1996
▶
What it is

Mount Misen is the island's highest point, at about 535 metres, and a sacred mountain in its own right. The Misen primeval forest is the evergreen broadleaf forest behind Itsukushima Shrine. It was designated a Natural Monument in 1929 and a Special Protected Area in 1957, and was included in the 1996 World Heritage inscription along with the shrine.

The forest is of real botanical interest. The operator describes a plant community of hemlock and Japanese andromeda near the summit as very valuable to the primeval forest. Hemlock itself grows along the main Misen trail (弥山本道) from near the trailhead all the way to the summit. Hiroshima University established a botanical research garden on the island in 1964 to study the forest.

Miyajima Ropeway official nature page (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-08-15; the hemlock sentences re-read and corrected · checked 2026-10-17

🔴 The summit belongs to Itsukushima Shrine as well as Daisho-in

Most of the halls on the summit belong to Daisho-in, but not all of them. The ropeway operator's history records that Miyama Shrine, near the summit, was built by Taira no Kiyomori as the inner shrine of Itsukushima Shrine itself. It enshrines the same three Munakata goddesses as the shrine standing in the water far below.

⇒ ⚑ Itsukushima Shrine, which you walked through this morning, has its inner shrine at the top of the mountain. ⚠ The summit has two owners: the Reikado, Misen Hondo, Sanki-do and the other halls belong to Daisho-in, and Miyama Shrine belongs to Itsukushima Shrine.

The great bell in the Misen Hondo, an Important Cultural Property, was donated by Taira no Munemori, Kiyomori's son.

Miyajima Ropeway official history page (Japanese), for Miyama Shrine's founding and deities and for the bell's donor · checked 2026-08-15

Named rocks along the paths, and the stories attached to them

The mountain is granite, and weathering has left named rock formations along the paths. Three rocks and one paved path are worth knowing before you set out, because each comes with a fact or a story:

Kuguri-iwa is the rock tunnel just below the summit that walkers pass through. ⚠ The operator's own page notes that in recent years earthquakes are said to have gradually lowered its height. ⇒ The tunnel is said to be getting lower.

The Yujo Ishidatami-michi ("courtesans' stone-paved path") is a stretch of path about 180 metres long, laid with flat stones. It was completed at the end of the Edo period with donations from courtesans who wanted the pilgrimage path repaired. ⇒ ⚑ The path's name records who paid for it.

Kaisen-iwa, above the Dainichi-do: the legend holds that impious visitors passing it catch a skin disease, while devout sufferers who touch it are cured, the illness passing into the rock.

Maku-iwa, "Curtain Rock", is a single exposed slab said to run for over 150 metres.

Miyajima Ropeway official nature page (Japanese), per-rock descriptions, translated · checked 2026-08-15; the paved path re-read, and an unsourced line about the courtesans cut · checked 2026-10-17

Getting up it, and what that actually involves

⚠ The ropeway does not go to the summit. It runs in two stages, a small gondola line and then a larger cable car. From the top station, the operator's own figure is about a twenty-minute walk to the Reikado and Misen Hondo area, and the true summit is a further short climb beyond that.

⚠ On foot from the bottom, Daisho-in's own advice for climbers allows up to two hours one way, on the old pilgrimage route past stone distance markers. Its safety advice: no food is sold on the mountain or at the summit; wear proper walking shoes, not sandals; and if you are coming down after three in the afternoon, use the Daisho-in route, because it stays light later.

⚠ 8 November is a reservation-only day on the ropeway, and a place on a car down is booked: one person, 14:00–14:15, from Shishiiwa, the upper station. The plan is to walk up by the Daisho-in trail and down by the Momijidani trail, so the ropeway is the way down only if your legs are finished at the top. ⇒ 🔴 To use it, leave the summit by 13:30: Shishiiwa is about 30 minutes' walk away, and the booking lapses once 14:15 has passed. Show the booking's QR code at the platform. The booking holds a place but is not a ticket, so pay the fare (¥1,100 one way) in cash. November is the ropeway's busiest month, so you may still wait for a car. The last car down is at 16:30.

Miyajima Ropeway official site (the two lines and the twenty-minute figure, read on 2026-08-15; the 8 November reservation-only status and the booked car from the operator's booking confirmation, 2026-09-04); Daisho-in official page for climbers (the two-hour figure and the safety advice) · checked 2026-08-15

The observation deck on the summit (2013)

The observation deck at the top of Misen was rebuilt in 2013 as the Miyajima Observation Rest House (宮島展望休憩所). It was designed by the Hiroshima-born architect Hiroshi Sambuichi.

The design idea is "sitting": the building is made so that you can sit and take in the view of the Seto Inland Sea, with the wind and the light. Sambuichi is known for building his designs around natural elements such as wind, water and sunlight.

Dive! Hiroshima, the Hiroshima Prefecture tourism portal, page on the Misen observation deck (Japanese), translated · not an operator or architect page · checked 2026-10-17

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

⚠ These points assume good weather and the climb on foot by the Daisho-in trail. The Reikado is a cleared site: see the Daisho-in entry. Four things to look at:

1. At Miyama Shrine, think back to Itsukushima Shrine, where you were this morning. Miyama Shrine has the same three goddesses, five hundred metres higher. ⇒ Kiyomori built both, and the shrine on the peak is the inner shrine of the shrine in the sea.

2. Go through Kuguri-iwa instead of round it. ⇒ The operator says the tunnel is lower than it used to be.

3. Look out for the courtesans' path. It is about 180 metres of path laid with flat stones, paid for by courtesans at the end of the Edo period. ⚠ The operator's page does not say which trail it is on.

4. At the summit observatory, look south-west over the Seto Inland Sea and its islands. ⇒ Ito Hirobumi paid for the climbing trail out of his own pocket. He argued that the true value of Miyajima is the view from the top of Misen, and his words are on a plaque in the Hondo and carved into a boulder below.

Miyajima Ropeway official history and nature pages (Miyama Shrine, Kuguri-iwa, the paved road, Ito Hirobumi's funding and quotation) · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Ropeway operator, incl. the reservation calendar (Japanese) Operator's page on the forest and the rocks (Japanese) Daisho-in advice for climbers (Japanese)

Tuesday 10 November — Edo and Ryogoku

Tokyo · two lords' gardens quoting places they had never seen, and a museum of the city that stood between them
Koishikawa Korakuen小石川後楽園
Daimyo garden · Tokyo · begun 1629 · the oldest surviving garden of Edo
▶
What it is

Koishikawa Korakuen was begun in 1629 by Tokugawa Yorifusa, founding lord of the Mito branch of the Tokugawa, as the garden of his Edo residence. It was completed under his son Tokugawa Mitsukuni. The garden's own page calls it the oldest surviving daimyo garden of Edo. It also says that its layout, a stroll garden circling a pond, became the model that later daimyo gardens followed.

The garden's page describes the layout as four landscapes: the great pond is a "sea" at the centre, with a "mountain," a "river" and "countryside" arranged in a ring around it.

⚑ A detail most visitors miss: the ponds were fed by the Kanda aqueduct, the city's drinking-water supply. The garden's own page remarks that it was rare for a water main to run through a samurai residence at all. Traces of the aqueduct are still marked on the ground.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page for Koishikawa Korakuen (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 The name is a Chinese sentence about how to govern

Mitsukuni took advice from Zhu Shunshui, a Ming Confucian scholar living in Japan as an exile after his own dynasty fell. The name they chose for the garden comes from the Song-dynasty writer Fan Zhongyan's Record of the Yueyang Tower, and the garden's page prints the line:

"Be first to worry about the troubles of the world, and last to take pleasure in its pleasures."

Koraku means "pleasure afterwards," the second half of Fan Zhongyan's line. The garden's own page adds that Mitsukuni made this line his political creed.

⇒ ⚑ So a Tokugawa lord named his private pleasure garden after a maxim telling rulers to take their pleasure last. The man who explained the maxim to him was a refugee from the Ming empire, which the Manchus had just destroyed.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page (Japanese), quoting the Chinese line and its Japanese gloss, translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 It is one of only nine places in Japan holding both top designations — and you will have seen three of them

In March 1952 the garden was given two designations under the national cultural properties law: Special Historic Site and Special Place of Scenic Beauty. Very few places hold both, and the garden's own page names every one:

Among Tokyo's metropolitan gardens, only Koishikawa Korakuen and Hama-rikyu hold both. Nationwide there are only nine sites in total. Besides the two Tokyo gardens, they are Kinkaku-ji and Ginkaku-ji in Kyoto, Daigo-ji's Sanbo-in, the Heijo-kyo palace site in Nara, Itsukushima in Hiroshima, the Motsu-ji garden in Iwate, and the Ichijodani Asakura garden in Fukui.

⇒ 🔴 Three of those nine are on this trip. You visited Sanbo-in on 4 November and Itsukushima on 8 November. Koishikawa Korakuen is the third, on 10 November, the last full day of the trip.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page (Japanese), which enumerates all nine sites by name, translated · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

A compact circuit of about an hour, with the city's buildings visible above the trees. Six things to look at:

1. The Full Moon Bridge. A stone arch said to have been designed by Zhu Shunshui himself, who had been invited as an honoured guest and teacher. Its reflection in the water completes a full circle, which is where the name comes from. The garden calls it one of the oldest bridges of its type outside Kyushu and Okinawa. ⇒ Stand where the reflection closes the circle.

2. The great pond and what it represents. The pond is the central "sea," with an island of the immortals in it. The garden states that Shogun Tokugawa Iemitsu himself is said to have drawn the shape of the pond, which was made to evoke Lake Biwa.

3. The West Lake causeway. A reproduction of the Su Causeway at West Lake in China. The garden claims it as the first depiction of that causeway in any Japanese garden. ⇒ ⚑ So one garden has two water features copying two different lakes: Lake Biwa in Japan and West Lake in China.

4. The Oi River stretch, with its two bridges. A reproduction of a river in Kyoto that Iemitsu liked. He is said to have sat on a rock in it directing the work. The two bridges are named Tsutenkyo and Togetsukyo, after real bridges in Kyoto. ⇒ You were near the real Togetsukyo, at Arashiyama, on 3 November.

5. The rice paddy, which is still farmed. The garden says Mitsukuni had it made to teach the wife of his heir about the hardship of farmers and the value of agriculture. Local schoolchildren still plant and harvest it every year. ⇒ The paddy is still used for teaching, three and a half centuries on.

6. Tokujin-do: a small hall, and the only original building left. At eighteen, Mitsukuni read the chapter on Boyi in Sima Qian's Records of the Grand Historian. He admired Boyi and his brother, who starved rather than serve an unjust ruler, and enshrined their statues in this hall. The hall's name comes from a phrase in the Analects: seeking benevolence, and attaining benevolence. ⚠ The garden states that Tokujin-do is the only building that keeps its original appearance from when it was built. The formal Chinese-style gate you enter through burned in the war, and its reconstruction was completed at the end of 2020.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page (Japanese), per-feature descriptions for all six, translated · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official page (English) Official page with the full feature list (Japanese)
Edo-Tokyo Museum江戸東京博物館
Museum · Ryogoku · opened 1993 · reopened March 2026 after a four-year rebuild
▶
✅ It was shut for four years and it is open again

The museum is open again. This is worth stating plainly, because most printed guidance is out of date. The museum's own history table records:

"1 April 2022 to 31 March 2026: closed for large-scale renovation work." And on the following line: "31 March 2026: Tokyo Metropolitan Edo-Tokyo Museum — renewal reopening."

⇒ ✅ It reopened on 31 March 2026, about seven months before your visit on 10 November. Its regular closing day is Monday, and 10 November is a Tuesday. ⚠ One minor caveat: the special-exhibition gallery may be between shows in early November. That does not affect the permanent Edo and Tokyo galleries, which are the reason for the visit.

Edo-Tokyo Museum official history page (Japanese), quoted from the page's own chronology table; homepage status read the same day · checked 2026-08-15

The building is a warehouse on stilts, and it is the height of a castle

The museum opened on 28 March 1993, on the former railway yard beside Ryogoku Station. It was designed by Kiyonori Kikutake, an architect of the Metabolist movement, and its shape deliberately copies an old building type. The building is lifted clear of the ground on four huge piers, in the form of a raised-floor storehouse, the elevated granary used in Japan since ancient times.

⚑ The building stands about 62 metres above ground, which is said to be roughly the height of the lost main keep of Edo Castle. ⚠ That comparison comes from a Tokyo government introduction page. The museum itself does not make it, so treat it as an often-repeated description. The museum's own figures give the height and two basement floors; the government page says one basement floor. That discrepancy is left unresolved here.

Edo-Tokyo Museum official facilities page (Japanese) for the architect, dates and dimensions; Tokyo Metropolitan Government regional-resource page for the storehouse quotation and the castle comparison, attributed and flagged as non-museum-sourced · checked 2026-08-15

What is inside, at full scale

The permanent exhibition mostly shows things by building them at their real size.

Nihonbashi. You enter the Edo zone by walking across a reconstruction of Nihonbashi bridge. According to the museum's English page, the original bridge was about 51 metres long and some 8 metres wide, and this model reproduces the northern half. It was built from reconstruction records and pictures dated 1806 and 1829. The piles, beams and posts are zelkova wood; the handrails and floorboards are cypress. ⇒ 🔴 The two bronze ornaments on the handrail are modelled on surviving originals that carry an inscription dated 1658.

The Nakamura-za theatre front. A full-scale facade of the Nakamura-za, one of Edo's great kabuki playhouses, about 20 metres wide and 5.5 metres deep. It was reconstructed from period drawings, and its signboards copy those for one specific performance: the season-opening programme of the eleventh month of 1805. ⇒ The facade shows one particular theatre in one particular month, which makes it more exact than a generic kabuki theatre.

Edo Castle, at one two-hundredth scale. A model of the Honmaru and Ninomaru palaces as they stood in the last years of the shogunate. The museum notes that the Honmaru palace of around 1845 covered some 37,531 square metres and was among the largest wooden buildings of its time.

🔴 The comparison the museum builds deliberately: two models side by side. One is a daimyo's grand residence, that of Matsudaira Tadamasa, which burned to the ground in the Meireki Fire of 1657; after that fire, such lavish residences were no longer built. The other is the townspeople's quarters around Nihonbashi, modelled at the same scale on purpose, so you can see the difference in space per person instead of reading about it.

Edo-Tokyo Museum official English permanent-exhibition page, quoted verbatim for the bridge, the theatre and the models · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

The museum is large, and two hours go by quickly. Four things to look at:

1. Cross the Nihonbashi bridge slowly and look at the ornaments on the handrail. They are bronze, modelled on originals inscribed 1658. ⇒ The way into the exhibition is itself an exhibit.

2. Stand between the daimyo residence and the townhouse blocks. The two models are at the same scale and placed side by side on purpose. ⇒ This is the clearest point the museum makes, and it makes it with models rather than labels.

3. The Ryogoku Bridge riverside diorama. It shows the entertainment district that stood immediately outside this building, with around fifteen hundred figures. ⇒ You are looking at the street outside the museum as it was two hundred years earlier.

4. Go outside and look up at the building on its piers. ⇒ It is a granary shape at the height of a castle keep, standing on the old railway yard where trains used to be parked.

Edo-Tokyo Museum official English permanent-exhibition page and Japanese facilities page · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official site (English) Official chronology, incl. the closure and reopening (Japanese) Official permanent-exhibition guide (English)
Kiyosumi Garden清澄庭園
Garden · Fukagawa · rebuilt from 1878 by the founder of Mitsubishi · a garden made of imported rocks
▶
What it is

Kiyosumi is a pond-and-woodland stroll garden. The style comes from the gardens of the Edo daimyo, and the garden's own page makes a specific claim about Kiyosumi: the style carried on into Meiji-era garden design and reached its modern culmination here.

By tradition, part of the ground was once the estate of the great Edo merchant Kinokuniya Bunzaemon. In the 1716–36 period it became the lower residence of the lord of Sekiyado, and the garden first took rough shape then.

🔴 In 1878 Iwasaki Yataro, the founder of Mitsubishi, bought the run-down property and made a garden of it for his employees' recreation and for entertaining distinguished guests. It was substantially finished in 1880 under the name "Fukagawa Friendship Garden." Work continued after his death: the great pond was dug and fed with water drawn from the Sumida River.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page for Kiyosumi (Japanese), translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 The rocks came by ship, and that is the whole point of the place

Kiyosumi is famous for meiseki, which means named stones. The garden's own page lists eight representative kinds by where they come from: Izu shore stone · Iyo blue stone · Kishu blue stone · Ikoma stone · Shikinejima stone · Sado red-jewel stone · Bitchu granite · Sanuki granite. These come from quarries all over the country.

🔴 The garden's own page explains how the stones got here: the Iwasaki family gathered them from stone-producing regions nationwide using their own company's steamships.

⇒ ⚑ The Iwasaki family ran a shipping company, and they built a garden out of heavy stones that a shipping company was uniquely able to move. The garden adds that, counting paving, bridges and stepping stones, the stones are too many to count, so the place takes on the character of a stone garden. ⚠ No total is published, so do not expect a number.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page (Japanese), the eight stone types and the steamship sentence quoted and translated · checked 2026-08-15

🔴 In 1923 it saved lives, and that is why you can walk in it

The garden's own account of the Great Kanto Earthquake explains why the place is public:

"Kiyosumi Garden suffered great damage in the Great Kanto Earthquake, but at that time it unexpectedly served as a refuge in the disaster and saved many lives. The Iwasaki family, placing weight on this disaster-prevention function that gardens possess, donated the eastern half — the part less damaged, which is the present garden — to the City of Tokyo as parkland the following year, 1924. The city developed it and opened it to the public in July 1932."

⇒ 🔴 The garden became public because it had already served, unplanned, as a refuge in an emergency. ⚑ The owners drew the right conclusion and gave it away.

The Tudor-style brick house that stood here, designed by the British architect Josiah Conder, burned in the same earthquake. The garden was designated a Tokyo Metropolitan Place of Scenic Beauty on 31 March 1979. ⚠ That is a metropolitan designation, a clear step below the national double designation of Koishikawa Korakuen, visited that morning. Both gardens are in Tokyo and run by the same body, but they hold two different levels of protection.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official page (Japanese), the earthquake passage quoted verbatim and translated, and the 1979 designation; the Conder house from the Association's own English page · checked 2026-08-15

Visiting it — and what to actually look at

Kiyosumi is small and flat, with paths close to the water. Four things to look at:

1. The isowatari, the stepping stones along the edge of the pond. The garden states the design intent outright: the stones are set at intervals so that the view changes with every step you take. ⇒ Walk them slowly, as the design intends.

2. Pick three stones and read their labels. Izu, Iyo and Sado are on three different coasts. ⇒ The stones come from quarries all over Japan, and a shipping line brought them here.

3. The Ryotei, the tea-style hall built out over the water. The Iwasaki family built it to receive Field Marshal Kitchener, who came as a state guest in 1909. ⚠ It survived the earthquake and the war but was completely rebuilt in 1985, and was named a Tokyo Selected Historic Building in 2005. ⇒ The hall still does its original job, but the building itself dates from 1985.

4. The Basho stone. The garden confirms that a monument here is carved with Basho's most famous verse: the old pond, the frog jumping in, the sound of water. ⚑ The stone was moved here: the Association's English page says it stood on the bank of the Sumida and was brought into the garden during the embankment works. ⇒ Like the rocks, even the stone carved with the poem about a pond was brought in from elsewhere.

Tokyo Metropolitan Park Association official Japanese page (the stepping stones, the stones, the Ryotei and its 1985 rebuilding, the Basho monument) and its English page (the monument's relocation) · checked 2026-08-15

Links
Official page (English) Official page with the full feature list (Japanese)

Rain days and substitutes

Six places no day visits · each one is the answer to a specific day going wrong, and each entry says which day, and what the swap costs
TAKAO 599 MUSEUM高尾599ミュージアム
Museum · at Takaosanguchi station · free · the fallback if a wet Monday 26 October cannot be swapped with Tuesday
▶
🔴 Why this museum: the fallback if Monday 26 October cannot be swapped

The Mount Takao day is Monday 26 October. Rain takes away what the day is for: the walk up and the view from the summit. First choice on a wet Monday: swap it with Tuesday's Kamakura day (27 October), the only day left that can swap with it. The two indoor Tokyo days (one in Ueno and Yanesen, one in Edo and Ryōgoku) cannot take Monday's place, because both are built around museums that close on Mondays.

⇒ ⚑ If you can't swap, do a shorter day at the mountain. Take the cable car up to Yakuō-in, then visit TAKAO 599 MUSEUM, beside Takaosanguchi station at the foot of the cable car. Entry is free. You keep the temple but lose the mountain walk.

Planning record — the Monday-swap gap and the substitution, checked 2026-07-28; place record re-checked 2026-08-08

What it is

Mount Takao's own visitor centre, a museum of the mountain's plants and animals. ⇒ On a wet day you lose the walk, but you still learn about the mountain you came to see. That makes it a better choice than an ordinary indoor museum.

Official site (Japanese), about page · checked 2026-08-08

What is inside

Nature Collection is the main exhibition hall, with sixteen exhibits. Wild flowers and insects of Mount Takao are shown set in clear acrylic resin, so the seasonal plants can be seen all year. The museum notes that the exhibits change.

Nature Wall is a display of a beech tree, which the museum calls the symbol of Mount Takao's nature, surrounded by the animals that live on the mountain. Video is projected onto it to show the mountain's wildlife through the four seasons.

599 Guide is a row of three screens with information to read before climbing the mountain.

TAKAO 599 MUSEUM official site, English "About the museum" page, facilities section · checked 2026-10-17

Hours, price, and the seasonal catch

8:00–17:00 from April to November, last entry 16:30. ⚠ From December to March the hours are shorter, 8:00–16:00. 26 October falls in the April–November season, so the longer hours apply.

Admission is free. Open year-round apart from scheduled maintenance days, which are posted.

Official site (Japanese) · checked 2026-08-08 · pin confidence medium

⚠ What you give up with the shorter day

You get a half-day about the mountain instead of a full day on it. Paired with Yakuō-in by cable car (the plan for a day of light rain, which works on any day of the week), the museum makes a real half-day. On its own it fills about an hour indoors.

Planning record, delta stated as MATERIAL · 2026-07-28
