Official site (Japanese) About the museum
Tokei-ji東慶寺
Temple · Kita-Kamakura · free · the substitution to lead with if rain shortens Tuesday 27 October
▶
Why Tokei-ji: no extra travel

Tokei-ji is four minutes on foot from Kita-Kamakura station, on the same street as the rest of the Kita-Kamakura Zen day (Tuesday 27 October). ⇒ If rain shortens that day, spend the freed hour here. The rest of the day's plan stays as it is.

⚑ Tokei-ji replaced Jōchi-ji as the first choice for a wet day. Jōchi-ji was already on the itinerary of the earlier 2019 trip to Japan, so Tokei-ji now comes first.

Planning record — the substitution and the reason for it, checked 2026-07-28 / 2026-07-29

Hours, admission, and the treasure-house date

Open 9:00–16:00. There is no admission charge. Instead, the temple asks visitors to pay their respects at the main hall and leave an offering.

🔴 The temple's treasure house closes on the 1st and 3rd Tuesdays of each month. ⇒ 27 October is the fourth Tuesday, so the treasure house is open on that date.

⚠ Photography is restricted inside the grounds.

Official site (Japanese), visitor guide · checked 2026-08-08 · pin confidence medium

🔴 The "divorce temple"

Tokei-ji was founded in 1285 by the nun Kakusan-ni (覚山志道尼), the wife of Hojo Tokimune. Shortly before Tokimune died in 1284, aged 34, husband and wife both took Buddhist vows under Mugaku Sogen, the founder of Engaku-ji. She founded Tokei-ji the following year.

🔴 Kakusan-ni set the rule that made the temple famous: a woman who fled to Tokei-ji could obtain a divorce. In the temple's words, this was an age when a woman could not divorce her husband, and the temple kept the rule for 600 years, until the Meiji era. ⚠ The oldest surviving document that proves the rule is said to be a divorce certificate from 1738.

⚑ The temple says it was one of only two religious institutions where a woman could take refuge and the shogunate would then order the divorce. The other, Mantoku-ji, no longer exists. About 800 of Tokei-ji's old documents were designated an Important Cultural Property in 2001.

Tokei-ji official site (Japanese), history page, Kakusan-ni page and old-documents page, translated · checked 2026-10-17

A high-ranking nunnery, then a Zen temple

A daughter of Emperor Go-Daigo became the fifth abbess, and the temple became a nunnery of very high rank, second among the five great nunneries of Kamakura. In the early Edo period a daughter of Toyotomi Hideyori was spared after the fall of Osaka and sent here. She became the twentieth abbess, Tenshu-ni.

The divorce rule was abolished in 1871, and the temple stopped being a nunnery in 1902. In 1905 the Zen master Shaku Soen moved here and restored the run-down temple. One of his followers was D. T. Suzuki, who spread Zen around the world and later set up the Matsugaoka Bunko library on the hill behind the temple. ⚑ Men were once strictly kept out of the temple.

Tokei-ji official site (Japanese), history page, translated · checked 2026-10-17

What to look at

The Matsugaoka Hozo (松岡宝蔵) is the treasure house, built in 1978 on the site of the abbot's quarters. It shows the temple's treasures, starting with a standing statue of Sho-Kannon. The original drawing of the dragon painted on the bell tower's ceiling is on permanent display there. The temple also owns more than sixty pieces of lacquerware from the Momoyama and early Edo periods. The temple believes they were handed down by its abbesses, centred on pieces Tenshu-ni used.

The main hall was built in 1935 and enshrines a seated Shaka Buddha, the temple's principal image.

The bell in the bell tower dates from 1350 and came from another temple, Fudaraku-ji. Tokei-ji's own bell, made in 1332, is now at Honryu-ji in Nirayama.

The Suigetsu Kannon, in the Suigetsu-do hall, shows Kannon leaning against a rock and looking at the moon reflected in water. The temple dates it to the thirteenth century and says statues of Kannon in this pose are found in Japan only around Kamakura. ⚠ It is shown only on the 18th of each month, so it will not be on view on 27 October.

Tokei-ji official site (Japanese), grounds-map page, guide page, Suigetsu Kannon page and lacquerware page, translated · checked 2026-10-17

Links
Official site (Japanese) Visitor guide
Taima-dera當麻寺
Temple · Katsuragi, SW Nara · the full-day alternative if the Yoshino ridge is rained off on Thursday 5 November
▶
Why Taima-dera: the same railway line, a shorter day, mostly under cover

Taima-dera is on the Kintetsu Minami-Ōsaka line, the same line the Yoshino day (Thursday 5 November) already uses, fifteen minutes on foot from Taimadera Station. ⇒ You can reach it from the same base as Yoshino without replanning the route, and most of the visit is under cover.

Planning record · checked 2026-08-09

🔴 Both ancient pagodas still stand, which the temple says is unique in Japan

The temple says it is the only temple in Japan whose two ancient pagodas, East and West, both still stand.

The East Pagoda dates from the Nara period and is the oldest building on the site. The West Pagoda was built a little later, in the early Heian period. Both have three storeys. ⚑ Both are National Treasures.

Official site (Japanese), cultural-property pages · checked 2026-08-09

The Mandara-dō and the Taima Mandala

The main hall, the Mandara-dō, is also a National Treasure. Its inner sanctuary keeps its Tenpyō-period form, and the outer part was extended at the end of the Heian period.

The Mandara-dō holds the Taima Mandala. The mandala is kept in a Tenpyō-period cabinet, a National Treasure, which stands on a dais given by Minamoto no Yoritomo, also a National Treasure.

⚠ The mandala on display is the Bunki copy, and it is an Important Cultural Property. It is not the original. According to the legend, Princess Chūjō wove the original in a single night, after copying a thousand scrolls of a sutra.

Official site (Japanese), cultural-property pages · checked 2026-08-09

The Kondō, and three statues that hold national records

The Kondō was rebuilt in the Kamakura period and is an Important Cultural Property. It holds the temple's original object of worship, a seated Miroku of the Hakuhō period. This statue is a National Treasure and the oldest clay statue in Japan. It is guarded by statues of the four heavenly kings, and three of them are the oldest dry-lacquer statues in Japan.

The Kōdō (lecture hall) is also from the Kamakura period and is also an Important Cultural Property. It holds a range of Heian- and Kamakura-period sculpture.

Official site (Japanese), cultural-property pages · checked 2026-08-09

🔴 The oldest bell and the oldest stone lantern in the country, both here

The bronze bell near the Niōmon gate was cast in the 680s. It is the oldest bell in Japan, older than the dated bell at Myōshin-ji, and a National Treasure.

The stone lantern in front of the Kondō is cut from Nijō-san tuff. It is the oldest stone lantern in Japan and an Important Cultural Property.

⇒ ⚑ For a backup sight, Taima-dera holds an unusual number of national records. Two "oldest in Japan" objects, the bell and the stone lantern, stand in the open. The Kondō holds more: the oldest clay statue and the oldest dry-lacquer statues.

Official site (Japanese), cultural-property pages · checked 2026-08-09

Hours and tickets — and what each ticket actually buys

The three main halls are open 09:00–17:00. The ticket costs ¥1,000 for an adult and ¥500 for a primary-school child, and is sold at the main hall itself. You can walk around the grounds without a ticket during the same hours.

Nakanobō has its own ticket, ¥500, with the same hours. It is the temple's oldest sub-temple, and the place where Princess Chūjō became a nun (took the tonsure). It holds the Kōgaen garden, which is designated both a historic site and a place of scenic beauty. It also has a tea room with a large round window, an Important Cultural Property, and a treasure hall whose displays change through the year. ⚠ During Nakanobō's own special display periods, which are announced each year, its ticket costs more.

Other sub-temples decide for themselves when to open. Sainan-in has an Edo-period garden, Gonen-in has peonies, and Okunoin honours Hōnen and has a treasure house of Pure Land Buddhist material.

Official site (Japanese), guide and access pages · checked 2026-08-09

The autumn exhibition falls inside this trip

The temple's autumn treasure exhibition runs from 10 September to 30 November 2026. The whole trip falls inside those dates, so the exhibition will be on whichever day this substitute is used.

Nara Prefecture "Inori-no-Kairō" listing — Tier-2, not the temple's own page · checked 2026-07-11

⚠ What you give up by swapping

Swapping the Yoshino day for Taima-dera loses the Shugendō side of the day, all three parts of the UNESCO World Heritage site on the ridge, the day's hike, and the kaichō (the rare showing of the hidden temple statues).

⇒ Taima-dera fills a shorter day, mostly under cover, and it is a much weaker day than the ridge walk. It is a real fallback.

Planning record, delta stated as LARGE · 2026-07-29

Hiroshima Prefectural Art Museum広島県立美術館
Museum · next door to Shukkei-en · the rain substitute for the garden on Saturday 7 November
▶
Why this museum: it is next door to Shukkei-en

The museum is immediately next door to Shukkei-en garden and uses the same tram stop. The stop is named after both of them: 「県立美術館前（縮景園前）」, "Prefectural Art Museum (Shukkei-en)". ⇒ If the garden is unusable on Saturday 7 November, the substitute is a few steps away, with no travel across the city.

Shukkei-en official access page (Japanese) · checked 2026-07-27

🔴 The evening extension is FRIDAY, and 7 November is a Saturday

The museum is open 9:00–17:00, last entry 16:30. It also opens late on one evening, and which evening matters:

From 24 September 2026 to 31 March 2027, the museum stays open until 19:00 on Fridays. ⚠ The opening until 20:00 belonged to the earlier season, 1 April to 23 September 2026.

⇒ 🔴 The Hiroshima day is Saturday 7 November, so there is no late opening. Plan on the museum closing at 17:00, with last entry at 16:30.

Official site (Japanese), utilisation page · checked 2026-08-11

Closures: none falls on 7 November

The museum is closed every Monday, over the New Year (25 December to 1 January), and while exhibitions are being changed over, on dates it does not publish. It does open on a Monday that is a public or substitute holiday, and on Mondays during certain special exhibitions.

⚑ The unpublished changeover closures are the real risk. The special exhibition during the trip is Sugai Kumi, Thirty Years After His Death, 2 October to 6 December 2026, and the third Collection Focus runs from 17 October 2026 to 17 January 2027. ⇒ Both run across 7 November, so no changeover closes the museum on that date.

Official site (Japanese), exhibitions and utilisation pages · checked 2026-08-11

Admission

Collection exhibition: ¥510 general · ¥310 university student · free for high-school age and under and for those 65 and over. A special exhibition is charged separately.

Official site (Japanese), utilisation page · checked 2026-08-11

The museum and its collection

The museum opened in 1968 as the first public art museum in the Chugoku region, next to the Asano family's garden, Shukkei-en. It was fully renovated and reopened in 1996. It stands on the site of the Kankokan (観古館), which showed the old Asano lords' art collection to the public and is said to have been the first private art museum in Japan opened by a daimyo family.

The museum collects in three areas: art connected with Hiroshima Prefecture, art of the 1920s and 1930s, and the crafts of Japan and Asia. The collection is now over 5,200 works.

Among the works the museum itself picks out:

🔴 Salvador Dalí, "The Dream of Venus." · Isamu Noguchi, "Remembrance." · a work by Henry Moore · a self-portrait in a hat by Ai-Mitsu · Hirayama Ikuo's "Hiroshima Shohen-zu" (広島生変図), which the museum calls one of his best-known works · Edo-period folding screens of Itsukushima.

⚑ The museum also holds more than 200 textiles from Central Asia, mostly from the former Soviet republics, including embroidered suzani hangings. The museum says the collection is known internationally as an outstanding one.

⇒ The collection exhibition on show on 7 November (17 October 2026 to 17 January 2027) includes Dalí's "The Dream of Venus" (about 1939), Noguchi's "Remembrance" and Henry Moore's "Stringed Figure", with many paintings by Kumi Sugai. ⚠ Ai-Mitsu's self-portrait, Hirayama's "Hiroshima Shohen-zu", the Itsukushima screens and the Central Asian textiles are not on its list.

Hiroshima Prefectural Art Museum official site (Japanese): "About the museum" page, "Collection selection" pages, and the English exhibit list for the third collection exhibition of 2026 · checked 2026-10-17

Miyajima History and Folklore Museum宮島歴史民俗資料館
Museum · Miyajima · ¥450 · a second indoor option if Sunday 8 November is a washout
▶
Why this museum: a second indoor option for a wet day on the island

If rain washes out the Miyajima day (Sunday 8 November), skip the climb. Itsukushima Shrine with its roofed corridors, Senjōkaku, Daishō-in and the Treasure Hall can all still be visited, and the Miyajima Public Aquarium is the main indoor stop. ⇒ Together they fill a half-day of about five hours on the island. This museum is a second indoor option on top of that.

Planning record, washout variant · 2026-07-26 / 2026-07-28

🔴 The building is the exhibit

Part of the display building is the main house and storehouse of the former Egami family — wealthy merchants who brewed soy sauce from the late Edo period into the Meiji — and that part is a national Registered Tangible Cultural Property.

⇒ ⚑ You walk through the Egami family's own house and storehouse.

The museum opened in April 1974 to preserve and pass on material on Miyajima's history and culture, including folk cultural property that modernisation was putting at risk.

Hatsukaichi City official page (Japanese) · checked 2026-08-11

Hours, closures and price: the Monday closure does not affect this trip

Open 9:00–17:00, last admission 16:30. Closed on Mondays and from 26 to 31 December. When a Monday is a public or substitute holiday, the museum opens that Monday and closes the following day instead.

⚑ The museum is closed on Mondays, but the Miyajima day is Sunday 8 November, so the closure does not affect this trip. ⇒ On 8 November the museum is open, as a second indoor option after the Miyajima Public Aquarium.

¥450 general · ¥250 high-school student · ¥220 for anyone 65 or over · free for elementary and junior-high pupils. The museum is about twenty minutes on foot from the ferry pier.

Hatsukaichi City official page (Japanese) · checked 2026-08-11 · the day-of-week reconciliation added 2026-08-15; fees re-read (page updated 2026-09-30) · checked 2026-10-17

What the museum shows

The museum is a group of buildings around a small garden: the Egami family's house from the early 1800s, an ordinary folk dwelling, and several buildings now used as exhibition rooms. The displays are about life on Miyajima, mostly from the Edo period onward.

The merchant house. Just inside the entrance, on the left, is a raised tatami room where the soy sauce merchant sat to serve customers. The big beam across the middle of the house, which carries the whole structure, was cut from a single pine tree.

Room A, in the Egami family's storehouse, shows everyday tools: jars, pots, baskets for picking mushrooms, saws and farm tools. ⚑ There are fewer farm tools than saws for a reason: farming was banned on Miyajima until the Meiji era, because the island itself was worshipped as the home of the gods. The trees were protected too: the lord of Hiroshima owned large parts of the island's forest, and people were strictly forbidden to cut down most of its trees.

Room B covers the island's yearly festivals, with scale models of the boats used in the Kangen-sai, a festival of court music played on a decorated boat at night.

Room C covers woodworking. It includes shakushi, wooden paddles used to serve and stir rice, which are said to have been invented on Miyajima in the late 1700s.

Room D is a modern two-storey building. The ground floor covers Itsukushima Shrine and Taira no Kiyomori, with a video on the island's history subtitled in English. The upper floor shows mostly Edo-period pictures of Miyajima, including folding screens that show the island like a map, and woodblock prints by Utagawa Hiroshige and Kawase Hasui.

Japan Tourism Agency multilingual database, six 2019 interpretive texts for the museum (overview, merchant house, Rooms A to D), English · checked 2026-10-17

Miyajima Public Aquarium宮島水族館（みやじマリン）
Aquarium · Miyajima · ¥1,420 · open every day · the main indoor stop if Sunday 8 November is a washout
▶
🔴 The main indoor stop for a wet day on Miyajima

The aquarium was picked for one reason. The Miyajima History and Folklore Museum is closed on Mondays. If the Miyajima day had fallen on a Monday, a wet day would have had nowhere indoors to go. The aquarium has no weekly closing day, so it covered that gap.

⇒ ⚠ The Miyajima day is Sunday 8 November, so the Monday closure does not apply, and the Folklore Museum is open that day too. The museum fits the island's history much better, but on a wet 8 November the aquarium is the main indoor stop and the museum is the second option.

⇒ On a wet day, skip the Misen climb. Keep Itsukushima Shrine, Senjōkaku and Daishō-in, add the Treasure Hall, and add the aquarium. Together they fill about five hours, half a day. If you want more time indoors, the Folklore Museum is the second option.

Planning record, the Monday replacement · 2026-07-28 · reconciled against the locked date 2026-08-15

Hours, closure and price

Open 9:00–17:00, last admission 16:00. ⚠ Last admission here is a full hour before closing. At TAKAO 599 MUSEUM, the Hiroshima Prefectural Art Museum and the Miyajima History and Folklore Museum, last entry is thirty minutes before closing.

The aquarium's own guide page lists no weekly closing day. Its only closure is a long one, 1 December 2026 to 31 March 2027, which starts after the trip has ended.

¥1,420 adult or high-school student · ¥710 junior-high or elementary pupil · ¥400 for a child of four and over. The aquarium is indoors, about fifteen minutes on foot from the shrine along the shore.

Official site (Japanese), guide page · checked 2026-08-11

⚠ What the aquarium makes up for, and what it does not

An aquarium has nothing to do with the island's history, so it fits the Miyajima day much less well than the Folklore Museum's Edo merchant's house.

⇒ The aquarium gives back the hours, but not the quality of the day you lose. On a washed-out island day it is somewhere indoors to spend time near the World Heritage shrine. It does nothing to make up for the real loss, which is Mount Misen.

Planning record, character delta stated and not softened · 2026-07-28

What it shows

The aquarium stands inside the Setonaikai National Park, and its displays are built around the sea life of the Seto Inland Sea. It shows more than 15,000 animals of about 380 species. The main building has ten zones, and the largest tank holds 350 tonnes of water. The building is in a Japanese style, designed to suit the look of Miyajima.

⚑ The animals include finless porpoises, which the aquarium calls the "whales of Setouchi." There is also an area for sea mammals and penguins, with sea lions, seals and small-clawed otters, and a sea-lion show is held every day.

Miyajima Public Aquarium official site: outline page and floor guide (Japanese) and English introduction page · checked 2026-10-17
